# Roadmap and decisions

**Status:** 2026-11-10. "The design" and "the spec" are `protocol.md`; a quoted name after "spec" is one of its headings.

## Decisions

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| D1 | **The protocol core owns its routing and table code**; `bark-proto` takes no external routing or mesh dependency. | The design's routing is small and specific: forwarding toward a 7-byte position that is also the relay identity, depth-first search (DFS) around gaps with per-message state in each relay, per-hop acknowledgement (ACK) with demotion. A general mesh library brings network-wide tables, position estimation and node IDs the design leaves out (spec "What the protocol omits"), and pins its own embassy versions. A few hundred lines of our own keep the API shaped by the design alone. |
| D2 | **Simulator first.** Before any firmware, build a discrete-event simulator that places relays and leaves on a grid, takes a sparse pairwise signal-power table (only pairs in range, per channel, varying over time), and runs traffic through the real protocol core. | The design's capacity numbers (spec "Capacity and sizing") are order-of-magnitude estimates with 2× uncertainty on hearing radius and hop count. The thresholds in O3 and the eviction policy in O4 can only be tuned against a model. Boards arrive in three weeks; the simulator is useful now and after. |
| D3 | **The protocol core does no I/O** (`bark-proto`): a state machine driven by `(event, now)` that returns actions; no executor, no async, no radio driver. Firmware wraps it in an embassy task; the simulator drives it from an event queue. | One implementation under test in both places. Thousands of nodes in one process need a core that does not own tasks or channels. Decided 2026-10-14 (Q1). |
| D4 | `lora-phy` from the lora-rs **git main**, not crates.io 3.0.1 (July 2024). | SF5, `get_rssi`, arbitrary sync word, `rx_switch_channel`, duty-cycled receive all landed after 3.0.1. Pin a revision. |
| D5 | BLE via **TrouBLE + nrf-sdc**, not nrf-softdevice. | nrf-softdevice last released January 2024; trouble-host 0.8 and nrf-sdc 0.4 both released August 2026 with GATT, notifications and security. Known to run on a T-Echo. |
| D6 | **Keep the stock bootloader.** Application linked at `0x26000`, flashed as UF2 (family `0xADA52840`, volume `TECHOBOOT`). | The T-Echo ships Adafruit's UF2 bootloader with SoftDevice S140 6.1.1 occupying `0x1000..0x26000`. Replacing it needs an SWD debug probe and gains nothing. nrf-sdc does not use the SoftDevice; the slot stays reserved. |
| D7 | Repository is `bark`; crates `bark-proto`, `bark-sim`, `bark-fw`. `bark-fw` is a separate Cargo workspace. | Answers design open item O9. A crate that only builds for `thumbv7em-none-eabihf` cannot share a workspace with host tests without nightly per-package targets. |
| D8 | **`bark-proto` does not allocate.** Tables are fixed-capacity arrays sized by the design. | Every table is bounded by the design anyway (spec "Neighbour table", spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46), "Delivery queue"). No allocator on the firmware means no allocation failure path; fixed-size `Leaf`s let the simulator hold twenty thousand in cache. |
| D10 | **No failure notices.** The per-hop ACK is the only acknowledgement in the protocol; whether a message arrived is the app's business, inside the encrypted payload. | A network-level notice needs a sender address in the header, which the design keeps out of the header on purpose. Decided 2026-10-14 after the first draft added one. |
| D11 | **DFS state lives in the relay.** Per message it handles, a relay keeps its parent (the short name and home channel of the neighbour the message first came from; none if it originated the message) and the set of neighbours it has tried, the parent included. The owner relay remembers delivered IDs for the queue TTL. | Backtracking needs to know who sent a repeat; DATA names its sender in `src_hop` (D16). Revised 2026-10-14, twice: the first draft keyed on visited-list length alone, which cannot tell a retransmission from a backtrack when the child's append was lost; the second kept a visited list in the packet as skip list and sender identity, which D16 removes. (Spec "Routing".) |
| D12 | **Neighbour-table eviction keeps incumbents.** When the table is full and a new relay is heard, it replaces the least recently heard demoted entry, else the least recently heard entry not heard for a worst-case rotation (13 × 15 s = 195 s), else it is not added. (`tables/neighbours.rs`, spec "Neighbour table".) | Least-recently-heard eviction, the first draft, fails on a rooftop relay hearing ≈270 relays: ≈2 heartbeats a second on its channel would evict a live neighbour every ≈30 s and lose every failure count within a minute. Keeping incumbents holds an equally arbitrary 64 and replaces only entries that fail or fall silent. A set spread around the relay was measured later and routes no better (D69). Decided 2026-10-14. |
| D13 | **Preamble 12 symbols** on every packet except those to sleeping leaves; airtime by the datasheet formula (`radio.rs` `time_on_air`): heartbeat 11.8 ms, ACK 11.8 ms (9 bytes, D19), 255-byte frame 138.6 ms. **The channel sweep is spread:** one RSSI reading every ≈77 ms instead of 13 in a burst each second. | The SX1262 needs at least 12 preamble symbols at SF5 and SF6 (datasheet §6.1.4); the first draft assumed 8. With 12, a single reading (≈0.5 ms away) is shorter than the preamble left after the modem locks, so a spread sweep loses no packets, where a 5 ms burst loses ≈0.4% of arrivals. Holds while the modem locks within 5 symbols; measure in bring-up step 1. Decided 2026-10-14 (closes Q5). (Spec "Physical layer", "Busy estimate", "Heartbeat", spec section "Leaf receive mode" (before D46), "Capacity and sizing".) |
| D9 | **One timer per core.** The core exposes `next_wakeup()`; the driver delivers `Event::Wakeup` when its clock reaches it. | No timer identifiers cross the boundary; the simulator keeps one heap entry per core, the firmware one embassy timer. Fixed in `docs/core-interface.md`. |
| D14 | **Relays are named on the wire by a 4-byte short name**: the low 16 bits of the latitude count, then the low 16 bits of the longitude count. ATTACH `relay`, DATA `dst_hop` and `src_hop` carry it. Fields used as coordinates, the heartbeat position and DATA `dst_pos`, stay 7 bytes. Identity is still the position (spec section "Relay identity" (before D46)). | Saves 3 bytes per name. The short name repeats only every 39 km of latitude and 25 km of longitude at 50° N, so among relays one node can hear (≈3 km) it is exactly the position: the receiver rebuilds the full position from its own, and two relays share a name only if they share a 0.6 m cell. A hash would collide with probability ≈10⁻⁵ and could not be inverted. Decided 2026-10-14. (Spec section "Relay identity" (before D46), "Packet formats".) |
| D15 | **No `msg_id` field.** A message is identified by `hash32(dst_pos ‖ dst_leaf ‖ payload)`, computed by every node that handles it; ACK and DELIVER carry it. The app must keep payloads unique (spec "Phone link"); encryption with a fresh nonce does. | 4 bytes less per DATA, and nothing for the sender to generate or keep. Two identical messages to one destination within the dedupe window are one message, which the app avoids for free. Decided 2026-10-14. (Spec "Phone link", "Packet formats".) |
| D16 | **No visited list in the packet** (revises D11). DATA carries `src_hop`, the sender's short name, and its home channel in `aux`. Next-hop candidates exclude only the relay's tried set. A neighbour that already has the message by another path is found out by sending it there: it sends the message straight back, and the sender counts it as tried. | The list cost 2 bytes per relay on every packet and still needed the receiver to resolve the sender. Without it a neighbour that already has the message costs one extra round trip, which overhearing would save, but neighbours sit on different channels and cannot overhear each other. The walk is still a depth-first traversal with a tried set and parent per relay; the `hops` cap remains the backstop for expired state. Decided 2026-10-14. (Spec "Routing", "Packet formats".) |
| D17 | **How a relay runs the search** (spec "Routing"). Candidates are tried in order of distance to the destination, so the tried set is the parent plus everything up to the last child. A repeat is a copy when its `hops` is no higher than any received before; a copy, a repeat from the parent, and any repeat at the owner are only ACKed; a repeat from the last child continues the search; a repeat from anyone else, an earlier child included, is sent straight back. Records expire after 188 s idle, at most 626, least recently active replaced (revised by D31: no expiry, 1788, never one whose message the relay holds). The owner is the addressed relay, or a relay within 50 m with no closer neighbour; farther away, no closer neighbour means a gap. Send queue of 10; no ACK when it is full (revised: 16 places by D22, 40 sized by the bucket and BUSY when full by D27 and D29, 254 hop places beyond them by D31). | The `hops` test catches a retransmitted return from a child, which a sender-only rule would take as a second return and so split the search. ACK-only for the parent keeps one copy of the message searching. The ordered tried set needs no list. Record lifetime covers the longest search the hop cap allows; the bound is the most messages the duty cycle lets a relay handle in that time. The 50 m rule separates a dead destination from a gap; the old rule made every gap an owner. Decided 2026-10-14.  Revised 2026-10-14: the tried set is explicit, the parent plus the neighbours actually handed the message (one bit per neighbour slot); a neighbour passed over because it was demoted, expired or not in the table is not tried, so its later offer is sent back (case 6) rather than dropped. The implicit, order-defined set dropped the only searching copy in that case. Revised 2026-10-15: an earlier child's repeat is sent straight back, no longer only ACKed. The child may be offering its own searching copy, which ACK-only ended without a trace: it had the message already and sent this relay's hand-over straight back, or it never took a hand-over this relay counted as failed because its replies were lost, and got the message later by another path. Seen in 25-relay random meshes (`tests/routing.rs`: `an_earlier_child_that_had_the_message_is_sent_it_back`, `an_earlier_child_whose_hand_over_failed_is_sent_it_back`; both fail under ACK-only). Where the child is returning a copy it did take, after a split, it only ACKs the send-back, since this relay is its parent, and one copy goes on (`a_split_copy_returned_by_an_earlier_child_ends_there`). The record bound is 626 (⌊18.83 s / 30.08 ms⌋); this row said 654. Simulator, settled phase: S1, seeds 1–40, 1.000, and S1b, seeds 1–100, 0.961, worst 0.900, bit-identical; S2, seeds 1–100, 0.878, worst 0.619, the same delivery and losses in every seed, with frames differing in 3 seeds where the case arose. |
| D18 | **Demotion is not cleared by a heartbeat.** A demoted neighbour returns to use when it ACKs a packet from this relay or after three rotations. | A heartbeat proves the neighbour is alive, not that it hears us. On a link that works one way only, clearing demotion every heartbeat would make every message pay the failed attempts again. (Spec "Neighbour table".) |
| D19 | **ACKs name their sender.** ACK carries the message ID and the acknowledging relay's short name or the leaf's ID; the sender matches both. | Relays share home channels, and the search can put the same message in two relays on one channel; a bare ACK could then end the wrong hand-over and strand the message. Cost 4 bytes, 1.3 ms per hop. (Spec "Hand-over", "Packet formats".) |
| D20 | **`lora-phy` at upstream `main` `7a9c2ec` (2026-10-06), copied into `bark-fw/vendor/lora-phy` with one change: the radio idles in STDBY_XOSC and the TCXO start is set by the firmware (5 ms, `radio::TCXO_START`)**; the firmware gives it its own board interface (`bark-fw/src/radio.rs`, `Pins`) rather than the driver's generic SX126x one. | That revision reports CRC failures as errors. The generic interface polls BUSY at once after each command, within the up to 600 ns BUSY takes to rise (SX1261/2 DS §8.3.1), and waits on BUSY and DIO1 without bound; ours waits out the rise and bounds both. The driver as published idles in STDBY_RC, which turns the TCXO off, and programs a 10 ms TCXO start that the chip waits out in full on every start (DS §13.3.6), so each retune, CAD and transmit cost 10 ms against the ≈0.1 ms spec "Busy estimate" assumes. In STDBY_XOSC only a CAD, which ends in STDBY_RC (DS Table 13-73), still costs a start. The library has no setting for either, so the change lives in a copy; a fork of lora-rs pinned to a commit would serve as well and keep the repository smaller (2026-10-15). |
| D21 | **The destination position alone decides who delivers.** A relay delivers or queues a message for a leaf only if it owns the destination position (spec "Owner"), never because the leaf is in its leaf table. | Simulator S1, 40 seeds: 4 seeds lost 1 to 3 messages each in the settled phase (8 in all, `QueueExpired` at B). Both leaves first attached to the middle relay B, heard at −110 dBm, and moved to their own relays one or two rotations later (spec "Relay choice"). B kept the far leaf in its leaf table for up to 180 s after it left (spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46)), took messages addressed to the far leaf at C from the near leaf, still attached to B, as its own to deliver, queued them on its own channel where nobody answered, and dropped them after the 10-minute delivery TTL. With the rule: 40 of 40 seeds deliver every settled-phase message. The table can also be right where the address is stale, for a leaf that has moved to this relay: delivering from the table would save such a message when this relay happens to be its origin or on its path. The rule gives that up; it is the loss the design already accepts for a moved leaf (spec "Delivery queue"), and one rule, by the address, replaces two that disagree exactly when a leaf moves. Decided 2026-10-14. (Spec "Owner".) |
| D22 | **The retry wait after a missing ACK is derived from the hand-over time.** Retry k after k unanswered transmissions waits uniform in [0, 2^k × 150.4 ms], 150.4 ms being a full frame and its ACK (`tables::RETRY_BACKOFF`); 0–301 ms, then 0–602 ms. The listen-before-talk backoff stays 0–50 ms. The send queue, sized by the time an upstream sender spends retrying, grows from 10 to 16 (revises D17), and the `Relay` from 48 304 to 49 880 B. Closes O14. | Two senders that cannot hear each other and collide at one receiver both miss the ACK; their retries miss each other only if they start more than one hand-over apart, with probability (1 − 1/W)² for a window of W hand-overs: zero for the old 50 ms. Simulator S1b (`scenarios/s1b-hidden.toml`: S1's line, a leaf at each end, both sending every 30 s from the same instant, so every first hand-over to the middle relay collides), 20 seeds, settled-phase delivery: old rule 0.136 mean (5 seeds delivered nothing; every loss a `NoRoute` drop at A or C after the hand-over to B failed, most of them while B was demoted); fixed window of one hand-over 0.553; growing 1, 2 hand-overs 0.694; 2, 3 hand-overs 0.905; **2, 4 hand-overs 0.958** (7 of 20 seeds 1.0, worst 0.688). Median per-seed p50 latency 599 ms against 205 ms in S1, the price of a retry on every message; S1 is unchanged (40 of 40 seeds 1.0). Most of what is still lost comes from demotion: two failed hand-overs demote B, the only route, and every message is then dropped until the demotion lapses (S1b with demotion off: 0.971). Decided 2026-10-14. (Spec "Hand-over", "Open items".) |
| D23 | **A leaf keeps its relay through a fade** (spec "Relay choice", spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46)). It chooses the strongest relay by the mean RSSI of its last two heartbeats after listening a worst-case rotation (195 s) from the first heartbeat it hears, or at once if it has a message to send; it switches only to a relay heard over two heartbeats and 8 dB stronger; it gives its relay up only after failed hand-overs in two keepalive periods in a row, or 390 s without hearing it; and it holds a message it cannot hand over for up to the 10-minute delivery TTL instead of failing it at once. The leaf tracks four relays besides its own; `Leaf` grows from 704 to 832 B. | The leaf attached to the first relay it heard, and one failed keepalive, three attempts inside one fade, cost it its relay; texts sent meanwhile failed at once with `NoRelay`. The 8 dB is two standard deviations of the difference of two two-heartbeat means under the scenarios' 4 dB fading (heartbeats of one relay reach a leaf ≈130 s apart, past the 60 s fading correlation time). Simulator S2 (`scenarios/s2-block.toml`), settled-phase delivery, seeds 1–10: 0.793 mean (pooled 221/274) before, **0.861 (230/264)** after, worst seed 0.621 → 0.700; leaf relay changes 174 → 70 an hour over 80 leaves; losses `NoRelay` 29 → 4, `QueueExpired` 13 → 21, `NoAck` 4 → 0, unaccounted 7 → 8. Seeds 1–100: 0.800 → 0.878, relay changes 179 → 73 an hour. Held messages lengthen the tail: per-seed median p90 latency 56 s → 176 s (p50 unchanged, 241 ms). The margin matters little: 6, 8 and 12 dB give 0.880, 0.878 and 0.875 with 84, 73 and 65 relay changes an hour (100 seeds). Most relay changes left follow two failed periods on indoor links within a few dB of sensitivity, after which the leaf takes the next strongest relay and stays there; most messages lost are queued at a relay the leaf has left (`QueueExpired`). S1 is unchanged (40 of 40 seeds 1.0); S1b moves from 0.958 to 0.923 over seeds 1–20, but 0.947 against 0.945 over seeds 1–100, within the spread between seeds. Decided 2026-10-14. |
| D24 | **The home channel's busy estimate counts only foreign traffic** (spec "Busy estimate"). Packets addressed to the relay, the ACKs of its own hand-overs, and a home reading taken while one of them was on the air are left out; receptions addressed to others, heartbeats included, and readings above the threshold at other times count. A home reading is held one sweep interval (1 s) until the packet it may have fallen in has ended. | A relay's own traffic follows it to any channel; counting it made a busy relay leave a channel for its own load, which then came along, and messages handed over before its neighbours heard the new channel failed. The three-hour duty-budget scenario (`tests/routing.rs`, F forwarding at its 10 % cap, run with the channels free instead of fixed, 4 h): F's home moves 1.25 → 0 an hour, D's 1.25 → 0.75, O's 0 → 0. D's remaining moves come from the frames that test discards on the way to D: on the air at D but never received, they cannot be told from other nodes' traffic, as a real packet that fails its CRC cannot. A relay sent a full frame every 2 s (7 % of its channel) moved and lost a message before; now it stays, its home estimate at 0.09 % (`a_relay_does_not_flee_its_own_traffic`). S1, S1b and S2 are unchanged: no relay moves after its boot choice in them, before or after, since no channel is more than ≈0.3 % busy. Decided 2026-10-14. |
| D25 | **A demoted neighbour is the last resort** (spec "Neighbour table", "Next hop"). When no live undemoted neighbour is left to try, the next-hop function takes demoted ones, in the same order, before returning to the parent or dropping; their failures count as usual. | Demotion is meant to stop a failing link from costing every message its attempts while another route exists, not to drop messages whose only route it is. In S1b two hand-overs lost to hidden-terminal collisions demote B, the only route between A and C, and both then dropped every message for 390 s. Simulator S1b, settled-phase delivery, seeds 1–20: 0.923 mean, worst seed 0.652, before; **0.957, worst 0.913**, after; seeds 1–100: 0.945 → 0.961, worst 0.633 → 0.900. Only the seeds that lost a demotion's worth of messages change; what is left is messages whose three attempts all collided. S1 and S2 settled phases are unchanged. The cost: a neighbour that is really gone is tried, three attempts, by every message that has no other way, until its entry expires 390 s after its last heartbeat; in S1 after B is removed, A drops each message after ≈1 s of attempts instead of at once for the first eleven messages (`line_of_three_loses_its_middle`). Decided 2026-10-14. |
| D26 | **The duty-cycle budget is a token bucket** (spec "Regulatory limits", "Duty cycle cap"; `tables/budget.rs`), replacing the rolling one-hour window. Depth 6 s, one minute at 10 %; rate (360 s − 6 s) per hour, 9.83 %; a transmission starts only if the bucket holds its airtime. Any interval of length L then carries at most 6 s + 9.83 % × L, so any hour at most 360 s, the 10 % of ERC/REC 70-03 band g1 (proof in the module documentation); a rate of a full 10 % with a 6 s depth would allow 366 s in an hour. The heartbeat reserve becomes one heartbeat, 11.84 ms, which the bucket refills in 120 ms. A relay refuses a packet to send on (no ACK, spec "Receiving DATA") when its send queue with that packet could not go out within the 2.38 s its sender spends retrying. `Budget` is 16 B for both roles: the `Relay` drops from 49 880 to 49 416 B, the `Leaf` from 832 to 736 B. | The window let a relay at the cap spend its 360 s in a burst and then fall silent for most of an hour. The setup of the three-hour duty-budget test (`tests/routing.rs`: O offers F 2.5 times what F's budget carries), F's airtime per minute: before, 34 minutes at ≈10.4 s (17 %) then 33 minutes at 0.1 s (heartbeats only), over and over, 75 of the 180 minutes with traffic below 0.5 s; longest wait for the budget 1019 s, longest gap between F's DATA frames 1619 s. After: 10.5 s in the first minute, then 5.9 s in every minute; longest wait 1.4 s, longest gap 1.9 s. F forwards 2307 messages instead of 2437 (−5.3 %): the lower rate costs 1.7 %, the rest is hand-overs to D repeated because D's ACKs were lost under O's retries of the messages F refused, which a relay at the edge of its bucket provokes all the time; 32 messages went back to O after four unanswered attempts (spec "Return to parent"). With the old refusal rule (refuse unless the budget allows the packet at once) F also dropped 24 such returns, which O, out of budget itself, refused; the queue rule removes them. S1, S1b and S2 are unchanged: no relay there spends more than 0.5 % of its time on air. Decided 2026-10-14. |
| D27 | **A relay refuses with BUSY** (spec "Receiving DATA", "Hand-over", "Duty cycle cap", "Packet formats"; replaces the refusal rule of D26 and the silent refusal of D17). BUSY, type 0x7, is the first byte and the refusing relay's short name: 5 bytes, 10.56 ms, sent where the ACK would have gone, with the same listen-before-talk. A relay refuses a packet that would give it work, to send on or to deliver, when its send queue is full or when its bucket holds less than the **reply reserve** and an ACK. The reserve is 22 BUSY replies, those the bucket refills in the 2.38 s a sender spends retrying an unanswered hand-over (9.83 % × 2.38 s = 234 ms, ⌊234 / 10.56⌋ = 22), and a heartbeat: 244.2 ms. Forwarding and the ACKs that take on work leave the reply reserve in the bucket; replies leave the heartbeat reserve; heartbeats leave nothing. A packet that gives no work (spec "Receiving DATA" cases 2, 3, 5) is still ACKed: BUSY would send its sender on with a second copy. The sender matches BUSY against its hand-over's receiver and treats it as a listen-before-talk give-up: the neighbour is set aside for this hand-over, not counted as tried, and no failure is counted. It also marks the neighbour busy for 2.48 s, the reserve's refill time; until then other messages take it only after every other candidate, demoted ones included. A refused return to the parent or send-back uses one attempt and is dropped after the last, as an unanswered one was, but no longer counts a failure against the receiver. A leaf told BUSY keeps its relay and the message and offers it again after the hold. `Neighbour` grows from 40 to 48 B: `Relay` 49 416 → 49 928 B, `Leaf` 736 → 744 B. | Silence made a refusing relay look like a failing one: its senders spent their 2.38 s of retries on its channel, as full frames that collide with its own traffic, and counted failures toward demoting a link that works. The three-hour duty-budget test (`tests/routing.rs`: O offers F 2.5 times what F's bucket carries; same seeds), before → after: F forwards 2333 → 2372 (+1.7 %), returns 32 → 1 to O; D receives 2363 → 2373; O's frames to F 7584 → 4746, of them retransmissions 3993 → 426; D's ACKs lost at F under O's frames 307 → 176; longest gap between F's DATA frames 1.91 s both. All 1947 of F's refusals are for a full queue, none below the reserve: forwarding never takes the bucket below it, so only replies can. The price is latency: with refusal only when full, F's queue stays full and drains at the bucket's rate, and O→D latency goes from a median of 13.5 s (max 21.0 s) to 72.1 s (max 78.9 s), each message here costing F three frames. Refusing also when the queue cannot go out within the hold gives 2333 forwarded and a median of 7.8 s (max 10.1 s); left open as O15. Simulator S1b and S2, seeds 1–100, are unchanged (S1b 0.961, worst 0.900; S2 0.878, worst 0.619): no relay in them comes near its cap, and none sends a BUSY. A test floods F with refusals for 65 minutes: its replies run the bucket down to the heartbeat reserve, it stays at 354 s an hour, and no heartbeat waits (`replies_stay_within_the_reserve`). BUSY as decided here named no message, so two senders on one channel handing to the same relay within one reply time, ≈100 ms, could both take a BUSY meant for one of them; D30 adds 16 bits of the message ID. Decided 2026-10-14. |
| D28 | **Scenarios that stress the search, and accounting that names every loss** (`scenarios/`, `simulator-design.md` §6). Three scenarios join S1, S1b and S2: `corridor.toml`, five relays in a line with a leaf at each end, both sending every 30 s from the same instant, so two flows meet head on at every relay; `mesh-burst.toml`, 25 relays at random in a 2.5 km square with S2's link model and a leaf at each, every leaf sending across the mesh at the same instant every 10 minutes; `mesh-spread.toml`, the same with each leaf starting at its own time and the middle relay restarting halfway. A scripted `restart` powers a node off and on: it keeps its position or leaf ID and loses everything else, as a solar relay does after a night. The summary sorts every message into delivered, lost (where and why), in flight (a core still holds it at the end) and unaccounted, and marks a message unreachable when no chain of relays hearing each other above sensitivity joins its origin's relay to its destination's at the start; the delivery ratio is also given over the reachable ones. The scenario tests fail if any message to a reachable destination is unaccounted. | One line, one flow and Poisson traffic never made many relays meet crossing traffic at once, and the bug and the gridlock this set found (D17's revision of 2026-10-15; an unmerged one-place send queue that delivered 44 % of the corridor) went unseen. Counting a message to an isolated relay as the protocol's loss, and a message still queued at the end as lost without a trace, hid what the count is for: S2's 103 unaccounted messages over seeds 1–100 were all in flight. A message to an unreachable destination can still end untraced when it has split in two (spec "Why the search finds a path"): both copies search the same relays, and a relay whose search for one has ended absorbs the other; one such message in mesh-burst, seed 5. Settled phase at this commit (D1–D27 and D17's revision), seeds 1–10: corridor 0.969 mean, worst 0.934 (seeds 1–3: 0.946, 0.934, 0.978), all 29 losses `NoRoute` at the end relays after three unanswered attempts, median latency 0.72 s; mesh-burst 0.984, worst 0.840, every reachable message delivered (984 of 984), 16 unreachable on seed 5, median latency 1.7 s; mesh-spread 0.973, worst 0.833, reachable 876 of 886, 14 unreachable on seed 5, median latency 0.25 s. S1, S1b and S2 are unchanged. Decided 2026-10-15. |
| D29 | **Overload becomes pushback, not latency and not silent loss.** The rules, decided together; all are built, (3) by D30. Each becomes its own commit, measured on S1, S1b, S2, the overload test, the corridor and the meshes, in this order. (1) *Admission by rate, not by occupancy.* A relay takes a packet that gives it work only if the airtime of everything it holds, that packet included, fits in its duty bucket as it stands (D26, with D27's reserves left in); otherwise it answers BUSY. The send queue's places follow from the bucket's depth over a full frame and its ACK, about 40; the memory comes from the delivery store, sized by how long leaves sleep. The store feeds the queue whenever there is room, and a message for an own leaf is refused when the store is full, not displaced. Closes O15. *Built 2026-10-15* (spec "Receiving DATA", "Hand-over", "Duty cycle cap", "Delivery queue", "Open items"; `core-interface.md` §6): a relay takes a packet to send on only if its send queue has a place and its bucket holds the ACK, the airtime of every queued frame and of the new one, and the reply reserve; its own application's message likewise, without the ACK. The queue has ⌈6 s / 150.4 ms⌉ = 40 places: for full frames the bucket binds first (a full bucket takes at most 38 with the reply reserve left), for short frames the places, which forty short hand-overs empty in a few seconds. The delivery queue gives up the memory, 64 → 41 places: a leaf that listens takes its DELIVER at the first hand-over (spec section "Leaf receive mode" (before D46)), so the queue holds messages only for dark leaves, and leaves in one train or basement go dark together, so it holds what arrives in one 10-minute TTL for a full leaf table, 256 leaves × 23 texts a day / 144 = 40.9. A message for an own leaf that finds it full is refused with BUSY. The delivery queue feeds the send queue whenever a hand-over ends, except to a leaf whose last DELIVER went unanswered, which waits for its keepalive or the 60 s retry; a DELIVER the bucket cannot fit yet is offered again the moment it will, as a deferred hand-over is started. `Relay` 49 928 → 50 344 B, tables 49 276 → 49 676 B. Overload test, same seeds, before → after: F forwards 2372 → 2337, D receives 2373 → 2342, F refuses 1947 for a full queue → 1978 for the bucket, latency median 72.1 → 2.0 s, max 78.9 → 4.7 s, longest gap between F's DATA frames 1.9 → 4.1 s. Both spend F's whole bucket; before, F also forwarded 15 from its full queue in the ten minutes after O stopped, so within the three hours 2357 → 2337 (−0.8 %), each message costing F 3.10 DATA frames instead of 3.08 (5 returns to O instead of 1). The longer gap is F, its queue empty, waiting for an offer its bucket fits; the bucket fills meanwhile. Settled phase, before and after alike: S1 1.000 (seeds 1–40); S1b 0.961, worst 0.900, S2 0.878, worst 0.619 (seeds 1–100); corridor 0.969, worst 0.934, every message reachable; mesh-burst 0.984, worst 0.840, reachable 1.000 (984 of 984); mesh-spread 0.973, worst 0.833, reachable 0.989, worst 0.987 (876 of 886); seeds 1–10, no reachable message unaccounted. Every summary is identical to before: no relay there comes near its bucket or fills either queue. (2) *Busy neighbours are banned and routed around, not waited for.* A BUSY bans its sender's neighbour for the time that neighbour's bucket needs to refill a full frame and its ACK, 150.4 ms / 9.83 % = 1.53 s, and a banned neighbour is not a candidate (replaces D27's "taken last"). A search whose every remaining candidate is banned ends as one exhausted by tried and failed candidates: it returns to the parent and, at the origin, drops (spec "Return to parent"). A leaf told BUSY holds, as D27 says. *Revised by D31:* such a message waits for the earliest ban to end and is routed again, wherever it is; a return or send-back refused with BUSY waits for the ban without using an attempt. *Built 2026-10-15* (spec "Neighbour table", "Next hop", "Return to parent", "Hand-over", "Duty cycle cap", spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46); `tables/budget.rs` `BAN`): the ban is a full frame and its ACK over the bucket's rate, since the refusal counts the ACK too; a banned neighbour is no candidate, and a demoted one is still the last resort. A search whose every remaining candidate is banned ends as one exhausted by failures: the message goes back to the parent or, at the origin, is dropped and traced, and no application is told (rule (6)); a relay's own application's message is held instead, below. A leaf told BUSY offers again after 1.53 s. Two variants measured: (a) a search exhausted only by bans ends there, as built; (b) it keeps the message at the front of the send queue, with its airtime, and chooses again when the first ban ends. S1 1.000; S1b 0.961, worst 0.900; S2 0.878, worst 0.619; corridor 0.969, worst 0.934; mesh-burst reachable 1.000; mesh-spread reachable 0.989, worst 0.987; no reachable message unaccounted; identical under (a), (b) and rule (1): none of them sends a BUSY. Overload test, (a) → (b): F forwards 2338 → 2271 (rule (1): 2337), D receives 2345 → 2278 (2342), F refuses 1970 → 4316, O's frames to F 4534 → 7588, of them repeats 219 → 5286, D's ACKs lost at F 174 → 196, latency median 2.0 → 6.0 s, max 4.7 → 85.6 s, longest gap between F's DATA frames 5.2 → 4.1 s. With F its only neighbour, O under (a) drops a message at once after F's BUSY and while the ban lasts (1982, its application told of 1975); under (b) its queue and bucket fill with waiting messages and its application's sends are refused instead (2018, plus 26 dropped after unanswered attempts, 2042 told), and F lost 5 returns that O, full of waiting messages, refused. (a) is chosen: under (b) two neighbours whose queues are full of messages waiting for each other's ban refuse each other forever, since what a relay holds drains only as it sends, and in the overload test (b) cost 2.9 % of throughput and 2.2 times the BUSY airtime (45.6 s against 20.8 s), O offering each waiting message once per ban. Known costs of (a): a full delivery store refuses for as long as its leaf is dark, so a sender with no other way drops the message at once rather than offering it again later (`a_full_delivery_queue_refuses_and_a_dark_leaf_waits`); and a full relay refuses its children's returns, which the child drops after its attempts, so a dead-end detour under overload loses the message: with X–A–B–Y and A and B each holding 40 messages for the far end, 78 of the 80 were lost this way under either variant. (3) *A refusal is said.* BUSY stays, and carries the low 16 bits of the message ID in the two bytes that cost no airtime at SF5. *Built by D30.* (4) *Taking a packet means being able to answer it.* One owed-reply place; a relay takes or refuses a packet only when that place is free, and is otherwise silent, which the sender's ordinary retry covers. *Built 2026-10-15* (spec "Receiving DATA", "Hand-over"; `core-interface.md` §6; `node/transfer.rs`): the reply list of nine, sized by how many ATTACH frames fit in one ACK timeout, becomes one place for both roles, and a relay takes or refuses a DATA or ATTACH only if the place is free or already holds that packet's reply, for an earlier copy. `Relay` 50 344 → 50 080 B, `Leaf` 744 → 736 B. The place is taken by a reply that waits out a busy channel, or whose check runs on the relay's own home, where it still hears: in mesh-burst 18 packets over seeds 1–10 found it so, in S2 one DATA and three ATTACH over seeds 1–100, in S1b, the corridor, mesh-spread and the overload test none. S1 1.000; S1b 0.961, worst 0.900; S2 0.878 → 0.879 (2321 → 2322 of 2635), worst 0.619; corridor 0.969, worst 0.934; mesh-burst reachable 1.000 (984 of 984); mesh-spread reachable 0.989, worst 0.987; no reachable message unaccounted; overload test unchanged, F forwards 2338, D receives 2345, latency median 2.0 s, max 4.7 s, O's application told of 1975 failures. Only the runs in which a packet found the place taken differ, each from that packet on. *Built for the leaf 2026-10-15* (spec "Delivery"; `node/leaf.rs`): a leaf takes a DELIVER only when it can owe its ACK, and otherwise ignores it for the relay's retry, where before it passed the message to its application unanswered; every summary above is identical. (5) *The radio's order.* An owed reply, then a due heartbeat, then the hand-over, then a sweep reading; the application only when the core says it is ready. *Built*: `relay.rs` `pump` already starts the radio's next job in this order, and the application's part is rule (6). (6) *The application's send returns nothing.* Either the node is trying to send the message or it is waiting to try. No accepted, sent or failed event: the first-hop ACK is never shown, so no client can read it as delivered. Leaf and relay have the same application interface: a readiness query and an inbound message event. *Built 2026-10-15* (spec "Addresses", "Return to parent", "Hand-over", spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46); `core-interface.md` §1, §4, §6; `docs/simulator-design.md`): `Core::accepts_send(now)` next to `next_wakeup`; `AppEvent` is only `Message`, and `FailReason` is gone. A relay accepts while it is running, its send queue takes a full frame without the ACK on top of what it holds, as rule (1) admits its own messages, and its delivery queue has a place; it wakes when the bucket will let it, so a waiting application is not left until the next event. A leaf accepts while it holds no message; one it gives up after the delivery TTL is traced as dropped (`DropReason::NoRelay`). A `Send` while not ready, or over 234 bytes, is ignored, and the test driver panics. The simulator's applications hold one message each and offer it the moment the core accepts; one due while the last still waits is skipped, and latency counts from when it was due. It computes `MsgId::of` itself to follow a message. `Relay` 50 080 → 50 096 B. S1 1.000, no waits; S1b 0.961 → 0.962 (8869 of 9224 → 8871 of 9222), worst 0.900, 4 waited, longest 30.8 s, 2 skipped; S2 0.879 (2322 → 2323 of 2635), worst 0.619, 4 waited (1 at the end), longest 415 s, none skipped; corridor 0.969, worst 0.934; mesh-burst reachable 1.000; mesh-spread reachable 0.989, worst 0.987; no waits there, no reachable message unaccounted. Every wait is a leaf holding a message it could not hand over, a failed hand-over waiting for the next keepalive period in S1b, a leaf without a relay in S2, when the next one came; before, that one failed at once with `QueueFull` and was lost. Overload test unchanged: F forwards 2338, D receives 2345, F refuses 1970, latency median 2.0 s, max 4.7 s; O drops 1982 for want of a route and its application never waits: under rule (2) O drops each message F refuses at once, so O's queue and bucket always have room. That loss now reaches no application. *Held like a leaf's, 2026-10-15* (spec "Addresses", "Return to parent", "Hand-over"; `core-interface.md` §4, §6): a relay holds its application's message, one at a time, from when it takes it until its first hop does, and accepts no other meanwhile. A search for it that only bans exhaust sets it aside, with its place and airtime kept in the admission of rule (1), and routes it again when the earliest ban ends; one exhausted by tried and failed neighbours is dropped as before. A message for an own leaf that finds the delivery queue full waits there for a place, so readiness no longer looks at the delivery queue. Forwards for others are not held, so the deadlock of variant (b) cannot form: the held message is one frame that does not grow. `Relay` 50 096 → 50 384 B. Scenarios unchanged, all of them: only leaves originate there. Overload test, before → after: O's application waits instead of O dropping, NoRoute drops at O 1982 → 91 (each after F stayed silent through all three attempts), 2141 of 2180 offers waited, longest 11.8 s, 2140 due messages skipped; but F forwards 2338 → 1989 and D receives 2345 → 2089 (−11 %). O offers its held message once per ban, 1.53 s, faster than the 2.5 s at which the application offered new ones before: F refuses 1970 → 4507 times, O's frames to F 4534 → 7562, D's ACKs lost at F under them 174 → 464, and O spends its own bucket on the offers, so it refuses 100 of F's returns, which F drops, where it refused none. Latency from when the message was due: median 2.0 → 10.1 s, max 4.7 → 20.2 s; from when O took it, median 6.3 s, max 14.3 s. *Hold doubled, 2026-10-15* (spec "Return to parent", "Hand-over", spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46); `tables/budget.rs` `hold`): offered once a ban, the held message is a 9 % probe on the refuser's channel that hides its ACKs and spends the holder's bucket, so after the k-th refusal in a row it waits one ban times 2^(k−1), up to the bucket's whole refill, 61 s (spec "Duty cycle cap"), and the wait starts again from one ban when the message is taken or dropped; a leaf's hold after BUSY doubles the same way. Overload test, held once a ban → doubled: F forwards 1989 → 2159 (commit 2: 2338), D receives 2089 → 2218 (2345), O's frames to F 7562 → 7575 (4534), of them repeats 5382 → 5286, F refuses 4507 → 3314, D's ACKs lost at F 464 → 293 (174), O drops 91 → 77 for want of a route, F drops 100 → 53 returns O refused, offers waited 2141 → 2109 of 2180 → 2289, longest 11.8 → 14.1 s, skipped 2140 → 2031; latency from when due median 10.1 → 9.5 s, max 20.2 → 19.0 s, from when O took it median 6.3 → 6.2 s, max 14.3 → 15.3 s. Still 5.4 % below commit 2 in D's count: of O's 5286 repeated frames, 3314 follow a BUSY and the rest are retries after F stayed silent, which the hold does not touch. Scenarios identical: only leaves originate there and none is told BUSY. (7) *Addresses are read at the moment of sending* from the latest heartbeats: the next hop, the parent's channel for a return, the leaf's listen mode for a DELIVER. *Built 2026-10-15* (spec "Neighbour table", "Message records", "Return to parent", "Hand-over"): before each attempt a frame's channel is read again, a DATA's from the neighbour table, a DELIVER's from this relay's home, with the preamble of the leaf's receive mode in the leaf table then. A return goes to the parent's home as the table has it; the channel kept in the record is used only for a parent that has left the table, so it stays. A DATA packet's `aux` now also sets its sender's channel in the table: it is where the sender listened when it sent, newer than its last heartbeat if it has moved since, and without it a return would go to a stale table entry where the record had it right. Already so: the next hop chosen from the table when the hand-over starts, ownership checked again then, and a DELIVER sent on this relay's home as it is at transmission. Not so before: a return used the record's channel, and a retry kept the channel of the first attempt, so neither followed a receiver that had moved, and a DELIVER kept the preamble of the mode its leaf had when it was queued. `a_return_goes_to_the_parent_s_home_as_it_is_now`: P moves home while its child C searches; before, C returned the message to P's old home and it was lost, now it reaches D. S1 1.000; S1b 0.961, worst 0.900; S2 0.878, worst 0.619; corridor 0.969, worst 0.934; mesh-burst reachable 1.000; mesh-spread reachable 0.989, worst 0.987; no reachable message unaccounted; overload test F forwards 2338, D receives 2345, latency median 2.0 s, max 4.7 s, O's application told of 1975 failures: every summary identical to before, event counts included: no frame there went elsewhere. | Measured 2026-10-14/15 on the three-hour overload test (O offers F 2.5 times what F's bucket carries) and the scenarios. A 16-place queue drains at the bucket's rate, median latency 72 s. A one-place slot gave 3.3 s at the same throughput but gridlocked under crossing traffic once relays held for each other: corridor 39–44 of 91–93 against 85–89 on `main`, mesh-burst 68–89 of 98–100 against 98–99; with start times spread the two were identical. So the bound must come from what a relay can send, not from what it holds, and nothing may wait on another node's occupancy: buckets refill continuously, so a ban always ends. Silent refusal with the same patience as BUSY lost 6 % of deliveries under overload: every unanswered attempt is a full frame on the refuser's channel, hiding its ACKs eight times as often. The owed-reply list at one place was bit-identical on S1b and S2 over 100 seeds; the delivery store at one place cost S2 five points (0.879 → 0.827), which is why it stays a store. Three holds for a busy neighbour lost 4 % in S1b where eight lost nothing, which is why patience is not a count. A reply before a heartbeat delays the heartbeat 13 ms in 5 s; a heartbeat before a reply can, through its listen-before-talk backoff, cost the ACK its 100 ms and the sender a 138.6 ms retry. A return to the parent used the channel stored when the message arrived (`records.rs`), stale once the parent moves. Decided 2026-10-15. *As the spec summed it up until 2026-11-05:* a send queue of one place per relay locked up under crossing traffic; two flows met head on in a line of relays delivered 39 to 44 of 91 to 93 texts against 85 to 89, and a mesh where everyone sent at once 68 to 89 of 98 to 100 against 98 to 99. |
| D30 | **BUSY carries the low 16 bits of the message ID** (spec "Hand-over", "Packet formats"; `wire.rs`). BUSY is 7 bytes: the first byte, the refusing relay's short name, and the ID's low 16 bits, big-endian. A sender takes a BUSY only if it names both the receiver and the message of its outstanding hand-over, as it does an ACK; a relay and a leaf alike. A relay owes one BUSY per message and channel, where it owed one per channel. | Two senders on one channel handing to the same relay within one reply time, ≈100 ms, both took a BUSY that named only the relay; one of them moved on from a relay that had taken its packet, and the message had a second copy. Now that happens only when the two IDs agree in their low 16 bits, one time in 65 536. The bits cost no airtime: at SF5 a payload of 5, 6 or 7 bytes takes the same 41.25 symbols, 10.56 ms (`radio::time_on_air`, checked in `wire.rs`), so the reply reserve and the busy hold of D27 do not change. The three-hour duty-budget test is unchanged (F forwards 2372, D receives 2373, median latency 72.1 s): its one sender never shares F with another. S1b and S2 send no BUSY. Decided 2026-10-14. |
| D31 | **Overload never drops a message for want of room; it waits.** The only ends of a message are delivery, a hand-over whose three attempts get no reply, a leaf that never wakes, the hop cap, and a node dying (spec "Next hop" to "Hop count", "Hand-over", "Duty cycle cap"). (1) *The regular room stays as D29 built it:* a relay takes a DATA or ATTACH into its send queue only if the airtime of everything it holds, the frame and its ACK fit in the duty bucket now, which gives 40 places, ⌈6 s / 150.4 ms⌉; else BUSY. (2) *Beyond it, one hop place per hop count*, numbered 1 to 254, since a packet received at 255 ends at the hop cap. A packet received with `hops` h that the regular room refuses takes the highest free place numbered h or lower. A hop place holds its frame whatever the bucket holds; the bucket still decides when it is sent. Fresh messages, count 0 from leaves and the relay's own application, use only the regular room, so pushback reaches the application. This cannot lock up: take the highest-numbered place held anywhere, k; its message has made at least k hops, is offered eventually, and its next hop has place k + 1 free, unless it is at the cap, where it ends; by induction every message held moves. The hop cap stays 255. (3) *A forward whose remaining candidates are all banned waits* in the queue until the earliest ban ends and is routed again then, at the origin or anywhere else; it is never returned or dropped for that. A return or send-back refused with BUSY waits for that ban and is sent again without using one of its attempts. A search exhausted by failed attempts alone still returns or drops. Waiting entries keep their place and their airtime in admission. (4) *The queue keeps arrival order,* except that when a neighbour's ban ends, the most travelled frame waiting for it is offered first: the induction needs the highest frame offered, and a lower one refused first would ban the neighbour again every time. Ordering the whole queue by count would starve fresh traffic. (5) *Records lose their idle timer* and are evicted least recently used, never while their message is queued; their number follows from the rate over the time the queue takes to drain, (40 + 254) × 1.53 s = 450 s. (6) *Memory follows from the design, not a target:* the relay grows to about 150 KiB with (5); the board's application owns 255 of its 256 KiB of RAM (`bark-fw/memory.x`), of which the BLE controller takes under 5 KiB (`implementation-research-2026-10-14.md`); the 50 KiB target of `core-interface.md` §6 becomes the derived sum, with the headroom stated. Each rule is its own commit, in the order 3, then 2 and 4 with 6, then 5. This closes the two known costs of D29 rule (2): a search ended by a busy neighbour, and a full relay refusing its children's returns into a drop. Decided 2026-10-15. *Rule 3 built 2026-10-15* (`tables/send_queue.rs`, `relay/forward.rs`): an entry keeps the time it waits until, the queue hands over the first entry that is due, and a return or send-back keeps its unanswered attempts while it waits; the relay's own message waits in the queue as well, and is set aside only for a full delivery queue. Every scenario at its own settings is identical to before. Alone it is the experiment's waiting variant and gives its numbers: the corridor at 1 s per leaf locks up (0.489, 303 a run, 929 held at the end, longest stall 119 s). Overload test: F forwards 2159 → 2141, D receives 2218 → 2203, O drops 77 → 136 for want of a route after D's ACKs were lost at F, F drops 53 → 0 returns. *Rules 2, 4 and 6 built 2026-10-15* (`tables/send_queue.rs` `HOP_PLACES`, `relay/forward.rs` `admit`; `core-interface.md` §6): a queued frame keeps its hop place, 0 for the regular room, until it leaves the queue, and the regular room's bucket test counts the airtime of every frame queued, hop places included. `Relay` 50 776 → 119 864 B, of which the send queue 79 976 B. Settled phase against D29, seeds 1 to 5, 1800 s: corridor at 1 s per leaf 0.468 → 0.902 (worst 0.395 → 0.812), 741 → 1317 a run in the second half, p50 / p90 0.7 / 1.5 s → 1.5 / 6.5 s, losses NoRoute 6551, ReturnFailed 706 → NoRoute 797, RecordLost 229, longest stall 3.0 → 7.6 s; at 0.5 s 0.248 → 0.937, 674 → 1353 a run, p90 2.1 → 34.6 s; the eight-relay ring at 2 s 0.560 → 0.996, 1150 → 2614 a run, p90 4.4 → 5.8 s; at 1 s 0.596 → 0.986, 1331 → 2353; mesh-burst at 64 times its traffic, seeds 1 to 3, reachable 0.991 → 0.996, 3516 → 3508 a run, BounceFailed 137 → 57, one message ended at the hop cap. The losses left under overload are searches ended by unanswered attempts (NoRoute, ReturnFailed) and records expired while their message waited (RecordLost), which rule (5) removes. The mesh carries slightly less than before because messages whose searches run long are now carried to the end rather than dropped, and a relay at its cap passing them on keeps its own leaves waiting, up to 272 s for one leaf in seed 1. Every scenario at its own settings is identical. Overload test: F forwards 2141 → 2300, D receives 2203 → 2308, O drops 136 → 46. *Rule 5 built 2026-10-15* (spec "Message records", "Hop count", "Why the search finds a path", "Bounds", "Implementation notes"; `tables/records.rs`, `relay/forward.rs` `record_place`; `core-interface.md` §6): a record has no lifetime. A new one takes the place of the least recently active record whose message the relay does not hold, in its send queue, the hand-over included, or set aside for a full delivery queue. The table holds what the duty cycle lets a relay handle in the 449.7 s a full send queue takes to drain at one ban a place, 1494, plus the 294 messages a send queue holds, whose records stay however old: 1788 records, where 626 covered 188.3 s. *Corrected 2026-10-16:* the duty cycle lets a relay send in that time what a full bucket holds at its start as well as the refill, 6 s + 9.83 % × 457.3 s = 50.97 s, not 10 % of it, so the table is 1561 + 294 = 1855 records (spec "Bounds"). A new message that finds every record held is refused with BUSY (`DropReason::NoRecord`); the table outnumbers the places, so it cannot happen. `RecordLost` is gone. `Relay` 119 864 → 157 040 B, 80 KiB under what the board leaves it. Settled phase against rules 2, 4 and 6, seeds 1 to 5, 1800 s: corridor at 1 s per leaf 0.902 → 0.950 (worst 0.812 → 0.905), 1317 → 1333 a run in the second half, p50 / p90 1.5 / 6.5 → 1.6 / 8.1 s, max 329 → 494 s, losses NoRoute 797, RecordLost 229, ReturnFailed 4 → NoRoute 549, ReturnFailed 3, held at the end 145 → 22, longest stall 7.6 → 6.2 s; at 0.5 s 0.937 → 0.930 (worst 0.922 → 0.892), 1353 → 1315 a run, p90 34.6 → 37.1 s, max 309 → 311 s, NoRoute 489, RecordLost 107 → NoRoute 660. That drop is the spread between seeds, which diverge from the first record that would have expired: over seeds 1 to 30, at 0.5 s 0.918 → 0.936 (worst 0.807 → 0.892), 1300 → 1308 a run; at 1 s 0.926 → 0.939 (worst 0.812 → 0.834), 1316 → 1308 a run. The relays there are at their cap, so rule 5 changes which messages arrive, not how many: those that lost their record now arrive late, p90 at 1 s 9.0 → 12.1 s over 30 seeds, and the longest, 805 s, is a leaf's message held 590 s at its origin relay; before, its record would have expired after 188 s and the message been dropped. It was passed over because when a ban ends, each waiting entry that finds its neighbour banned again is moved to the front in turn, which reverses the order of the waiting entries at every ban: the queue does not keep arrival order among them, as rule (4) says it should. That was a fault of rule (3)'s queue, not of rule (5); it is fixed below. NoRoute at 1 s over 30 seeds rises 3212 → 3748: 136 of them are messages that would have lost their record, the rest searches ended by unanswered attempts in a network that now carries those messages too. No packet found a record older than the old lifetime (counted at 1 s, seeds 1 to 8), so no search went differently for a record kept longer. The ring at 1 and 2 s per leaf, mesh-burst at 64 times, S1, S1b, S2, the corridor, mesh-burst and mesh-spread at their own settings give identical summaries: nothing there depended on how long a record lived. Overload test identical: F forwards 2300, D receives 2308, O drops 46. *The delivered window likewise, 2026-10-15* (spec "Owner", "Delivery queue"; `tables/delivered.rs`): its 64 IDs no longer expire after the delivery TTL; when it is full, the one delivered or last repeated longest ago makes way. Its timer was no promise to anyone; the delivery queue's TTL, which is the wait for a dark leaf, stays. The size is unchanged, what a relay delivers in 10 minutes at the load the design plans for. `Relay` 157 040 → 157 024 B. Every run above, and the overload test, identical to rule 5 alone; no message delivered twice. *Arrival order kept, 2026-10-15* (`tables/send_queue.rs`, `relay/forward.rs` `prepare`): choosing an entry for its hand-over no longer moves it to the front. The hand-over refers to its entry where it stands, and the set-aside neighbours (`skipped`) stay with that entry, so of the waiting entries with equal counts the one that arrived first is offered first at every ban's end, as rule (4) says (`waiting_entries_keep_arrival_order_through_bans`, which the moving queue fails: it reversed the four entries). `Relay` size unchanged. Settled phase against rule 5, 1800 s, seeds 1 to 30: corridor at 1 s per leaf 0.939 → 0.936 (worst 0.834 both), 1308 → 1316 a run in the second half, p50 / p90 1.7 / 12.1 → 2.0 / 44.5 s, max 805 → 121 s, NoRoute 3748 → 3875, ReturnFailed 33 → 38, held at the end 382 → 507; at 0.5 s 0.936 → 0.942 (worst 0.892 → 0.902), 1308 → 1344 a run, p90 42.5 → 42.1 s, max 774 → 160 s, NoRoute 3518 → 3183, held at the end 661 → 643; the ring at 1 s 0.987 → 0.987, 2328 → 2346 a run, max 335 → 225 s; at 2 s 0.996 → 0.995 (worst 0.991 → 0.988), 2581 → 2566 a run, max 282 → 281 s, NoRoute and ReturnFailed 358 → 402, held at the end 350 → 419. The waits are now shared: over the corridor's 30 runs at 1 s, p99 246 → 82 s and no message waits over 120 s where 1545 did, but more wait 20 to 120 s (3853 → 9651 over 20 s), which is the higher p90; at 0.5 s p99 235 → 87 s. The ring at 2 s loses 0.1 points, 1.8 standard errors over the 30 paired seeds, with 9 % more offers refused with BUSY; the likely reason, not shown, is that the entry offered first is now the one that has waited longest, which is the one for the busiest neighbour. One message in the corridor at 1 s, seed 20, is lost without a trace by a fault older than this change: an ACK names only the message and the receiver, so when a split copy and a return of the same message are handed to the same relay at once, the ACK for one ends the other's hand-over, and the return is never received. S1, S1b, S2, the corridor, mesh-burst, mesh-spread and mesh-burst at 64 times: identical summaries. Overload test unchanged. | Measured 2026-10-15 on an experiment branch, 1800 s, seeds 1 to 5. In the corridor with both leaves sending every second, about 0.95 messages a second crossing every relay, D29 as built delivered 0.468 of what the network took, 741 a run in the second half, p90 latency 1.5 s, and dropped 6551 messages for want of a route after bans ended their searches. Waiting without a reserve locked up: every relay stood still for 16 to 119 s a run with its queue full of messages for its neighbours, only record expiry freed places (3065 messages lost with their records), and it delivered 0.489 of the fewer messages it took, 303 a run, p90 115 s. Waiting with 16 hop places offered most travelled first delivered 0.885, 1200 a run, p90 32 s, longest stall 16 s. At one message every 0.5 s per leaf the same three gave 0.248, 0.430 and 0.820. In an eight-relay ring at 2 s per leaf, D29 delivered 0.560 and the hop places 0.993 (1150 and 2601 a run). Two things that experiment did wrong shaped the rules: its hop cap of 16, the number of places, dropped reachable messages whose searches in a random mesh ran longer (219 of 17 134 at 64 times the traffic), so the places run to the full count; and ordering the whole queue by count starved fresh messages (931 lost with their records at 0.5 s), so only the frames that waited are ordered. The price is latency, p90 from about 1.5 s to tens of seconds under overload, and an application kept waiting where the network is full. |
| D32 | **One reply frame, the ACK, answers every offer, taken or refused, and names both parties** (spec "Receiving DATA", "Hand-over", "Duty cycle cap", spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46), "Delivery queue", "Packet formats"; `wire.rs`, `node/transfer.rs`; replaces the BUSY frame of D27 and D30). An ACK is the first byte, the message ID, `from` and `to`: 13 bytes, 14.40 ms. `from` is the answering relay's short name or leaf's ID; `to` the answered one's: a DATA's `src_hop`, zero from a leaf, whose ID DATA does not carry; the leaf's ID for an ATTACH; for a DELIVER, the short name of the relay the leaf is attached to, since DELIVER does not name its sender. Bit 0 of the ACK's `aux`, unused until now, says refused, and type 0x7 is free again. A hand-over takes an ACK only if message, `from` and `to` all match; the refused bit then decides between the hand-over's end and everything D27, D29 and D31 gave BUSY: the ban, the neighbour set aside, a return waiting out the ban, a leaf offering again after the hold. *Built 2026-10-15.* The longer ACK moves every constant derived from it: a hand-over holds its receiver 138.56 + 14.40 = 152.96 ms (was 150.4), so the ban is 152.96 ms / 9.83 % = 1.56 s (1.53), the retry windows 306 and 612 ms (301, 602), the retrying span 2.39 s (2.38), and the reply reserve ⌊9.83 % × 2.39 s / 14.40 ms⌋ = 16 refusals and a heartbeat, 242.2 ms (22 BUSY replies, 244.2 ms). The send queue keeps 40 regular places, ⌈6 s / 152.96 ms⌉ = ⌈39.2⌉, of which a full bucket fills 37 with full frames (38). The record table is 1401 + 294 = 1695 (1494 + 294 = 1788): a message costs at least 32.64 ms (30.08), over 294 bans, 457.3 s (449.7). `Relay` 157 024 → 154 080 B, `Leaf` 736 → 752 B. `an_answer_to_another_sender_is_not_ours` reproduces the fault: a relay handing a message to D ignores D's answers, taken or refused, to another sender of the same message; with the old matching it fails. Measured against D31 (`18d7ce6`), 1800 s. Every run differs, since every ACK is 2.56 ms longer. A diagnostic build with the new frame and constants and the old matching gives the same summary as D32 in every run of S1, S1b, S2, the corridor, mesh-burst and mesh-spread and in all 60 runs of the corridor at 1 and 0.5 s per leaf; it differs in 17 of 30 runs of the ring at 1 s, 9 of 30 at 2 s and all of mesh-burst at 64 times, where the fault's shape occurs, by no more than the spread between seeds. Settled phase, D31 → D32: S1 1.000; S1b 0.956 → 0.963; S2 0.864 → 0.866; corridor 0.953 → 0.978; mesh-burst reachable 1.000; mesh-spread reachable 0.988. Under overload the longer ACK costs more throughput than its share of airtime. Delivered a run in the second half, seeds 1 to 30: corridor at 1 s 1316 → 1139 (−13 %), at 0.5 s 1344 → 1209 (−10 %), ring at 1 s 2346 → 2148 (−8 %), at 2 s 2566 → 2404 (−6 %), where the hand-over of a 40-byte message grows by 5 %, 50.6 → 53.1 ms. Refusals in the corridor at 1 s double, 26 752 → 52 441, and each refused offer is a DATA frame sent for nothing; p90 latency there 46 → 102 s, held at the end 507 → 1437. With the constants kept at D31's values the loss is the same, and a 12-byte ACK, 13.12 ms, loses about half as much (corridor at 1 s 1234, ring at 2 s 2498 a run): the cost is the ACK's time on air. Mesh-burst at 64 times, seeds 1 to 30: 3458 → 3463 a run, reachable 0.990 → 0.992, unaccounted 37 → 38, so the two copies seen there have another cause. The corridor at 1 s, seed 20, no longer takes the path that lost #1417, and no corridor run loses a message unaccounted (D31: that one). *A pick by hop count alone, tried and rejected 2026-10-15:* the send queue handing over the most travelled due entry first, whether or not it had waited for a ban, in place of D31 rule 4. Seeds 1 to 30, against the ACK change alone: the corridor at 1 s per leaf carried 8 % more (1139 → 1234 a run) with shorter tails (p99 167 → 111 s); the rings and the corridor at 0.5 s were unchanged within the spread; in mesh-burst at 64 times refusals doubled (6234 → 12 444), the worst seed fell from 0.842 to 0.768, and a message that had just entered waited 1179 s at one relay, where the longest such wait under rule 4 was 3.8 s: packets from farther away kept arriving and went ahead of it, so it was offered about once every 50 s instead of at every ban's end. Rule 4 is the bounded form of the same idea. It puts the most travelled first only among the entries a ban has held back, which is all the induction of D31 rule 2 needs, and keeps arrival order otherwise, so a message already taken is offered again at every ban's end. | Found 2026-10-15 in the corridor at 1 s per leaf, seed 20, message #1417. E retried a hand-over to D after a lost ACK while C returned the same message to D. Both waited for an ACK naming the message and D, so D's ACK to one ended the other's hand-over too, and the message D had not taken from that sender was lost without a trace. A BUSY could be taken the same way by the other sender, which then banned D and went elsewhere with a message D had taken, leaving two copies. A separate refusal frame would carry the same names at nearly the same length, so one frame with one bit is simpler. Decided 2026-10-15. |
| D33 | **Rejected: each hop travelled buys one place in the send queue's line.** The second tier of D31 rule 4, arrival order, was changed to the highest (hop count + entries queued since), so a message that arrived with h hops stood as if it had arrived h hand-overs earlier; the first tier, most travelled first among entries a ban held back, was kept. Tried and measured 2026-10-15, not merged. | In the corridor at 1 s per leaf, 100 paired seeds: delivered per run in the second half 1175 → 1245, latency spread 38.8 → 27.7 s, p99 151 → 108 s, refused offers 49 363 → 30 681 over 30 seeds. The corridor at 0.5 s and both rings: unchanged within the spread between seeds, and the longest wait of a fresh message in the 8-ring at 1 s rose 122 → 207 s. The mesh at 64 times its load: delivery −0.0006 (standard error 0.0002), refusals 104 → 322, bounce failures 97 → 201. The worst seed of both corridors fell (0.859 → 0.800 at 1 s) with more no-route drops at the end relays. Breaking ties toward the earlier arrival, so h hops buy h − 1 places, was within one or two standard errors of this everywhere. A gain in one stress case against more drops in its worst seeds and a small loss in the mesh: not taken. |
| D34 | **A relay takes fresh work only when it could start moving it now** (spec "Receiving DATA", "Hand-over", spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46); `core-interface.md` §4; `relay/forward.rs` `bans_end`). Fresh work is a DATA from a leaf, count 0, and the relay's own application's message. Besides the room of D29 and D31, it needs a neighbour the relay could hand it to now: a live one that is not banned, demoted or not, since the message has tried none (spec "Next hop"). If every live neighbour is banned, a leaf's DATA is refused with a refused ACK, as for a full room, and the leaf holds and offers again (spec section "Attach and polls" (before D46)); the relay's application is not accepted (`accepts_send` false) and is woken when the earliest ban ends. A relay with no live neighbour at all takes it as before, and the search ends as spec "Return to parent" says. A packet already in the network, count 1 or more, is taken whatever the bans, into the regular room or a hop place: D31's argument against gridlock needs every such packet to move on. The refusal is traced as `DropReason::Banned`. *Built 2026-10-15.* `Relay` size unchanged. Measured against D32 with the new reception model and with an ACK refreshing its neighbour's entry (spec "Neighbour table", "Hand-over"; the commit before this one), 1800 s (mesh-burst at 64 times 2700 s, its own length). S1, S1b, S2, the corridor, mesh-burst and mesh-spread at their usual seeds, and mesh-burst at 64 times, seeds 1 to 10: identical summaries; no relay there has all its neighbours banned. Overload test identical: O holds one message at a time already. The corridor at 1 s per leaf, seeds 1 to 30, before → after: delivered of taken 0.948 → 0.961 (worst 0.910 → 0.896), of what the applications had to send 0.680 → 0.723, 1173 → 1279 a run in the second half (+106 ± 8, paired), latency from when due p50 / p90 / p99 / max 5.6 / 99.4 / 153.2 / 241 → 1.6 / 6.6 / 14.7 / 73 s, wait at the first relay p90 / p99 90.6 / 141.3 → 0.0 / 4.5 s, refusals 51 043 → 24 888 (8869 of them for bans), leaf offers refused 9625 → 8869, a refused leaf's hold p90 / max 11.1 / 239 → 5.5 / 66 s, held at the end 1264 → 151; at 0.5 s 0.941 → 0.945 (worst 0.891 → 0.878), of demand 0.346 → 0.359, 1225 → 1264 a run (+39 ± 13), p90 54.7 → 13.4 s, max 199 → 124 s, wait at the first relay p90 42.6 → 1.0 s, held at the end 779 → 412. The eight-relay ring at 1 and 2 s per leaf: no change beyond the spread between seeds (2217 → 2217 ± 12 and 2456 → 2462 ± 7 a run); a ring relay seldom has both neighbours banned. Lost for want of a route in the corridor at 1 s 1915 → 2338, 3.2 → 3.7 % of what was taken, at 0.5 s 2806 → 3031, 4.5 → 4.7 %: each is a search ended by a hand-over, anywhere along the line, whose three attempts went unanswered, 1.0 → 1.2 % of hand-overs. The attempts lost in those hand-overs are of the kinds lost before, each a little more often: in the corridor at 1 s, a collision at the receiver with another relay's hand-over to it from the far side, which the sender cannot hear (48 → 56 % of those attempts; 7.2 → 9.3 % of all DATA frames), the receiver transmitting (21 → 17 %), an ACK lost at the sender under a frame it cannot hear (16 → 13 %). Relays no longer sit idle waiting for bans, so the two neighbours of a middle relay offer to it at the same time more often. With random traffic at the same rate the rise is the same, 4.8 → 5.2 % of what was taken, spread over the same kinds, so it is not the leaves' fixed period lining the streams up. Offers made as bans end are a small and falling share of those collisions (both senders 3.5 → 1.4 %). | Under D31 an origin relay whose neighbours were all banned took its leaves' messages anyway, and each waited in its queue for a ban, to be refused again and wait for the next: in the corridor at 1 s per leaf the wait at the first relay was 91 s at p90 and was most of the latency. Overload is better felt at the origin, where the application can see it, than held inside a relay that cannot send it anywhere. Measured, the wait did not move to the leaf: a refused leaf held for 5.5 s at p90, against 11.1 s before, since the relay's room was no longer full of messages waiting for bans, and the network carried 9 % more. The price is more hand-overs lost to collisions between senders that cannot hear each other, which carrying more brings. Decided 2026-10-15. |
| D35 | **A relay ACKs a copy of a message without acting on it only where the message lives on** (spec "Message records" to "Hop count", "Hand-over", "Why the search finds a path", "Bounds"; `tables/records.rs`, `relay/forward.rs` `data`, `classify`, `holds`, `count`). (1) *A retransmission is the same sender and count again.* The record keeps the sender (`src_hop`, 0 for a leaf) and the count of the last DATA it received, in place of the last child, and a packet with that pair is ACKed only. The test "count no higher than the highest received" is gone. (2) *Each hand-over sets its count at its first transmission,* the record's highest count plus one, and its retries repeat it; a hand-over that succeeds raises the highest count to what it carried. A frame that waited or goes to another neighbour after a failed hand-over carries its count as it stands then, and a relay sends a count for a message once but in retries, so one pair is enough. (3) *A copy is ACKed only when the message has arrived,* delivered or queued here, owned here, or taken from this relay by the relay it is addressed to; *when it is a retransmission; or when this relay holds another copy that goes on,* in its send queue or set aside. A hand-over under way whose frame has been on air does not count: its receiver may have taken it with only the ACK lost, and the copy offered may be that one come back. (4) *Otherwise the relay takes the copy and goes on with its own search* from its record, whoever sent it: from a neighbour it handed the message to it is a return, and from any other relay the sender stays a candidate unless tried. Its next candidate, else its parent, else at the origin a drop. Nothing is sent straight back: case 6, the ACK only of case 5, the `returned` mark and the refinement for a relay still holding its searching copy are gone, with `Kind::Bounce`, `DropReason::BounceFailed` and `Trace::Bounced`. A relay's parent never changes. No wire change; type 0x7 stays free. The record stays 32 bytes (the last child's 4 bytes and the parent's channel option go, the pair's 5 come), `RECORDS` 1695, `Relay` 154 080 B. *Built 2026-10-15*, the count first and the rest in the commit after. Checked: the hop places' argument of spec "Hand-over" still holds, as admission is unchanged and a packet received with count h is sent with more than h; spec "Why the search finds a path" is rewritten, since copies now share the relays' records and each ends by merging where the other is held, where the message has arrived, at the origin, or at the hop cap, and never where it is the last. The three faults: (A) the child's return reaches a parent holding nothing, which goes on with it; (B) the child offered again by its parent takes it and returns it at once, and the parent goes on; (C) the copy from another sender with the same count is taken. Core tests reproduce each in three to five relays and lose the message without a trace on the commit before (`routing.rs`: `a_return_to_a_parent_that_gave_up_on_the_child_goes_on`, `a_child_offered_the_message_again_by_its_parent_returns_it`, `a_copy_with_a_count_already_seen_is_not_a_retransmission`); others check that a retransmission and a copy are ACKed only while held, that a copy is ACKed only once the addressed relay took the message, that a return between two relays holding nothing is not handed back, and the count. Measured against the commit before D35, 2700 s for the mesh at 64 times its load, 1800 s otherwise, paired seeds, differences ± one standard error. Mesh-burst at 64 times with the old threshold reception, seeds 1 to 100: messages lost without a trace 36 → 0; delivered of what was taken, reachable, 0.9948 → 0.9956 (worst 0.819 → 0.827, +0.0008 ± 0.0002), of demand 0.978 → 0.983, 3523 → 3541 a run in the second half (+17.6 ± 4.0), airtime 2969 → 2930 s a run (−39.5 ± 12.8), hand-overs that succeeded 22 039 → 21 277 a run (forwards 19 922 → 21 147, returns 238 → 131, sent straight back 1879 → 0), refused 10 658 → 9227, drops BounceFailed 2739 → 0, hop cap 53 → 0, NoRoute 1864 → 2244, ReturnFailed 360 → 276, messages ended by a drop 2698 → 2205 (failed send-backs 864 → 0, no route 1467 → 1835, failed returns 119 → 102, expired in a delivery queue 232 → 254), latency p50 / p90 / p99 / max 1.7 / 3.2 / 8.7 / 801 → 1.8 / 3.3 / 5.9 / 585 s, delivered twice 1 → 3 (the owner's delivered window forgets after 64), second copies reaching the owner 48 813 → 44 395. With the current reception curve: 7 → 0 lost without a trace, 0.9988 → 0.9994 (worst 0.991 → 0.994, +0.0006 ± 0.0001), 3565 → 3572 a run (+7.0 ± 1.7), airtime 2717 → 2691 s (−25.2 ± 8.9), refused 998 → 147, BounceFailed 2143 → 0, hop cap 17 → 0, NoRoute 245 → 337. The corridor at 1 s per leaf, seeds 1 to 30: 0.961 → 0.959 (−0.0020 ± 0.0017), 1279 → 1273 a run (−6.3 ± 3.6), airtime +0.1 ± 2.3 s; at 0.5 s 0.945 → 0.949 (+0.0042 ± 0.0035), 1264 → 1267; the eight-relay ring at 1 s 0.9875 → 0.9871, 2217 → 2213 (−4.1 ± 10.7); at 2 s 0.9956 → 0.9962 (+0.0006 ± 0.0003), 2462 → 2473 (+10.4 ± 8.1); one message lost without a trace in each ring before, none now; latency and hop-cap drops unchanged in the four. S1, S1b, S2 and the corridor at their usual seeds: identical. The six-relay ring, seeds 1 to 5: 0.9952 → 0.9964 (+0.0012 ± 0.0011). Mesh-burst, seeds 1 and 5: all delivered, 246 → 238 hand-overs a run. Mesh-spread, seeds 1 and 5: same delivery, airtime 140.4 → 143.4 s a run (+3.0 ± 1.0), hand-overs 315 → 334; all of the rise is one message a run for a leaf no relay can reach, whose search now visits every relay it can reach and ends at the origin (138 → 159 and 118 → 144 hand-overs), where a failed send-back ended it before. The count alone (the first commit): the corridors identical, the mesh at 64 times with threshold reception 36 → 19 lost without a trace and airtime −27.9 ± 11.7 s a run. Tried and not taken: counting a hand-over whose frame was on air as a held copy, as first proposed, lost 10 messages without a trace in seed 18 of the threshold mesh alone: R7 handed a message to R17 and missed R17's ACK while sending one of its own; R17 sent it on round R21 back to R7, which only ACKed it, holding its hand-over; its retry was then a retransmission for R17, ACKed only. Making a neighbour whose hand-over failed a candidate again when it offers the message, which takes a second mask and a 40-byte record: within one or two standard errors of this everywhere. Leaving out the arrival through the addressed relay: 266 hand-overs a run in mesh-burst at seeds 1 and 5 against 238, as a second copy wandered through every untried link of the mesh; within the spread at 64 times. Taking the sender as parent where a relay has none, as first proposed, lets the parents form a loop: in a model of the search over 20 000 random networks without losses, a search with no owner then ran past 100 000 hand-overs in 2479; without it, the search reached every relay it could reach in all 20 000 and ended at the origin. | An investigation of the mesh at 64 times its load, seeds 1 to 100, on an earlier build, found 54 messages lost without a trace with threshold reception, 3 with the curve, and 1 in the ring over 20 seeds: every copy had ended at a relay that ACKed it while holding none. A return sent straight back to a child that had returned it, which then only ACKed it from its parent (13); a parent offering the message again to a child whose table slot had changed hands, which only ACKed it (20); and a fresh copy taken for a retransmission by its count, which was stale because a frame kept the count it was queued with, or had been raised by another copy (12). Each rule that ACKed a copy assumed the message lived on elsewhere, and a relay cannot see elsewhere. Only what it holds itself, its records of arrival, and the pair it last received are things it knows; so those alone end a packet, and every other copy goes on. A relay that searches on, rather than sending a copy straight back, keeps the search whole: the sender is still its candidate, and the parents bring it back. A silent loss is the one end the design must not have (spec "Hand-over"). Decided 2026-10-15. |
| D36 | **A neighbour is tried only once it has ACKed the message** (spec "Message records", "Next hop", "Return to parent", "Hop count", "Hand-over" step 6, spec "Why the search finds a path", "Bounds"; `tables/records.rs`, `relay/forward.rs` `handover_done`, `handover_failed`, `refused`). The record's `handed` bit is set when a forward succeeds, by the ACK or by a packet from the receiver that ends the hand-over, and no longer at its first transmission. A failed forward, three attempts unanswered, still counts a failure against the neighbour (spec "Neighbour table") and sets it aside for the current choice, as a listen-before-talk give-up and a refusal do, so the next choice is another neighbour; the neighbour stays a candidate the next time the message is routed here. "Tried" now means "known to have the message". A refusal no longer takes the bit back (`Record::not_handed` is gone); `Record::sent_to_child` became `sent` for every frame and `handed_to` on success. A split costs one round trip: the sender offers the message again to the neighbour that took it, which holds it and ACKs only, or has finished with it and returns it. What did not change: admission and the hop places of spec "Hand-over" (a packet received with count h is still sent with more than h), the fresh-work gate of D34 and the bans, which never read the bit. A forward retried after a wait for a ban, with no hand-over between, carries the same count; a neighbour that took the first frame then takes it for the retransmission it is.
