The strip at the spec rate 95.12 → 95.13 %; the loaded corridors and the ring at 2 s identical, the ring at 1 s −0.02 ± 0.30, mesh-burst at 64 times +0.26 ± 0.20, S2 +0.17 ± 0.13 with 15 % more airtime, the rest identical. Of the texts main ages on their way, 78 to 100 % are for nodes without an anchor, and every text ending at the hop cap but one; at ×3 main ends them after about 660 frames, without the time left they run 1100 to 1600 frames over 2000 to 4000 s, up to 35 at once, in the highest hop places, which go first at a ban's end, and the relays' buckets refuse their sleepy nodes more. Control, texts to such nodes not sent, ×3 seed 1: 81.72 → 81.66 %. At ×1 the hop cap ends them first, at 900 to 1000 frames each, 3.7 and 4.6 % of all airtime. Records: full tables at ×3 keep a record 3100 to 3500 s; a message met a lost record 2, 2, 8 times on main and 2, 3, 27 without, at seed 3 for six of those searches, which outlived their records before the hop cap ended them, and three texts delivered late. *Measured 2026-10-19; decided 2026-10-20: the time left is kept, the candidate not adopted; a search that cannot succeed not starting was a later item, dropped 2026-10-30: whether a search can succeed is not known until it reaches the position, and the time left ends the ones that cannot.* |
| D68 | **Relays among the people, as on USB power: no rule for density and full power kept; a relay within 50 m always finds a place in the neighbour table; a node that moves sleeps** (spec "Hardware", "Neighbour table", "Hop count"; `tables/neighbours.rs` `near_victim`, `relay/forward.rs` `count`; the simulator's `relay_share`, `[movement]` and `[vehicles] relay_fraction`; `docs/dense-relays-2026-10-20.md`; `scenarios/dense.toml`, `scenarios/dense-movers.toml`). Asked: what happens when every node on USB power is a relay, so that relays are dense and some move, and whether a relay that hears many relays should lower its power. *Kept:* the simulator places a share of the people's nodes as relays, which still send and receive the texts and may walk or ride, and counts who keeps whom in the neighbour tables; a core fix found on the way, a message that reached its owner at the hop cap was dropped at every try, milliseconds apart, and now goes at the cap; and the candidate proposed: when the table is full and no entry is demoted or stale, a relay within 50 m, the owner radius, takes the place of the least recently heard relay beyond it. *Measured,* S4 with a share of the people's nodes as relays, delivered of the texts sent in the settled window, seed 1: S4 (269 relays) 94.04 %, 1300 relays 94.91 %, 5317 relays 97.06 %, 20 269 (every node) 97.81 %; at three times the rate 73.37, 65.47, 96.86 and 97.54 %; airtime per delivered text 1.36, 5.67, 1.88 and 1.75 s; relay heartbeats 97, 397, 1597 and 5550 s a channel an hour over the domain, the channels at S4's relays 1.8 to 4.3 % busy; a sleepy node's current 0.633 → 0.536 mA at 5317 relays; seed 2 within 0.4 points. What breaks: at 1300 relays the indoor ones reach the outdoor ones near the margin, 81 % of their failed hand-overs were taken and the ACK lost, the split copies of 3.8 % of the texts spend 73 % of the frames, texts to those relays' people arrive 91 % against 95.5 %; and among many relays the owner rule against a full table: a relay that does not keep the relay within 50 m claims its texts and queues them for a beat that never comes, 0.9 % of the texts to relays at 5317 relays, 2.4 % at 20 269. The candidate: 5317 relays 97.06 → 97.43 % and 97.14 → 97.50 % (seeds 1, 2), claimed 41 → 5 and 58 → 10, at three times 96.86 → 97.11 %; 20 269 relays 97.81 → 99.59 %, claimed 232 → 16, the airtime per text unchanged, at three times 97.54 → 99.41 %; S4 94.04 → 94.20 % and 94.01 → 93.89 %, at three times 73.37 → 73.40 %. *Measured and reverted:* (1a) one power for every frame, stepped 2 dB a rotation by the relays heard with margin, at 5317 relays and targets 16 and 32: 95.65 and 96.00 %, 9.80 and 6.20 s a text, the median hop count 8 → 35 and 24, the relays on roofs and windowsills at −9 dBm, 15 % of DATA between relays to one that cannot hear it with margin; it settles there (0.13 steps a relay an hour) but not at 20 269 relays: 5.2 steps a relay an hour, the mean power sliding 1.8 → 0.3 dBm over the settled half hour, 28 % of DATA one-way, 91 relays no other keeps with margin, 97.10 %, 11.8 s a text. (1b) the same with the sender's power in a tenth heartbeat byte, the heartbeat having no spare bits since the short position, 11.84 → 13.12 ms: one-way DATA 2 %, 96.82 and 96.70 %, 4.17 and 2.28 s a text; at 20 269 relays the mean power settles near 5 dBm with most relays stepping every rotation or two, 98.79 % at 5.68 s a text. (2) Standing down, where it settles: at 5317 relays and 8 relays heard 1581 sleep, 97.09 %, heartbeats −29 %, data +18 %, all airtime +4 %; at 20 269 and 16, 10 431 sleep, 96.95 %, heartbeats −51 %, data +30 %. (3) A heartbeat interval growing with the relays heard, not built: heartbeats bind nothing measured, and the fixed interval by ID is what a sleeping node wakes by (D47). *Moving relays,* 1004 of the 5317 walking: keeping the first fix (D63) texts to them 58.1 %, to their sleepy nodes 59.9 %, all 91.12 %, 7.2 s a text; taking each fix 79.4, 64.8 and 92.94 %; the walkers' nodes sleepy instead 89.9 % to them, 96.55 % all, 1.84 s; seed 2 alike. Proposed (spec "Hardware"): a node on USB power is a relay only within 50 m of its first fix as one, and sleeps once it is farther. | The design binds on a relay's duty cycle where routes meet; every relay among the people is another budget there and a shorter link to someone, and the channels were not the limit at S4's density and are not at 75 times it. Lowering power wins only where the channel binds (D40), and adds a flaw of its own: a relay's power changes what others hear, not what it hears, so the relays that hear most, on roofs and windowsills, turn down and stay down, and they were the long hops; among 20 000 relays the loop through the neighbours does not settle. The power byte makes the count answer to the relay's own power and takes the one-way links out, but cannot give back the long hops; what it gains at 20 269 relays is mostly the near relays that a smaller reach lets into the tables, which the candidate gives at full power. Standing down saves heartbeats that bind nothing and changes the address of each node that switches. The owner rule reads from the table that no relay within 50 m is closer, which held at 9.6 relays per km² (0.08 within 50 m) and fails at 714 (5.6) when the table turns them away. A relay's position is its neighbours' way to it and its sleepy nodes' address, so one that moves strands both; a sleepy node's address is its anchor's, which stays (D46). The switching itself, its address change and the mail it holds, is the runtime switch of a node's role, not measured here. *Measured 2026-10-20; decided 2026-10-21: no rule for density, full power kept; the hop-cap fix adopted; the candidate adopted, and kept when the table filled by sector measured no better (D69); the proposal that a node that moves sleeps is not adopted: a node that moves is to route, take each fix and hold no mail, said by a heartbeat flag (D70), with its sender told its new address at the app layer.* |
| D69 | **The neighbour table filled by sector: measured, not adopted** (spec "Neighbour table"; the simulator's `--tables`; `docs/neighbour-table-2026-10-21.md`). Asked: whether the table should hold relays by value instead of by arrival (D12, O4). *Built and reverted:* the 64 places are 8 sectors of 45° around the node, by the bearing to each neighbour's heartbeat position, 8 places each; in a sector every relay within 50 m, then the closest until 4 are held so, then the farthest; relays with margin first, then those without, then entries demoted or not heard for 195 s, then sleepy nodes with an anchor; a newcomer to a full sector takes the place of the entry it outranks, or is turned away; a neighbour that moves, and the table when the node moves, are ranked again. It replaces D12's eviction and D68's place for a relay within 50 m. 8 sectors: the best entry toward a destination is within 22.5° of its bearing, cos 22.5° = 92 % of the progress, 87 % with 6; 4 closest: at 714 relays per km² they hold every relay within 120 m (714 × π r² / 8 = 4), at 9.6 per km² within 1.0 km; an entry is 64 bytes, the table 4 KB. *Measured,* delivered of the texts sent in the settled window, seeds 1 / 2, main (D12 with D68's place) against sectors: S4 94.20 / 93.89 against 93.97 / 94.10 %; 1300 relays 94.95 / 95.45 against 94.75 / 95.25 %; 5317 relays 97.43 / 97.50 against 97.39 / 97.53 %; 20 269 relays 99.59 / 99.52 against 99.66 / 99.51 %; three times S4's rate 73.40 / 70.64 against 67.47 / 61.32 %, the median hop count 6 → 7, p90 12 / 13 → 15 / 18, 2.51 / 2.73 → 3.01 / 3.47 s of airtime per delivered text. Among dense relays failed hand-overs 22 → 25 / 26 % (5317) and 24 → 29 % (20 269), airtime per text 1.82 / 1.76 → 1.89 / 1.92 s and 1.75 → 2.10 s, latency p50 2.1 → 3.5 / 3.7 s and 4.1 → 4.8 / 5.0 s; median hop count 8 → 7 and 9 → 8. Texts claimed by the wrong owner 5 / 10 → 8 / 4 (5317) and 16 / 22 → 8 / 16 (20 269). Fill to 2 and to 6 within the spread of fill to 4 everywhere; at three times S4's rate fill to 0 and to 8, far places only for relays with margin, one sector of 64, the sector cap with D12's admission, and sleepy nodes kept until stale all lose alike, 60 to 70 %, and the rewritten table with D12's admission repeats main to the byte. Anchors: the anchor is the strongest relay heard 50.3 / 49.9 % against 49.6 / 49.7 % at 5317 relays, anchor changes 0.557 / 0.541 against 0.569 / 0.546 a node an hour, current 0.536 / 0.531 against 0.536 / 0.533 mA; no fix needed. 1300 relays: 501 / 531 against 501 / 543 relays average fewer than 4 relays with margin, none an empty table; texts to them 97.9 / 97.5 against 96.0 / 95.5 %. *Measured and reverted:* (A) a relay heard once ranks with margin only a fade, 4 dB, above it: failed hand-overs 22.0 / 22.7 % and 24.0 / 24.8 %, airtime per text 1.74 / 1.74 s and 1.66 / 1.68 s, latency as main, delivered 97.31 / 97.50 % and 99.69 / 99.74 %; at three times S4's rate 67.98 / 60.78 %. | At the spec rate the table does not bind: the duty budget where routes meet and marginal links do, and D68's place already gives the owner rule the relay next door. Among dense relays the far places go to the farthest relays whose single heartbeat cleared the margin, mostly heard at the top of a fade; they fail, make way for the next such newcomer (placements 3.4 and 1.7 times main's) and cost airtime; candidate A stops it. Under load the arrival-order table does better in every form tried, the sector cap alone and the ranking alone as much as both; why is not shown, and a spread table must win there before it is taken. *Measured 2026-10-21; decided 2026-10-21: not adopted; D12 with D68's place for a relay within 50 m stays, and the spec says why: the table is a sample of the relays heard, weighted toward the strong links, with the relay within 50 m guaranteed; the simulator's `--tables` kept; O4 resolved.* |
| D70 | **A node that moves is carried: it routes, takes each fix, holds no mail, and says so in its heartbeat** (spec "Position", "Heartbeat", "Neighbour table", "Owner", "Sleepy nodes", "Relay choice", "Delivery", "Delivery queue", "Packet formats", proposal; `relay/motion.rs`, `relay/delivery.rs`, `wire.rs`, `tests/carried.rs`; `core-interface.md` §4, §6, §7; the simulator's `[gps] relays_every_s`, `error_m`, `error_correlation_s`, `[protocol] fixed_after_ms` and `research.carried`; `docs/carried-2026-10-21.md`). *Asked* by the user after D68: a node that moves is to route, take each fix and hold no mail, say so with a heartbeat flag that publishes a property of the node, flush its delivery queue at its first far fix, and need a long time still before it holds mail again; mail for a node heard as a relay goes to it. *Built:* the heartbeat's type takes 0x8 to 0xF, bit 2 carried, valid with any of the others (0xA and 0xE rejected), and the bench frames move from 0xF to 0x6. `AppCommand::Fix` hands the core each fix that passed the driver's checks, `SetPosition` is the map point. A fixed node keeps its first fix (D63, now in the core); a fix farther than the 50 m allowance is a far fix; the first of a run flushes the delivery queue, each text once with the long preamble, one attempt, ahead of the queue through the bucket; 72 s of far fixes with none near, a walker at 1.4 m/s crossing the allowance's 100 m in whole fixes of the L76K's one a second, make it carried: it takes each fix, flags its heartbeats, owns nothing by position, answers no anchorless beat, and keeps no neighbour from owning; four hours within 50 m of one place make it fixed again. A sleepy node skips carried relays and leaves an anchor that turns carried, for another or into anchorless mode. A holder that hears a node it holds texts for as a relay hands them to it with the ordinary preamble, done on the ACK (D66 left them waiting). *Measured* against main `c4b83cd`, `dense-movers.toml` (5317 relays, 1004 walking), delivered of the texts sent in the settled hour, seeds 1 / 2: all 91.46 / 91.94 → 96.87 / 97.03 %; to the walking relays 58.5 / 57.1 → 99.35 / 99.55 %; to sleepy nodes whose anchor walked 60.7 / 62.1 % (1280 / 1254 texts) → 91.6 / 90.5 % (680 / 691, since no sleepy node anchors to a walker any more); airtime per delivered text 6.89 / 7.23 → 3.15 / 3.07 s; latency p50 8.0 → 5.1 s, p90 45 → 29 s. Run of far fixes to carried p50 77 s, p90 82 s at a fix a heartbeat. Texts held at a first far fix: 21 / 16 an hour, 2 / 1 by the flush, 1 / 0 at a beat, the rest never; flush 23 frames, 9.7 s an hour over the domain. To a node as a relay 170 / 192 texts an hour, 14 s. 1 h against 4 h within 0.1 point in the hour; over four settled hours (seed 1) 97.90 % both, main 90.47 % (walkers 35.1 %, 11.4 s a text); at 1 h 1158 turns of walkers to fixed and 30 of 36 texts they held when they walked on lost, at 4 h 12 and none. A still relay gets no fix in the simulator, so none turns carried; with an assumed wandering GPS (15 m an axis, 300 s, a fix a second) 193 / 194 of 4313 still relays turned carried in the run, 533 over five hours, nearly all in the first half hour; their texts arrive (98.9 / 96.2 %), their sleepy nodes' texts sent while they were carried 0 / 11 and 2 / 18 (4 h over four hours 5 / 38, 1 h 2 / 46); all delivered unchanged; flush 199 / 229 frames, 84 / 97 s an hour. S4 seed 1 repeats main to the byte; `dense.toml` seed 1 differs only by the hand-over to a relay heard (6 texts; 97.43 → 97.31 % all, within its seeds), and with it off repeats main to the byte. | A relay's position is its neighbours' way to it and its sleepy nodes' address; carried, it keeps the first true by taking each fix and gives up the second, so nothing addressed to it goes stale but its own texts, which the app must re-address. The flush saves little because a walker holds little when it leaves, mostly texts for nodes it could not reach anyway; it is bounded and cheap. One hour lets walkers that stop for an hour take mail and strand it; four costs nothing measured, since a relay carried by mistake loses its sleepy nodes at once either way. The 72 s run cannot be judged against real drift: the error model is assumed, and a slowly drifting spoofed fix now makes a relay carried where D63 kept it still. *Measured 2026-10-21; decided 2026-10-21: adopted, fixed again after four hours; the boards' GPS wander indoors is a Phase 2 bench item (spec O16), and a far fix beyond twice the allowance is added to the carried run only if that measurement shows a still node reaching 72 s of far fixes.* *Amended by D136: a far fix is one beyond 250 m, and the run counts only time with fixes.* |

`relay/power.rs`, `relay/motion.rs`, `tests/power.rs`, `bark-driver`, `bark-fw/src/power.rs`; `core-interface.md` §1, §2, §4, §5, §6; the simulator's `[power]`, `[protocol] grace_ms`, `research.power`, `scenarios/dense-power.toml`; to the 7223 / 7201 switching nodes 94.57 / 94.97, 98.91 / 99.22 and 97.67 / 98.04 %; to sleepy nodes whose anchor switches 94.71 / 94.45, 96.01 / 95.92 and 93.96 / 93.71 %; to nodes that neither switch nor anchor to one 98.35 / 98.49, 98.25 / 98.52 and 98.23 / 98.56 %; airtime per delivered text 3.13 / 3.09, 4.45 / 4.55 and 3.99 / 3.84 s; latency p50 5.1, 9.3 / 9.6 and 8.7 / 8.8 s. Of 4707 / 4605 unplugs after the settle time, USB back within the grace 4335 / 4177 (1 h) and 2489 / 2391 (10 min); turns to sleepy 67 / 66 and 2214 / 2179; sleepy nodes moved off an anchor that turned sleepy 23 / 34 and 575 / 631; texts held at a grace's end 3 / 6 and 36 / 38, of them by the flush 0 / 1 and 2 / 6, the rest dropped; heartbeats for a change of flag 2024 / 1990 frames, 24.7 / 23.9 s an hour, and 6817 / 6600, 95 / 92 s. Only still nodes switching (seed 1): 96.97 % (1 h), 96.75 % (10 min), 2.74 and 2.89 s a text against main's 3.13 s. Over four settled hours (seed 1): main 97.90 %, 1 h 99.12 %, 10 min 98.51 %, airtime per text 3.00, 4.49 and 3.87 s, unplugs back within the grace 17 701 and 9709 of 18 544, turns to sleepy 562 and 8868. S4 and `dense.toml` seed 1 repeat main to the byte at every simulator commit and at the tip. *Measured and taken out:* the heartbeat for a position a fix changes, once an interval: 219 117 / 208 695 frames and 2594 / 2471 s of airtime in the settled hour at 1 h, delivered 98.18 / 98.31 % against 98.09 / 98.31 % without. *Measured and not built:* a node keeping its carried state while sleepy, fixed again only after four hours still: 97.50 / 97.65 % against 97.45 / 97.70 % at 10 min, 98.03 % against 98.09 % at 1 h. | A relay is where its sleepy nodes' mail waits and their addresses point, so each turn of one to sleepy moves its sleepy nodes and strands what was sent to them meanwhile; the grace decides how many unplugs become a turn. People unplug often and briefly: an hour keeps 92 % of the unplugs from changing anything, ten minutes 53 %, and the difference is the 2 points in texts to the sleepy nodes of a switching anchor and most of the 0.6 in all. The hour costs a node at most 0.68 % of its battery an unplug and nothing on the air; what costs airtime here is the walkers, relays through a walk at an hour's grace, carried and routed through as D70 makes them, while still nodes switching lower the airtime per text. The mail held at a turn is small, as at D70's far fix: a relay that leaves holds little. A heartbeat for a flag is a few frames an hour and gives an old anchor the node's mail within seconds; one for each change of a carried node's position doubled the walkers' heartbeats for nothing measured, since the slots carry it. How a solar relay, whose battery a panel charges by day, reports its power is open (O17). *Measured 2026-10-21; decided 2026-10-21: adopted, the grace one hour; the heartbeat for a position a fix changes not adopted; the carried state across a sleepy spell not built then, built by D72; O17 and the firmware's power path on hardware are Phase 2 bench items.* *Amended by D81: a fixed relay on battery relays until its battery is low.* |

| D77 | **A format version byte in every packet, and a heartbeat flags byte** (spec "Packet formats", "Heartbeat", "Neighbour table", "Hand-over", "The ACK", "Message records", "Bounds", "Node identity", "Capacity and sizing", "What the protocol omits", "Implementation notes"; `wire.rs`, `tables/neighbours.rs`, `tables/records.rs`, `node/relay/motion.rs`, `store.rs`, the simulator's trace, `bark-fw/src/console.rs`, the simulator's scripted test signals; `core-interface.md` §6, §7). Each field has one place. The first byte of every packet is its kind and that kind's one small field: a heartbeat's `1000 cccc` with its home channel, DATA's `00aa aaaa` and the ACK's `01aa aaaa` with `aux` as in D76; a heartbeat with any of bits 4 to 6 set is rejected, and the first-byte codes 0x8 to 0xF go. The second byte is the format version, and this spec is format 1. In a heartbeat it is the newest format the sender speaks; in DATA and the ACK the format of the frame. A DATA goes to a neighbour in the newest format both speak, the lower of the two, or in the format of the DATA a node known only from it sent; the ACK answers in the DATA's format; a DATA or ACK in a format the node does not speak is rejected. Two promises follow, for every later format: a heartbeat's first 13 bytes are fixed, and a node reads them in every heartbeat whatever its version byte says and ignores anything a later format appends after them (a heartbeat shorter than 13 bytes is rejected); and a node speaks every format from 1 to its newest. Each neighbour's format is kept in its table entry and shown by the firmware console and the simulator's trace; no rule uses it while format 1 is the only one. A heartbeat's third byte is its flags: bit 0 sleepy, bit 1 anchorless, bit 2 carried, bit 3 map point, bits 4 to 7 zero and ignored by a receiver, so a later format can add to the heartbeat without a new layout. The map point says that the sender's position is a map point a person set in the app, not a GPS fix; the node sets it while its position is the map point, keeps it in the stored record's flags across a reboot, and clears it when it takes a fix as its position (since D84, when the app clears it: no fix is read while it is set); each neighbour's is kept and shown, and no rule uses it yet. The bench frames keep the first byte 0x8F, carry the version, and put their kind in the flags byte's place. DATA and the ACK have no flags byte. Lengths: the heartbeat 11 → 13 bytes, the ACK 17 → 18, the DATA header 28 → 29, the largest payload 227 → 226. Airtime: the heartbeat and the ACK fill the same 6 and 8 blocks of symbols as before, 14.40 and 16.96 ms (compile-time asserts in `wire.rs`), so nothing derived from them moves: the ban, the reserves, the burst gap, the beat window. 13 bytes fill 6 blocks exactly, so the first byte a later format appends to the heartbeat costs a block, 1.28 ms. In DATA the 29th header byte adds a block, 1.28 ms, for two payload lengths in five, 91 of the 227, those whose frame is 1 or 4 bytes mod 5: 0.51 ms on average (a test in `wire.rs` counts it). The 100-byte text of "Capacity and sizing" is one of them: 128 → 129 bytes, 73.3 → 74.6 ms, 1.7 % of its frame, 90.2 → 91.5 ms a hop with its ACK, 0.72 → 0.73 s over 8 hops, about 74 → 73 texts/s; the ID sizes of "Node identity" 85.1 / 90.2 / 96.6 → 86.4 / 91.5 / 96.6 ms a hop. The empty DATA grows a block too, 22.08 → 23.36 ms, so the records fall 1613 → 1571 (1320 → 1278 + 293). The neighbour entry stays 72 B; `Relay` 172 440 → 170 760 B, `SleepyNode` 151 744 B. | Nodes are planted by anyone and updated only by hand, so the wire format must be able to change without replacing every node; the user chose an explicit version byte over reserved codes. A heartbeat's capability tells each node, at no traffic, which formats its neighbours speak. The flags byte gives later formats room in the heartbeat for nothing: 13 bytes take the airtime of 11 at SF5. Reading only the first 13 bytes lets a later format add to what a heartbeat says while an old node still sees the neighbour. The map point: every node is assumed honest, since the design breaks otherwise (D78), so a map point is a position a person vouched for, where a fixed relay's first GPS fix could have been spoofed; which checks use that trust is a later decision, so today the bit is carried, recorded and shown, nothing more. *Measured* against main `2ee8b2d`, release builds, delivered of the texts sent in the settled hour, airtime per delivered text the settled airtime over them, latency p50 / p90 of all texts. S4 at the design rate, seed 1: 94.08 → 93.89 % (16 425 → 16 389 texts), 2.13 → 2.10 s, 1.6 / 187 → 1.7 / 188 s; the build with the flags byte repeats the build with the version byte alone exactly. S4 at three times the rate, seeds 1 / 2 / 3: 63.35 / 62.05 / 57.89 → 56.34 / 59.62 / 63.79 %, mean 61.10 → 59.92 %, within the spread between seeds (D76: 57 to 75 %); airtime per text 3.82 / 3.98 / 4.76 → 4.67 / 4.30 / 4.16 s; latency 120 / 613, 140 / 643, 157 / 676 → 150 / 666, 149 / 655, 143 / 639 s. `dense-power.toml`, seeds 2 / 3: 98.17 / 97.92 → 98.29 / 98.00 %, 9.11 / 8.64 → 9.62 / 8.94 s, 10.0 / 43, 9.5 / 44 → 10.3 / 42, 9.8 / 45 s. **Seed 1 falls into a forwarding storm on the branch and not on main:** 97.97 → 90.95 %, 9.26 → 54.26 s a text, latency 10.2 / 45 → 17.6 / 251 s; the run's airtime 272 306 → 1 006 409 s, all of the rise in DATA (×5.2) and ACK (×4.7), heartbeats unchanged; frames to sleepy nodes ×9, drops at the hop cap 4077 → 20 381, copies expired 514 → 57 757, texts given back at their origin 56 → 1578, relays ever at their cap 32 → 1354. Two probes on seed 1: the branch with the records held at 1613 repeats the storm byte for byte, and main with the records at 1571 repeats main byte for byte, so the records bound never binds there and the storm follows the frame lengths: the longer DATA, or this seed's sensitivity to any shift of timing. Seeds 4 to 7 do not storm on either build: 98.3 / 98.2 / 98.2 / 98.2 → 98.1 / 98.2 / 98.3 / 98.2 %, the branch's airtime 3 to 5 % above main's, as the longer DATA predicts. So dense-power has shown one storm in seven seeds on the branch and none in seven on main, and the branch is not what makes the scenario fragile but what revealed it. What starts the storm and why it sustains itself, nine times the frames to sleepy nodes and five times the drops at the hop cap, is not known: a later item, to be studied on the seed 1 run before dense-power is trusted as a stable scenario. The release timing test runs in 13.4 s against its 20 s bound at a load average of 8. *Measured 2026-10-25; merge left to the coach.* |
| D78 | **No authentication, and the threat model said plainly** (spec "What the protocol omits", "Phone link"; `README.md`). The network is open and nothing on the air is authenticated. An outside review noted that one radio in range can forge a refusing ACK and ban any relay for up to 100 s, forge a taking ACK and lose a message, forge a heartbeat and send an anchor's sleepy nodes elsewhere, or claim the position of a destination and own it, all within the duty cycle and unnoticed. The spec now says so: any node in radio range, by a bug or on purpose, can forge any frame and disrupt its district; the design resists collisions and overload, not adversaries; anyone in radio range learns every node's ID and position, and nobody farther away does. The review also found "Phone link" wrong in saying that only the recipient learns where the sender is, since heartbeats carry ID and position in clear; it now says that the relays carrying a message do not learn who sent it. | A real node of the network with a few judicious bugs, by accident or intent, breaks large parts of the network whether or not frames are signed, since it is a legitimate participant. Signing every frame would also need the hardware locked so that a holder cannot extract the key, which is unrealistic for an open device anyone can buy and build. And no radio network withstands a serious adversary, who can in any case jam. Location privacy is impossible by design: a node tells its position every rotation on a channel anyone can hear, and a rotated ID is linked by anyone who hears the old one vanish and the new one appear in the same place. *Decided 2026-10-25.* |
| D79 | **A loader of our own at `0x26000`, behind the board's bootloader, swaps a staged image in and swaps it back if it does not confirm itself; the flash layout is fixed with it** (`bark-loader`, `bark-image`, `bark-fw/src/update.rs`; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Flash layout" and "Updates"; `docs/mesh-update-2026-10-25.md`, "The bootloader: the recommendation" and "Flash layout"). The board's UF2 bootloader stays untouched as the USB recovery path: D6 stands. Where it jumps sits a loader built on embassy-boot 0.7.0. At each boot on which the firmware has asked for a swap, the loader checks the staged image against the length and SHA-256 the firmware recorded, refusing it on a mismatch, then lets embassy-boot swap the two slots a page at a time, which a power cut cannot break, and swap them back at the next boot if the new image did not mark itself booted; on those boots the watchdog runs, 600 s. Before it jumps it write-protects the MBR, the SoftDevice, itself and the bootloader until the next reset (ACL). The firmware confirms itself once its core is set up and its radio is up, and feeds the watchdog from then on. The layout: the loader 4 pages, its state 1, the running image 88 from `0x2B000`, the staged image 89, the staged image's record 1, coded pieces for the mesh 22, D73's page still at `0xF3000`; one table writes both linker scripts. One UF2 carries the loader and the firmware, so bring-up does not change; the firmware alone goes to a board that has the loader. The image's 256-byte header (magic, format, version, length, SHA-256, room for three signatures) is unsigned until the key policy, and staging is a console command, a bench tool until a transport exists. | From the note. The UF2 path stays, so every board can be recovered by a double tap. The signature will be checked by the running firmware before it stages, never by the loader, so no key sits in the one part that is never updated, where it could never be replaced or withdrawn; the loader checks the hash only, against flash that went bad. A second stage in application flash rather than a replaced bootloader: replacing the bootloader, with MCUboot or with a rebuilt UF2 bootloader that verifies, gains nothing for the mesh, whose images come through the firmware anyway; it risks a board that only a probe on its SWD pads can recover, and it either loses the UF2 path or keeps it open, which defeats the signature. A loader in application flash gives the same swap and swap back and can itself be replaced by a UF2 at any visit. embassy-boot rather than MCUboot chained the same way: 8748 B as built against the 48 KiB partition Nordic's SDK gives MCUboot on this chip, and built in Rust beside the firmware. *Measured on the host*: the swap, the swap back and the refusals on a flash in RAM with the board's layout, and the power cut at every flash operation of a staging, a swap (529 operations), a swap back and a confirmation, after each of which one image is whole (`bark-image/src/boot/tests.rs`). The update module costs the node image 3648 B: 171 644 B against 167 996 B. *Built 2026-10-25; bench on the boards.* *2026-11-06 (D139):* the staged slot moves to the external flash, where the swap copies each running page out and the staged one in, so the previous image waits there for the swap back; the loader powers the chip only on the boots with a swap to do, and a chip that does not answer cancels a swap not yet started. |
| D80 | **A sleepy node runs its GPS only when the radio says it may have moved: when it turns sleepy, takes or changes an anchor, turns anchorless, and once a delivery TTL while anchorless; a search lasts at most 60 s, and one that finds nothing holds every search back for 8 h; the fix at a new anchor is where the node chose from** (spec "Beats", "Delivery", "Position"; `bark-proto/src/gps.rs`, `relay/gps.rs`, `Action::Locate`, `tests/gps.rs`; `core-interface.md` §4, §5; the simulator's `gps.rs` and `[gps] no_sky`; `docs/gps-schedule-2026-10-25.md`). Spec "Beats" had a sleepy node refresh its fix before each beat, a search of up to 10 minutes; the simulator counted no GPS. The L76K draws 29 mA searching and 20 µA in standby, starts hot in 2 s with orbits under four hours old and cold in 30 s, and acquires only 18 dB below the open sky (L76K Hardware Design V1.0, Table 2), so indoors a search finds nothing and a search a beat keeps it on. What a sleepy node uses its own position for: its address while anchored is its anchor's, so no fix serves it; the fix serves the anchorless address, the rebuilding of short positions after a trip of more than 12.5 km, the move check, the carried flag (which nobody reads while the node sleeps) and the place kept across a reboot. *Measured* (S4 seed 1, settled hour, no indoor node under the sky; before the airtime correction): spec as written 28.8 mA a sleepy node, 1.2 days on 850 mAh, delivered 93.74 % (main 94.10 %), to walkers 89.22 %; events with 8 h 0.632 mA, 56.0 days, 93.56 %, to walkers 87.18 %; with 1 h 0.683 mA, 51.8 days, 93.66 %; no search while anchored (the user's hypothesis) 0.632 mA, 93.50 %, to walkers 87.40 %; a search each beat while the fixes move 87.3 to 88.0 % to walkers at 0.63 to 0.78 mA. With half the indoor nodes under the sky: as written 14.8 mA, 2.4 days; events 8 h 0.635 mA, 93.45 %. `dense-movers.toml` seed 1: as written 28.7 mA, 96.85 %, to walkers 91.55 %; events 8 h 0.535 mA, 66.2 days, 96.47 %, 89.91 %. Sleepy walkers turned carried 2561 as written, 315 with events, none without searches while anchored; walking relays' fixes are unchanged. *On the tip, after the airtime correction:* S4 seed 1 delivered 93.49 % against 94.08 % on main `3c33c80`, which handed every walker a fix at every beat and counted no GPS (to walkers 87.18 against 89.61 %, to the others 95.12 against 95.24 %); seeds 2 and 3 93.81 and 93.27 %; a sleepy node 0.636 mA, the radio 0.613 and the GPS 0.023, 55.7 days on 850 mAh. `dense-movers.toml` seed 1 96.48 %, 0.536 mA, 66.1 days. | A fix each beat buys the move check, two points of the texts to walkers and four to six to a walker while it walks, for 28 mA; following a node that moves does not buy it back, since it starts at the first anchor change of a walk. Events and no search while anchored deliver alike; events cost the same where the sky is not seen, since the first failure holds the rest back, and where it is they take a fix at each anchor change, which keeps the rebuilt positions right on a long trip and the stored place fresh. 8 h: a failed search's 0.48 mAh once in 8 h is 0.060 mA, a tenth of a sleepy node's 0.63 mA, for a node that never sees the sky; 1 h would cost such a node up to 0.48 mA, 31 days. Indoor reception and the hot start's backup power are bench items. *Adopted 2026-10-25.* Rederived at 0.56 mA by D112: 9 h. |
| D81 | **A fixed relay on battery relays until its battery is low, 3.741 V, and relays again at 3.775 V or on external power; a carried relay keeps D71's grace; a fixed relay rebooted on battery relays on; the driver reads the battery once a minute on battery, between radio operations, and the core calibrates the readings at a rested full charge; the flush before a turn to sleepy lasts a delivery TTL at most** (spec "Nodes", "Sleepy nodes", "Implementation notes", O17; `relay/power.rs`, `node.rs` `BatteryLevels` and the calibration constants, `store.rs` flags 0x8 and 0x10, `AppCommand::Battery`, `Trace::BatteryLow`, `tests/battery.rs`; `bark-driver` `App::battery`, `BATTERY_EVERY`; `bark-fw/src/battery.rs`; `core-interface.md` §4, §5; the simulator's `[battery]`, `battery.rs`, `--battery`, `scenarios/grid-down.toml`; `docs/battery-role-2026-10-25.md`). Under D71 every relay turned sleepy an hour after its power went, so in a grid outage, which takes every relay of a district in the same second, all turned together. A typical Li-ion cell's open-circuit voltage is 3.741 V at 20 %, 3.775 V at 30 % and 4.177 V full, each read after an hour's rest (Maxim AN4189, Table 1); the T-Echo's cell is 850 mAh and its charger 500 mA (LILYGO T-Echo README); the SAADC at gain 1/6 has a gain error of up to ±3 % (nRF52840 Product Specification, SAADC), which at 3.74 V spans 4 to 57 % of charge; the board reads the cell on AIN2 behind a 2:1 divider, 1.76 mV a step at the cell. A relay draws 6.4 mA on battery (S4's settled hour: 5.98 to 7.40 mA), so 80 % lasts 106 h and the rest keeps it asleep 11 days; the L76K tracking (29 mA) would make that about 16 h. Calibrated at a full charge, a board turns between 14 and 32 % of charge, the charger's tolerance on its end voltage, about 1 %, assumed; before its first calibration, between 4 and 57 %. *Measured* (`grid-down.toml`, S4 with batteries and the grid down from 3600 s, seed 1; D71 against this rule, texts delivered within an hour): (a) four hours off: D71 94.61 % in the grace hour, then 7.35 % with the 269 relays sleepy and 16 136 sleepy nodes anchorless, the domain's airtime ninety-fold, 67.87 % in the hour after the power returns; this rule 96.24 % through the outage (seeds 2 / 3: 96.57 / 96.12 %) and 96.52 % after, no turn. (b) 24 hours off: D71 7.60 % in hours 1 to 4 and 12.34 % in hours 4 to 7 (run stopped there); this rule 96.27 % all day, the relays at 79.9 % of their charge at its end. (c) four hours off and four on, three times: D71 collapses in each outage (7.35 %, 5.73 %); this rule 96.1 to 96.5 % in every phase. (d) (b) with each relay's GPS tracking: the relays turn between 13.1 and 20.5 h, half by 17.0 h (seeds 2 / 3: 17.2 / 17.2 h); 96.26 % while all 269 relay, 93.61 % from 269 to 204, 64.34 % from 204 to 90, 12.09 % from 90 to 25, 3.81 % with none, as under D71: spreading the turns does not keep a domain up, the relays' lasting does. The people's nodes used 0.37 points of their charge in (a)'s outage under this rule against 1.75 under D71, whose anchorless nodes listen and send long frames; none ran flat. `dense-power.toml` with batteries, no outage: 97.96 % under both, turns to sleepy 9 against 67. In (d) as first run three relays ran flat flushing for hours through a full duty budget, hence the flush's bound; with it none did, 69.61 % over the outage against 70.26 %. S4 seed 1 repeats main to the byte with batteries off. *On the merged tip*, after main's version byte, loader and GPS schedule, with the core's calibration and the SAADC's 3 % in grid-down: S4 seed 1 repeats main `da979a9` to the byte (JSON summary, messages CSV and printed table but the wall time; settled 93.37 %); (a) seed 1 95.70 % through the outage and 96.03 % in the hour after, 95.03 % before it, 269 relays throughout, no turn, the people's nodes 0.36 points of charge in the outage. *Later:* how a fixed relay on battery runs its GPS, which decides whether the rule buys four days or under one: resolved by D82, which rests it after the grace; a district with no relay left after a longer outage, whose anchorless nodes fill the channels. | A relay is where the routing and the sleepy nodes' mail live, and an outage takes all of a district's at once; relaying on, a fixed relay costs only its own cell, four days of it, and the owner keeps a fifth. A carried relay is its owner's and nobody's anchor, so it keeps the grace. The levels sit at 20 and 30 % of the curve, 34 mV apart, several times a reading's noise and the load's drop; the cell rises without external power only on a solar box, which so relays by day and sleeps when low (O17). Calibration in the core, not the driver, so that the simulator runs it too: the gain error alone spans half the cell. A minute between readings is under a tenth of a percent of the cell at the most a board draws. No spread of the grace: it would stretch the collapse, not avoid it. The flush's bound: a delivery TTL is what the network promises a node it cannot reach. *Adopted 2026-10-26.* |
| D82 | **A relay's GPS runs all the time on external power and for the grace after it is lost; after that, and whenever its position is a map point, it rests in standby; a resting relay searches at boot and when the radio says it may have moved, the squared surprises of its fixed relay neighbours' heartbeats over a worst-case rotation passing their two-sigma point; a search that finds a far fix keeps the GPS running until the node is carried or a fix is near; a relay's search has no back-off** (spec "Position", "Beats"; `bark-proto/src/node/relay/gps.rs`, `Node::gps_tracks`, `Trace::MoveSign`, `tests/relay_gps.rs`; `core-interface.md` §4, §5; the simulator's relay GPS accounting, walks of fixed relays and a relay's GPS drawn from its cell under `[battery]`; `docs/relay-gps-2026-10-26.md`). Under D81 a fixed relay on battery relays until 20 %, and the L76K's 29 mA (L76K Hardware Design V1.0, Table 2) beside the radio's 6.4 mA takes 680 mAh in 19 h instead of 106. After its first fix a fixed relay needs the GPS only to see that it is carried (D70), and a map point stops the fixes altogether. The surprise is a heartbeat's RSSI less the neighbour's averaged RSSI before it; its variance under σ = 4 dB fading (D38) is 4σ²/3, the frame's σ² and the average's σ²/3; the sum of k is chi-square with k degrees, its two-sigma point by the Wilson–Hilferty approximation; moving x relative to a neighbour r away changes its RSSI by 36 log10(1 + x/r) dB, plus shadowing and walls. At 2.3 % of 18.5 rotations an hour a relay that never sees the sky searches 0.42 times an hour, 0.20 mA, 3 % of its radio, so no back-off. *Measured*, the options as research switches (removed with the adoption): S4 seeds 1–3, delivery the same to the byte under every option, a still relay's GPS 29 → 0.020 mA, its mean squared surprise 0.30–0.32, the sign never fired. `dense-power.toml` (walkers on battery within their grace; seed 1 storms on main), seeds 2–6, delivered A (always) 98.20 / 97.93 / 98.18 / 98.08 / 98.08 %, D (rest from the first second on battery, radio sign) 98.02 / 97.71 / 97.82 / 97.86 / 97.98 % (−0.22), B (rest on battery, no sign) seeds 2–4 87.08 / 85.91 / 87.00 % with 47.6 / 44.8 / 46.2 % to walking relays and 69 s of airtime per delivered text; walks seen carried within the walk A 82–83 % at p50 117 s after setting off, D 59–61 % at 283 s, B 2 %; time walked while fixed A 37–39 h, D 82–87 h, B 191–193 h; E (this rule) seed 2 equal to A to the byte; the sign fired in 0.9–1.3 % of still relays' rotations and 98–99 % of walking ones'. `dense-movers.toml` seed 1 with every relay resting (the sign alone): A 96.42 %, D 95.78 %, B 92.13 %; a still relay's GPS 0.068 mA, 0.10 searches a relay-hour; the sign fired in 0.66 % of still rotations, 75 % of walking ones. `grid-down.toml` seed 1: four hours off, every option 95.70 % through the outage, the relays' charge at its end A 81.5 %, D 96.6 %, E 92.8 %; a day off, A turns every relay sleepy, the first after 8.9 h, delivers 70.19 % over the outage, 2–12 % in its last five hours and 51.46 % in the two after; D and E turn none, 95.76 % throughout, charge 79.9 / 76.1 %. The surprise against the neighbour's previous heartbeat instead of its average, whose variance 2σ² depends on nothing kept, measured no closer to its derivation (still relays 0.38 of it in `dense-movers.toml`, 0.39 in S4, against 0.38 and 0.32 for the average) and saw walkers later (70 against 75 % of walking rotations, p50 267 against 249 s, 29.8 against 27.7 h walked fixed); not adopted: the frames heard are those above sensitivity, which cuts the deep fades off either difference. | The hour on battery is when a person unplugs a node and walks off, and there the rule is today's: resting from the first second (D) costs 0.22 points and twice the time walking relays spend at the wrong place, for 29 mAh, 4 % of the cell, once an outage. Resting with no search (B) leaves a relay walked off fixed at its old place, owning it, holding its mail and anchoring its sleepy nodes: 11 points. After the hour a relay on battery is one left in an outage, and resting keeps the domain up for a day where always on loses it. The sign errs towards silence; a lower point, or ending a rotation as soon as its sum passes, would see walkers sooner, not measured. Hot starts after hours of rest and standby at 20 µA are bench items (the L76K's hot start). *Adopted 2026-10-27.* |
| D83 | **A node with no place takes its first accepted fix as its place, fixed, at once, and sends no heartbeat before it** (spec "Position"; `Config::placed`, `Node::placed`, `relay/motion.rs`, `tables/neighbours.rs` `rebuild`, `bark-fw/src/node.rs`, `tests/first_fix.rs`; `core-interface.md` §4; `docs/position-fixes-2026-10-27.md`). A node has a place when built with one, when it has one stored, or when the app sets a map point. One with none sends no heartbeat and stores nothing, since a heartbeat carries only the short position and the node can rebuild neither its neighbours' positions nor its own beyond the short form's period; its driver checks its first fix against no neighbour for the same reason. At its first fix it rebuilds the positions it heard near its place, and a relay heartbeats its first rotation's burst from then, as at boot (D73); a sleepy node may take an anchor before but beats only after. The simulator boots every node placed, so no scenario changes. | The first fix is the only position such a node has; measured from a position it never had, every rule that compares fixes reads it as a move. *Adopted 2026-10-30.* |
| D84 | **The app sets and clears the map point; no fix is taken while it is set, and the next accepted fix after it is cleared is the place** (spec "Position", "Packet formats"; `AppCommand::ClearMapPoint`, `relay/motion.rs`, `relay/gps.rs`; `core-interface.md` §4; `docs/position-fixes-2026-10-27.md`). Found by D82: spec "Packet formats" said a fix taken as the position clears the map point, the core interface that the driver hands no fix while one is set, so nothing could clear it. While it is set the core takes no fix, the driver hands none, a relay's GPS rests (D82) and a sleepy node starts no search; cleared, the node stays where it is, heartbeats at once, and its next accepted fix is its place, not a far fix. No scenario sets a map point. | A person set it, so a person ends it; a fix cannot, since none is read while it is set. *Adopted 2026-10-30.* |

Found by D82: plugged in, a sleepy node kept its last fix, which D80 may leave hours old, and on USB its GPS found out through the carried rule, so it was carried for four hours at the place it had reached. Nobody addresses a node at its own position while it is sleepy, but from its first heartbeat as a relay sleepy nodes may anchor to it and its messages carry its address, so the first fix must come before many hold the old place: on USB the GPS runs, a cold start under the sky takes 30 s (L76K Hardware Design V1.0, Table 2), and D80's search limit, 60 s, bounds it; a later fix is of a node that does not see the sky, as likely a stray as a move, and is checked as any other. One storm on each side in seven seeds reads as the scenario's, not the rule's; it stays unexplained. Indoor first fixes at plug-in are the bench's (O16). *Adopted 2026-10-30.* |
| D86 | **The busy estimate reads another channel four symbol periods after the receiver starts on it, the home channel as it listens, and one channel a second; its window is 30 minutes** (spec "Busy estimate", "Channel at boot", "Implementation notes"; `bark-proto/src/radio.rs` `RSSI_SETTLE`, `node.rs` `SWEEP_INTERVAL` and `BUSY_EMA_INVERSE`, `relay.rs`; `bark-driver` `RSSI_DWELL`; the simulator's radio costs, `phy.rs`; `core-interface.md` §3, §5; `docs/bench-2026-10-30.md`, `docs/busy-rate-2026-10-30.md`). A node alone on the bench moved its home channel 47 times in 10.5 hours, as often as the move rule allows: the driver read other channels 300 µs after the retune, at −91 dBm, and its home settled, at −79, so home was busy at every reading and nothing else ever was. The SX1262's reading climbs a step each symbol period after the receiver starts and a strong signal reads a fixed −55 dBm until about 1 ms; from four symbol periods, 1.02 ms, it reads the level, and the settled home reading matches it within 1 dB. A correct reading of another channel is then a retune (0.67 ms), four symbols, the read (0.16 ms) and a retune back, 2.5 ms, where the spec assumed 0.5 ms; each loses the packets whose preamble starts in a 1.5 ms window. Every channel each second, (a), deafens a relay 3.3 % of the time and loses 1.9 % of packet starts; one channel a second, (b), 0.25 % and 0.15 %, with the window stretched from 10 to 30 minutes, α 1/600 → 1/138, so each channel keeps 138 samples, ±2.6 points on a 10 % channel. Under (b) the choice at the end of the first rotation rests on 15 readings a channel, ±8 points: enough to keep off a busy channel, not to rank quiet ones, which the move rule does on its window. Neighbour expiry, which ran once a round of the channels, now runs at every reading, keeping its one-second step. The simulator first takes the bench's costs: a retune 668 µs whatever the hop (86 µs before), a CAD 0.75 + 4.64 + 1.30 ms from listening to listening at the 2 ms oscillator start (D87; 2.35 ms before), a transmit 2.05 ms before its first symbol and 1.30 ms back to listening (0.18 ms before); the three retune fields keep their names, their sum measured and their split not. | *Adopted 2026-10-30. S4 at three times the rate, seeds 1 / 2 / 3: main 62.0 % at seed 1; (a) 38.7 / 36.5 / 39.3 %, (b) 47.9 / 45.2 / 43.6 %; blocking bus seed 1 (a) 42.5 %, (b) 45.0 %. Of the fall from main at three times the rate, seed 1, the sweep's costs alone take main to 51.7 % under (a) and 55.7 % under (b); the rest is the listen-before-talk, a CAD and a reading with a return to listening between, 9.2 ms where main charged 2.8 ms, paid by every hand-over and answer: cheaper listen-before-talk is a later item. `dense-power.toml` seed 1: main 97.9 % with 295 000 s of airtime, no storm; (a) 78.9 %, (b) 80.9 %, airtime 2 030 000 s under both, a storm the measured costs set off whatever the rate, a later item; blocking bus (b) 84.5 % ((a) 78.6 %). The ring, seeds 1 to 40, sleepy nodes listening: (a) 0.964 to 0.986, (b) 0.958 to 0.982, the same 3200 texts on average; its bound 0.96 → 0.95. At the design rate the relays lost 125 000 receptions to retunes and readings in the run under (a), 23 000 under (b). (b) because it is as good at the design rate, better under load on both buses, and moves are meant to be rare. |

The SX1262 waits out a programmed start each time it powers the board's TCXO: after every CAD, which ends with it stopped, at each wake of duty-cycled receive, and at power-up. No source names the module's TCXO, and 5 ms was an assumption with margin. On the bench 1 ms worked on two boards at room temperature, CADs and transmissions alike; 2 ms keeps a factor two for a cold board or a slower part. | D88 | **The radio is driven with only the commands that change: a listen-before-talk is one excursion, the CAD with the RSSI read inside it, and the oscillator's start is probed** (spec "Heartbeat", "Busy estimate", "Hand-over", "The ACK", "Duty-cycled receive and the long preamble"; `core-interface.md` §3, §5; `bark-proto` `Action::Lbt`, `Event::LbtDone`; `bark-driver/src/sx1262.rs`, `bark-driver/src/tcxo.rs`; `bark-fw/src/radio.rs`, `console.rs`, the vendored `lora-phy`'s one-command methods; the simulator's `lbt_prepare_us` and `lbt_return_us`; `bark-fw/README.md` "Radio driver"; `docs/lean-driver-2026-10-30.md`). The driver re-sent every setting on every operation, drove the bus through the interrupt path, returned to listening between the CAD and the reading, and restarted the oscillator with a 2 ms wait after every CAD: a listen-before-talk cost 9.2 ms, paid by every heartbeat, hand-over and answer. The bench showed each unnecessary (`bench-2026-10-30.md`): the bus driven blocking takes a third off every command; modulation, packet parameters, the symbol timeout and the receiver gain never change between operations; the reading is right four symbol periods into the CAD; and the oscillator restarted within a millisecond of stopping needs no delay. So (1) the core asks for one excursion, `Lbt`, and gets the busy result and the reading in one event, the channel clear if neither says busy; the sweep's reading stays `Rssi`. (2) A sequencer in `bark-driver`, tested on the host against a chip that records its commands, keeps what the chip holds and sends only what changes: the configuration once with a union interrupt mask, a retune as standby, frequency and receive, a listen-before-talk as standby, frequency, CAD, the read inside, the result, frequency and receive; everything again after a reset, a sleep or an error; the firmware is the chip one command at a time over the bus driven blocking. (3) The reading inside a CAD, which the datasheet documents for receive only, is checked at boot against a reading while listening in five pairs on the home channel; the median offset, under 12 dB, twice the largest the bench saw, is taken off every reading inside a CAD, and the reading is taken after the CAD otherwise. (4) The oscillator's delay is two: 100 µs for the restart after a CAD, twice the 50 µs that never failed within a millisecond of stopping, since zero hangs the chip; and a probed one for cold starts, found at boot by a binary search over 0.25 to 4 ms in 0.25 ms steps, the bench's resolution and wider than the band in which the flag flickers, each probe a standby with the oscillator off 2 ms, the delay set, the errors cleared, a receive and the error word read, four confirmations at the step found, one step of margin: 0.5 ms on the bench's board. In use the flag is read after every cold start and the delay stepped up at once; a probe two steps below it, which lowers it by one if it passes, comes when the die's temperature has moved 3 °C since it settled and not within an hour, which recovers the margin as the board warms; the die sensor is uncalibrated and only its change is used, and no bench number sets the 3 °C, which only paces a recovery the step-up makes safe. The simulator charges a listen-before-talk 0.41 ms to prepare, the CAD's 2.18 ms and 0.51 ms back, and a reading of another channel 1.9 ms; the spec's cost tables follow. The CAD's parameters go before every CAD, not once: held once, the CAD flag alone found a beacon at 10 % duty 0.5 % of the time on the board, where the driver that sent them before each CAD found it 10 %; the length took, the thresholds did not. | *Adopted 2026-10-30, built and run on board 1* (`lean-driver-2026-10-30.md`): radio up in 73.5 ms, the delay 500 µs after 8 probes, the self-check's offset 1 dB over five pairs within −1 to 3; a retune 388 µs, a read 51, a listen-before-talk 2.9 ms home to home (prepare 359, CAD 2.0 to 2.3 ms, back 510), a transmit 547 to 794 µs to prepare and 163 over the airtime, 369 back; under a beacon the reading inside the CAD tracked the reading in receive within 2 dB. The CAD flag's regression above is fixed and awaits the beacon test again. *Measured* in the simulator against main `1bce3d6`, at the board's costs, delivered of the texts sent in the settled hour: S4 at the design rate, seed 1, main 93.5 %, D88 93.4 %, latency p50 1.79 → 1.64 s; S4 at three times the rate, seeds 1 / 2 / 3, main 44.5 / 45.3 / 43.7 %, D88 59.7 / 56.8 / 63.8 %, 15 points, airtime 188 000 / 187 000 / 211 000 → 169 000 / 174 000 / 181 000 s, p50 211 / 209 / 270 → 143 / 160 / 151 s, p90 783 / 771 / 850 → 654 / 685 / 645 s; `dense-power.toml` seed 1 still storms, less: main 81.9 % with 1 933 000 s of airtime, D88 90.3 % with 1 134 000 s, against the 295 000 s of a run without a storm (D86), p50 60.6 → 20.2 s. The note's first defaults, before the board ran the driver, gave the same within the seeds (58.0 / 57.8 / 57.1 %, `dense-power` 90.2 %). Its cause stays a later item. *The transmission costs the spec listed until 2026-11-05:* 0.71 ms to prepare and start it, 0.55 ms for its packet parameters, payload and start and 0.16 ms before the first symbol, its airtime, 0.37 ms back to listening. |
| D89 | **A copy from elsewhere is absorbed: a relay that has handled a message and holds no copy of it ACKs a copy from a node it did not hand the message to, and drops it; a return, from a node in the record's `handed` set or the neighbour being handed to, goes on with the relay's own search as before, and a copy for a destination the relay owns now is delivered** (spec "Receiving DATA" cases 5 and 6, "Message records", "Next hop", "Why the search finds a path"; `bark-proto/src/node/relay/forward.rs` `Case::Absorb`, `tables/records.rs` `handed_to` on a return as well as a forward, `event.rs` `DropReason::Absorbed` and `Trace::Copy::absorbed`; the simulator's `absorbed` counter; `bark-proto/tests/routing.rs`; `docs/storm-absorb-2026-10-31.md`). Part 1 of the storm note's candidate (`docs/storm-2026-10-30.md`): a hand-over that fails with the receiver holding the message leaves two searchers, and each copy that reached a relay that had handled the message reopened the search there, case 5 as it was, so every split started the depth-first search again from every relay it reached and each search split in its turn; in the storm's hour 3 060 986 copies reopened a search against 442 031 merged. Absorbed, a copy goes on only to relays that have not seen the message, and the chain ends where the search has been. The cost is in the search itself: its one token comes back to a crossed relay by a link that is not a return whenever the greedy way is a dead end, and before this rule that relay took the token on from where its own search stood; now the sender is done when the ACK comes and the relay does not go on, so the traversal no longer visits every reachable relay. Seven routing tests that described that mechanism are rewritten to the rule's behaviour. | *Candidate, measured 2026-10-31, the user decides.* *Measured* against main `bdfd090`, delivered of the texts sent in the settled hour, the run's airtime: `dense-power.toml` seed 1, the storming seed, 90.3 → 97.9 %, 1 134 454 → 202 693 s, p90 288 → 42 s; seed 7, 98.2 → 98.1 %, 276 300 → 200 781 s; S4 at three times the rate, seeds 1 / 2 / 3, 59.7 / 56.8 / 63.8 → 72.8 / 71.8 / 83.6 %, airtime 169 431 / 173 508 / 181 241 → 144 371 / 143 620 / 146 908 s, p50 143 / 160 / 151 → 61 / 67 / 43 s; S4 at the design rate 93.4 → 93.5 %; the ring seed 1 0.924 → 0.921, its test's bound 0.95 kept; `dense-movers.toml` 96.5 → 95.4 %. On the random mesh of 25 relays, `mesh-burst.toml` seed 1, 100 → 86 % of a burst, every loss an absorbed copy, and `mesh-spread.toml` seed 1 100 → 95.6 %: the scenario test `mesh_routes_bursts_and_a_restart` fails under the rule and was left failing, its bound not loosened. *Not adopted as stated 2026-10-31:* the pruning ends the storm, but a sender that is done when its copy is absorbed ends a single search where it crosses itself. The form to build next, D90: the relay answers "seen, not taken", and the sender marks it tried, bans nothing, and goes on to its next candidate as if the relay had returned the message at once, a change to spec "The ACK" and "Hand-over". |
| D90 | **A copy from elsewhere is answered seen, not taken: a relay that has a record of a message, holds no copy of it, and receives it from a node it did not hand the message to answers an ACK with a seen flag, bans nothing and takes on no work; the sender marks it tried and goes on to its next candidate at once, as if the relay had returned the message the moment it got it** (spec "Packet formats", "The ACK", "Hand-over", "Message records", "Next hop", "Receiving DATA" cases 3 to 6, "Return to parent", "Overload waits", "Hop count", "Why the search finds a path", "Bounds"; `wire.rs` the ACK's first byte `s 1 w w w w w w`, `relay/forward.rs` `Case::Seen`, `seen` and the shared `tried` step, `tables/records.rs` the last packet's answer and the parent in `handed` on a return, `event.rs` `Trace::Seen`, `Trace::Copy::seen`, `DropReason::ReturnSeen`; the simulator's seen counters and summary line; `bark-proto/tests/routing.rs`, `relay/tests.rs`; `docs/storm-seen-2026-10-31.md`). The form D89 said to build next: the same copies D89 absorbed, answered seen, stay with their sender, which has the one record that knows what it has still to try; a retransmission is answered as the first packet was, the relay counts the sender as tried too, and the count the relay recorded is spent. A return answered seen is dropped, the parent having gone on without it. | *Adopted 2026-10-31.* Main's fourth case was wrong as a search: a relay that took a copy from a node it had not handed to kept its first parent, so when it had nothing forward it returned there, and the sender, done by the ACK, never tried its other candidates: a branch of the graph was dropped. Answered seen, the sender exhausts its candidates before anyone searches on; the measured cost against main's shortcut, 8 to 12 points at three times the design rate and 18 % airtime in the mesh burst, is paid where main's splits searched in parallel, and the splits are the storm candidate's parts 2 to 4, measured on this base from here on.* *Measured* against main `ab38727` and D89, delivered of the texts sent in the settled hour, the run's airtime: `dense-power.toml` seed 1, the storming seed, 90.3 → 89.3 % (D89 97.9), 1 134 454 → 1 162 844 s (D89 202 693), 1.84 M seen ACKs in the run; seed 7 98.2 → 98.1 %, 276 300 → 298 467 s (D89 200 781); S4 at three times the rate, seeds 1 / 2 / 3, 59.7 / 56.8 / 63.8 → 51.4 / 48.7 / 51.7 % (D89 72.8 / 71.8 / 83.6); S4 at the design rate 93.4 → 93.6 %; `dense-movers.toml` 96.5 → 96.5 % at 12 % more airtime; the ring unchanged. `mesh-burst.toml` seed 1 100 → 98 % (D89 86), `mesh-spread.toml` 100 → 100 %; the scenario test's bound of 0.9 holds. The storm is the split copies' life, not their path: answered seen, every copy lives until its own search ends, and spends a full frame and an ACK at each crossed relay it backtracks past; D89 ended the copies there, and ended a single search's copy too. Nothing in the records tells a split's copy from a single search's; the splits themselves are the candidate's parts 2 to 4, not yet built. The note's trace also found a trap of main's that the backtrack reaches more often: a relay whose last untried neighbour is heard but cannot be sent to waits for that neighbour's bans until the time to live (roadmap D42), since its entry never expires. |
| D91 | **A sleepy node with an anchor works by the anchor's position and runs no GPS: it beats the anchor's position, centres its plane on it and rebuilds the short positions it hears near it, takes no fix, runs no search and keeps the carried flag it had; it searches only without an anchor, when it turns anchorless and once a delivery TTL while it stays so, and a search under way ends when it takes one; a node with no place searches until it has one; the search at boot and at a new anchor and the anchor's move check go** (spec "Nodes", "Position", "Heartbeat", "Relay choice", "Beats", "Delivery", "Positions on the wire", "Packet formats", O19; `relay/gps.rs`, `relay/anchor.rs`, `relay/motion.rs`, `Node::address`, `Action::Locate` at or before now; `tests/gps.rs`, `tests/carried_sleepy.rs`, `tests/first_fix.rs`; `core-interface.md` §4, §5; the simulator's `gps.rs` and its `ended at an anchor` count; `docs/sleepy-gps-2026-10-31.md`). Hearing its anchor each rotation says the node is within reach of a fixed relay, which is all its address needs. Of what its own fixes bought it under D80, the carried flag nothing reads while it sleeps and the relay's GPS settles at plug-in (D85); the move check could not fire, since the only fix an anchored node took was the one at the choice, which reset its reference; and the rebuilding of short positions after a trip the anchor's position serves within reach, while beyond half a period, 19.5 km north-south, the fix at the new anchor did not serve it either, since D74 drops a fix farther than the hearing radius from every relay the node knows and the node then knows its anchor a period off (O19). *Measured* against main `ab38727`, delivered of the texts sent in the settled hour: S4 seeds 1 / 2 93.42 / 93.90 → 93.57 / 93.58 %, to walkers 87.57 / 88.32 → 87.59 / 87.62 %; `dense-movers.toml` 96.54 → 96.35 %, `dense.toml` 96.93 → 96.88 %, `strip.toml` 40.68 → 40.05 %, the ring identical; to walkers while they walk −0.6 to −4.5 points on 444 to 1610 texts, the same direction in every scenario; the sleepy nodes' GPS 0.022–0.023 → 0.020 mA, standby, a sleepy node 0.634 → 0.632 mA in S4; sleepy nodes turned carried 236–1053 → 0. | The saving is 0.003 mA, half a percent of a sleepy node's current, what D80 measured for the variant; the rule is taken for its simplicity, one position for a node that sleeps with an anchor, and for the bench, where a node in a pocket runs its GPS only when it hears no relay. The cost is a point or less of the texts to walkers while they walk. *Decided by the user 2026-10-31, built and measured 2026-10-31.* |
| D92 | **The receive duty cycle re-derived for the measured wake, and each node's periods from its own oscillator delay** (spec "Physical layer", "Duty-cycled receive and the long preamble", "Regulatory limits", "Heartbeat", "Hand-over", "Retry wait", "Beats", "Delivery", "Sleepy nodes alone"; `bark-proto/src/sniff.rs` `SNIFF_RESTORE`, `SNIFF_TCXO_START`, `SNIFF_GAP`, `periods`; `bark-driver/src/sx1262.rs` `sniff`; `core-interface.md` §3 rule 10; `bark-fw/README.md`, `scripts/measure.md` step 5; `docs/duty-cycle-2026-10-31.md`). The cycle was derived for a 6 ms wake, an oscillator start assumed at 5 ms and about 1 ms to restore the chip's state (datasheet §13.1.7); the bench probed the start at 0.5 ms (D88), so the wake is 1.5 ms. From it, each step as before: the window 3.75 ms where it was 6 (the header's wait and the two detections set 5.7 of its doubled 7.5 ms, the wake 1.5; without any wake it would be 3 ms), the cycle 238.9 ms where it was 361.1, the sleep 233.6 ms, the long preamble 927 symbols (237.3 ms) where it was 1396 (357.4), a long heartbeat 248.6 ms, a long full frame 372.8 ms where it was 492.9, the mean current receiving duty-cycled 0.129 mA where it was 0.194; a heartbeat's lag behind its slot 872.8 ms and the burst gap 1087.2 ms; an anchorless node's beats 26 s an hour where they were 38. `D` = 4 symbols, the currents, the 1 ms and the 60 s beat interval stay as assumed, none measured. The form was wrong in one thing: the wake is each board's own, probed from 0.25 to 4 ms and stepped up when cold, while the preamble is one length for the network, so a cold board would miss one long frame in seventy. What the network fixes is the gap a receiver is deaf for between two windows, 235.1 ms, and the preamble that spans it; a node whose delay is above the 0.5 ms the cycle is derived for, the least the probe can land on, takes the excess off its sleep and half of it onto its window, and the driver programs those. | *Adopted 2026-10-31, user direction of the same day.* Measured against main `9a7b82a`: S4 at the design rate, seed 1, delivered 93.6 → 93.5 %, p50 1.61 → 1.40 s, airtime 50 052 → 44 713 s, the busiest relay after the settle time 6.2 → 5.4 %, a sleepy node's radio 0.612 → 0.538 mA, with the GPS's standby 0.63 → 0.56 mA, 63 days on the battery; dense-movers 95.6 → 95.7 %, p50 5.6 → 5.4 s, airtime 176 000 → 170 000 s, 0.513 → 0.451 mA; ring over four seeds 92.6 → 93.4 % delivered, p50 57 → 49 s, within its spread. The simulator's test of the sniffing receiver spread its frames in step with the cycle and is spread by a hash now. Bench step 5 is still to run: every 927-symbol preamble caught, the shortest preamble caught gives `wake + 2D`, the pulse 1.5 + 3.75 ms every 238.875 ms. *The S4 currents the spec listed until 2026-11-05,* a sleepy node with an anchor at the design rate: listening, its windows, its anchor's heartbeats and checks, 0.41 mA; receiving duty-cycled 0.12 mA; transmitting, its beats and its own messages, 0.005 mA; GPS standby 0.020 mA; GPS searches none with an anchor, without one at most 0.054 mA; total 0.56 mA, 63 days on the 850 mAh battery; anchorless at most 0.61 mA, 58 days. |
| D93 | **A take is final when its ACK has gone out, and the ACK goes at once, without listening first: a relay decides on reception whether it takes or refuses a DATA, owes the ACK, and acts on a packet it takes, into its send queue, its delivery queue and its record, only once the ACK has left the antenna; an ACK that cannot go out leaves the packet untaken, and the sender's retry brings it again** (spec "Summary", "Hand-over" step 5, "The ACK", "Receiving DATA"; `bark-proto/src/node/transfer.rs` the owed reply carrying the DATA it takes and `Replies::untaken`, `relay/forward.rs` `verdict`, `act_on`, `taken` and the reply sent without a check, `event.rs` `DropReason::Unanswered`; the simulator's `unanswered` refusal count; `bark-proto/tests/routing.rs` `an_ack_goes_at_once_and_the_take_is_final_with_it`, `relay/tests.rs` `a_message_for_this_node_is_taken_when_its_ack_has_gone_out`; `docs/storm-ack-2026-10-31.md`). Part 2 of the storm note's candidate. Before, the relay took the packet on reception and the ACK listened before talking on the sender's channel; a reply whose backoff passed the sender's timeout was dropped with the packet kept, so the sender, hearing nothing, retried, failed, and handed the message to another neighbour as well: in the storm's hour 245 277 failed hand-overs had a receiver that sent the message on without any ACK having gone out. The sender listens for the ACK on its home channel and expects nothing else there, so listening first only moved the collision to the ACK's backoff. | *Adopted 2026-11-02.* *Measured* against main `09b7d73`, which does not storm at seed 1 (97.8 %, 299 754 s), delivered of the texts sent in the settled hour and the run's airtime: `dense-power.toml` seeds 1 / 7, 97.8 / 98.0 → 97.8 / 98.1 %, 299 754 / 305 040 → 283 128 / 271 385 s; S4 at the design rate 93.4 → 93.6 %, 44 213 → 43 498 s; S4 at three times the rate, seeds 1 / 2 / 3, 51.3 / 52.1 / 48.8 → 63.6 / 61.1 / 77.5 %, 169 524 / 167 990 / 187 758 → 150 125 / 151 739 / 144 791 s, p50 158 / 162 / 197 → 83 / 94 / 32 s; `dense-movers.toml` 96.4 → 96.5 %; the ring 91.4 → 92.6 %; the mesh burst 100 → 99 of 100, spread 100 → 100 %. Hand-overs that failed with the receiver holding a record 125 885 → 108 676 on the dense city, 44 738 → 28 661 under load. The take at the ACK alone, the ACK still listening first (form B1), gets about half under load, 57.0 / 55.8 / 64.8 %, and counts the silent takes the old rule made: 4 299 in the dense city's hour, 14 557 to 20 252 under load. *As the spec summed it up until 2026-11-05:* at three times the design rate the two together deliver 9 to 29 points more with 10 to 23 % less airtime, and at the design rate the same with 2 to 11 % less. |
| D94 | **A probe in place of the hand-over's listen-before-talk, answered by the receiver: not adopted.** After its check the sender transmits a 14-byte probe on the receiver's home channel and listens there for the receiver's answer, sent at once; the frame follows the answer, and no answer within 18.5 ms is one unanswered attempt (`docs/storm-ack-2026-10-31.md`, "The forms", B2). The form of the bench list's "listen-before-talk on the home channel answered by the listening receiver", derived in the note since no document fixed it. Its code, a probe kind on the wire, two hand-over states and the answer as a reply, was removed after the decision, as measured and rejected code is; the note describes it closely enough to rebuild. | *Measured 2026-10-31, not adopted; the code removed 2026-11-02.* Where the channel binds it is worth a quarter of the airtime: `dense-power.toml` seeds 1 / 7, 97.9 / 98.0 % at 231 861 / 224 803 s against main's 299 754 / 305 040 s, with a third of main's DATA frames (0.51 M against 1.37 M) and no relay ever at its cap against 108; the walkers 12 % less airtime at the same delivery. Where the bucket binds it halves delivery: S4 at three times the rate 51.3 / 52.1 / 48.8 → 26.6 / 26.1 / 25.2 %, with 10 % more airtime, a third of it probes and answers, and hand-overs held back by the bucket 1.0 M → 22 M; with D93 as well 33.0 / 33.0 / 34.8 %. At the design rate S4 93.4 → 92.9 % at 5 % more airtime and the median latency 1.4 → 1.8 s. What it revealed: 57 % of the probes on the dense city found the receiver deaf or busy, and half the answers did not reach the sender, so two frames in three of the old rule's DATA went to a receiver that could not take them. A cheaper form would start from the note. |
| D95 | **A neighbour silent past its demotion is tried for the message: the search goes back instead of waiting** (spec "Neighbour table", "Hand-over" step 6, "Bans", "Overload waits", "Message records", "Next hop", "Return to parent", "Why the search finds a path", "Bounds"; `tables/records.rs` `Record::silent`, `silent_to`, `tried`; `relay/forward.rs` `handover_failed`, `hop`, `others_open`, `ban_ends`, `route`; `event.rs` `Trace::PastSilence`; the simulator's `past_silence` counters; `docs/return-silence-2026-10-31.md`). A hand-over that fails to a demoted neighbour, or whose failure demotes it, marks the neighbour silent for that message in the relay's record: in its tried set, so the message is offered to it no more there and does not wait for its ban, and with nothing else left returns to its parent, or ends at the origin as no route. A refusal is pushback as before, and so is the first silence: the message waits one ban and tries the neighbour again (D42). Per message, a bit per slot: 8 bytes a record, 12.3 KiB a relay; the next message tries the neighbour once more, after its ban. | *Candidate, built and measured 2026-10-31 and 2026-11-02, not adopted as built: it brings the dense-power storm back.* `dense-power.toml` seed 1, 97.8 → 88.2 % of the settled hour's texts, the run's airtime 299 754 → 1 269 227 s, failed hand-overs whose receiver held the message 125 885 → 870 599; seed 7, 98.0 → 94.6 %, airtime 305 040 → 428 401 s. A hand-over whose ACKs were lost twice leaves the receiver holding the message, demoted and silent for it at the sender; before, the sender's copy, when it came back, was offered to the receiver again and merged with the copy held there (spec "Receiving DATA" case 4, D36, D42), and that offer, which the rule stops, was the split's heal. Elsewhere, delivered of the texts sent in the settled hour against main `09b7d73`: S4 at three times the rate, seed 1, 51.3 → 55.2 %, latency p50 158 → 135 s, airtime 4 % less, 13 134 fewer failed hand-overs, the offers a search coming back through a relay made again to a neighbour silent for the message; S4 at the design rate 93.4 → 93.7 %; the walkers 96.4 → 96.5 %, p90 57 → 50 s, losses moved from the time to live (114 → 59) to the hop cap (161 → 212) and failed returns (1 → 21); the ring 91.4 → 91.8 %, three settled texts lost as no route that main delivered late; the mesh at seed 5 delivers every reachable text on both and ends the six main held to the time to live as failed returns, the spread run one text more. The D90 note's trap: a relay whose last candidate is heard but never reached waited for the ban ladder, 1.58 s doubling to 61 s, until the time to live, since its heartbeats kept the entry alive and a banned neighbour not tried kept the message waiting (D42). Silence twice is not pushback: a refusal names its wait, a dead or one-way link does not refill. Per message rather than the table's demotion alone so that each message gets one hand-over of its own at the neighbour, after its ban: the demotion was earned by other messages' frames, lost to collisions far more often than to a dead link (D34), and a line's only way on is not closed by two lost frames. On a line whose only way on is a faint link the message gets two hand-overs, six frames, where it got up to 33: a link carrying one frame in ten delivers 47 % instead of 97 %. To measure next, from the note: the silent mark as a reason not to wait only, the neighbour staying a candidate; or a mark that lifts with the neighbour's demotion, so that one alive enough to ACK anyone is offered the message again. *Variant (b), the mark counting only while the neighbour is demoted, measured 2026-11-02 on main `8ff3a74`, with D93 (the note's last section; the code on the branch `worktree-agent-a7dcf1e57960c8617` at `e09b541`): not adopted.* Level with main everywhere, within the seed spread: `dense-power.toml` seeds 1 / 2 / 7, 97.8 / 98.1 / 98.1 → 97.8 / 98.2 / 98.1 %, airtime 0 to 2.6 % less, hand-overs failed with the receiver holding the message 6 to 16 % fewer, relays ever at their cap 73 / 86 / 73 → 56 / 55 / 43, no storm; at twice the rate 88.8 / 89.1 → 88.5 / 88.9 %; S4 at the design rate 93.6 → 93.5 %, at three times the rate 63.6 → 63.7 % with the median text 83 → 95 s; the walkers 96.5 → 96.6 %; the ring 92.6 → 94.3 %; the mesh at seed 5 delivers the texts main held to the time to live, burst 92 → 93, spread 83 → 87 of 90. The losses move rather than shrink: on the city those at the time to live halve and those at the hop cap, as failed returns and as no route rise as much or more. The base no longer storms, so whether the lift or D93 keeps the storm away is not measured. After D102 the mark as built would contradict the hold, which keeps a message with its demoted neighbour until three failures; ported, set when the message moves on, its one remaining effect is to end the wait for a demoted last candidate's ban ladder after the hold, the tail D102 left as it was (p90 181.8 s, longest 597 s, on S4 at three times the rate). Not to be re-measured unless that tail is wanted gone for its own sake: on this base ending it moved losses and gained only the mesh's trapped texts. |
