A message whose next hop has been banned since it was queued, or that has had a wait of its own, goes ahead of every message that has not, the most travelled first, for as long as it is queued; the rest keep their order** (spec "Send queue", "Overload waits"; `tables/send_queue.rs` `Outgoing::waited`, `SendQueue::next_due`; `relay/forward.rs` `pick`; tests `a_message_that_waited_keeps_its_turn`, `the_offer_keeps_the_mark`; `docs/waited-2026-11-05.md`). A read-through found the code's mark sticky against the spec's rule that only the messages a ban held go first when it ends. Three other rules were built and measured against it, release, the same seeds: the spec's rule as written (cleared at the offer and on a new next hop, own waits not counted; commit `A message that waited for a ban goes first once`), the mark cleared only on a new next hop (research feature `mark-until-reroute`), and every message by count (research feature `all-by-count`). *Measured*, main / fix / all-by-count / mark-until-reroute: the district at 32 times (seeds 1–3, settled half hour) 7 998 / 7 873 / 7 666 / 7 880 texts per 10 min, p50 53 / 54 / 43 / 58 s, p99 973 / 914 / 955 / 961 s, 83.0 / 82.2 / 83.0 / 82.3 % of the accepted delivered, 32 999 / 35 097 / 29 450 / 34 094 refusals, queue stays over 20 min 2 / 2 / 14 / 3; at 64 times 59.3 / 57.6 / 65.2 / 58.4 % of the accepted, p50 211 / 227 / 170 / 234 s, 3.52 / 3.60 / 3.26 / 3.53 s of air per text, 74 819 / 78 497 / 63 064 / 76 113 refusals; the city at four times, the last hour of four, p90 208 / 221 / 243 / 187 s, p99 913 / 946 / 1 137 / 918 s, 95.4 / 94.9 / 94.7 / 95.6 %, 4.61 / 4.90 / 4.40 / 4.39 s of air; at the design's rate all four the same within a run's noise. | The gate lets a higher count in whatever the wait on its way and refuses a lower one once that wait passes the budget, so the argument for the most travelled first holds at every offer to a loaded way, not only at a ban's end. Main's mark picks out the messages that have met loaded ways and leaves the rest in order: it is never the worst of the four, its p99 is within 7 % of the shortest everywhere, and no starvation showed. Clearing the mark sooner offers lower counts first on loaded ways, which are refused, cost air and ban the neighbour again. Ordering every message by count is far the best at 64 times, where four texts in five are told to wait, but starves the least travelled nearer capacity, as the spec said: at 32 times fewer texts per 10 min in every seed and seven times the queue stays over 20 min, and in the city a p99 25 % longer. *Measured 2026-11-05; main's rule kept and specified. The research features are not kept; their commits are on the branch.* |
| D115 | **A node's own application's message, refused, goes round the refuser as a forward does (D110): routed at once to the next candidate while the refuser's ban lasts, and waiting on its hold only where no other is left that is no worse a last resort; every refusal in a row, gone round or not, is a step of the hold; a sleepy node's own message still goes back to its application with the wait told** (spec "Bans", "A sleepy node's own message"; `relay/forward.rs` `refused`, `goes_elsewhere`; `docs/pushback-2026-11-03.md`, "Round seven: the own message's detour"). *Measured* as the research build `own-detour` on main `9c8b35f`, release, district seeds 1–3 settled half hour, city seed 1; main → this rule. District at 32 times: 7 934 → 7 971 texts per 10 min, 82.4 → 81.8 % of the accepted delivered, 92.4 → 92.2 % within 10 min, p50 54 → 52 s, every figure within the seeds' range; a relay origin's own message refused 4 344 → 2 530 times, 97 % of them going round, and the origin clear of it sooner, p90 from taken to first sent on 56 → 29 s. At 64 times, past capacity: 8 900 → 9 269 texts per 10 min, but 58.3 → 55.7 % of the accepted delivered and 78 172 → 83 088 refusals sent; relay origins deliver 4 420 → 4 849 texts per 10 min, sleepy origins 4 481 → 4 420, their median 227 → 241 s; in each seed the accepted delivered fall 2 to 3 points, relay origins deliver more and a sleepy origin's median rises 6 to 23 s. City at four times, the last hour of four: 11 730 → 11 798 texts per 10 min, p50 / p90 45 / 251 → 38 / 214 s, 94.9 → 95.4 % of the accepted delivered, 97.3 → 97.6 % within 20 min, air per delivered text 5.04 → 4.67 s, a relay origin's own message refused 24 371 → 10 429 times. At the design's rate no relay origin's own message is refused in the city, S4, the ring or seeds 1–10 of the corridor, the mesh burst and the spread mesh, and each run is main's to the message. The adopted build is the research build's to the byte (`docs/pushback-2026-11-03.md`, "Round seven", "Verdict"). | The refuser has not taken the message, so going round it leaves no copy, and an origin with several neighbours offers where there is room instead of waiting for the one that has none, as a relay does. A sleepy node has one candidate, its anchor, and is told to wait. The hold stays for the origin alone with its refuser, counting every refusal so that more neighbours do not make it offer faster. Past capacity the rule moves who waits: a relay origin, clear of its message sooner, writes and delivers more, and a sleepy origin, whose anchor is the refuser it cannot go round, a little less. *Adopted 2026-11-05, the user:* one rule for a refused message, with no special case for the origin's own, taken for its simplicity, at the cost to sleepy origins past capacity. |
| D116 | **The triangle rule: a relay that, with two neighbours it has tried for a message, encloses the destination position drops it** (spec "Next hop", "Overload waits", "Hop count", "Why the search finds a path"; `geo.rs` `encloses`, `relay/forward.rs` `enclosed`, `event.rs` `DropReason::Triangle`; `docs/triangle-2026-11-05.md`). A corner is a neighbour in the record's `handed` set but the parent that holds mail; the relay is the third where it may own a destination near it. Tested each time the message is routed here, before a candidate is chosen, so when the second corner's return or seen arrives; exact in position counts; three on a line enclose nothing. The message ends and nothing is sent for it. Why: a text whose node has gone from its address, mostly a carried node that walked on, has no owner, and the search walks the city to the hop cap. The record and `Relay` are unchanged. | *Measured 2026-11-05, not adopted 2026-11-06:* with the addressed node first (D116a) most of its searches end at the node, and on top of that it saves about one percent of the texts' air (`docs/triangle-2026-11-05.md`, status line) for a geometric test at every routing. Main `e2e2761`, the city (`dense-power.toml` seed 1): 0.55, 1.00 and 0.92 % of accepted texts end at the cap at ×1, ×3 and ×4, 72 to 91 % of them to carried nodes, at 41 to 51 s of air each against 2.0 to 4.0 s per delivered text, 14.7, 14.0 and 11.4 % of all air. With the rule: air of lost texts 8.7, 9.7 and 8.3 % of all air, a text ended by the triangle 7 to 12 s; delivered of accepted 97.55 → 97.28, 97.28 → 96.95 and 95.34 → 95.27 %, all of the loss on texts to carried nodes (−1.5 to −1.8 points), which on main arrive about half the time after a long walk that meets the walker; at ×4 latency p50 36.5 → 31.2 s. District ×32 seeds 1 to 3 82.12 → 82.88 %, S4 seeds 1 to 10 94.48 → 94.50 %, ring, corridor, mesh burst and spread mesh unchanged. A variant that drops only when no untried live neighbour could own the destination, by its ID or holding mail within 50 m, is measured in the note and not built: 97.39, 97.14 and 95.35 % delivered, 10.3, 10.6 and 9.1 % of all air on lost texts. |
| D116a | **The addressed node goes first: a candidate whose ID is the message's `dst_id` is first in the order of spec "Next hop", before margin, demotion and distance, and a stale carried relay is no last resort for a message addressed to it; a sleepy node with an anchor stays a last resort even then** (spec "Routing", "Owner", "Next hop"; `relay/forward.rs` `choose`, `ends_here`; tests `the_addressed_node_goes_first`, `the_addressed_sleepy_node_stays_a_last_resort`, `an_owner_hands_to_the_addressed_neighbour_at_once`; `docs/addressed-2026-11-06.md`). The same holds at the owner of the destination position (spec "Owner"): with the addressed node a live neighbour it has not tried, other than a sleepy one with an anchor, it hands the message over at once rather than queue it for the node's beat on its channel, which on the bench, three relays within 50 m of each other indoors, came 60 to 170 s later. Only the neighbour table is read, the heard table is not consulted, and the owner's delivery queue keeps its job for a node not in the table. Why: a carried node is seldom at the position a message bears, and the search for it otherwise ends only when it meets the node by chance, or at the hop cap; a stale walker costs three frames against a search of 41 to 51 s of air. A sleepy node with an anchor is reached at its address, its anchor's position, by the anchor's delivery in its window with the ordinary preamble (spec "Delivery"); handed the message by any relay that hears it, it costs the long preamble each time. | *Adopted 2026-11-06, the user, as variant (c) of the note.* City (`dense-power.toml` seed 1), main `324d1c1` → the rule (`b919329`, before the owner case): delivered of accepted 97.55 → 98.29, 97.33 → 98.50, 95.67 → 97.58 % at ×1, ×3, ×4; to carried nodes 93.54 → 98.71, 92.56 → 98.76, 89.56 → 97.89 %; lost at the hop cap 0.55 → 0.18, 0.95 → 0.11, 0.98 → 0.09 %; lost texts' share of their window's air 10.3 → 4.2, 15.8 → 2.5, 12.1 → 1.5 %; air per delivered text 2.00 → 1.94, 2.33 → 2.11, 3.70 → 3.12 s; median / p90 hops to a carried node 9 / 17 → 7 / 14, 10 / 20 → 8 / 15, 13 / 26 → 10 / 19; at ×4 latency p50 / p90 31.5 / 173 → 20.9 / 116 s. District ×32 seeds 1 to 3 82.22 → 83.55 %, carried 63.78 → 84.12 %. S4, ring, corridor, mesh burst and spread mesh as on main or within their seed spread. Not adopted, on main `e2e2761`: (a) a sleepy node with an anchor first too, the same city (98.27 % at ×1) but in mesh burst air per delivered text 0.89 → 1.13 s and latency p90 6 → 14 s, from relays handing other relays' sleepy leaves the message with the long preamble; (b) fresh entries only, 97.69 % at ×1, carried 94.92 %. The owner case, measured 2026-11-06 (main `389c30b` with and without it): on the bench's desk (`scenarios/desk.toml`, seeds 1 to 10) a text for a relay whose position its neighbours own arrives in 0.06 to 0.26 s at the median, under 1 s at most, against 44 s and up to 162 s; city ×1, ×3, ×4 98.29 → 98.38, 98.50 → 98.66, 97.58 → 97.46 %, to carried nodes +0.2 to +0.5 points, latency p90 24 → 22, 34 → 30, 116 → 106 s; district ×32 seeds 1 to 6 83.57 → 82.98 % (−1.9 to +1.2 by seed) and ×4 seeds 1 to 10 96.12 → 96.23 % with 34 texts at the hop cap against 24, both within their seed spread. |
| D117 | **The phone link is one GATT service of six characteristics, Node, Status, Send, Inbox, Given back and Map point, whose values keep the radio's encodings of positions, node IDs and message IDs; the phone is the queue: the node takes a message only while Status says ready, answers a write it cannot take now with the ATT error 0x80, and gives a refused message back with its ID and the seconds left of the wait; a later link version appends, and a change of meaning takes a new service UUID** (spec "Phone link", O20; `bark-link`; `docs/phone-link-2026-11-05.md`). Resolves O20. The node already holds one message of its application's at a time and gives back what is refused (D54, D115), so the link only has to say when the node takes one and what came back; the app keeps the rest. The write is answered once the node has taken the message, so a success means the node has it. Given back stays readable until the next message is taken, since the phone may be away when it happens. One encoding of a position, an ID and a message ID for the radio and the link, so the app computes a message's ID as every node does. Versions grow as the heartbeat does: readers ignore appended bytes. |
| D118 | **Messages for the app wait in an inbox of 23, read oldest first and forgotten only when the app writes the message's ID back; Status's count says when to read** (spec "Phone link"; `bark-link` `Message`, `Confirm`; `docs/phone-link-2026-11-05.md`). A phone is often not connected, and a notification is confirmed by nobody, an indication only by the phone's Bluetooth stack: the ID written back is the app's own word that it keeps the message. 23 is a day's texts per person at the design rate (spec "Capacity and sizing"), in the core 23 places of the delivery queue's 264 B, 5.9 KiB (D122). |
| D119 | **The node offers an ATT MTU of 247 and takes long writes and long reads; the app asks for 247 at once; notified values fit 20 bytes** (spec "Phone link"; `bark-link::ATT_MTU`). 247 is the longest link-layer payload with the data length extension, 251 bytes, less the L2CAP header's 4 (Bluetooth Core Specification 5.0 Vol 6 Part B §2.4, Vol 3 Part A §3.1), so the longest value, a 240-byte Send, goes in one PDU and one packet. Long writes and reads too, because iOS chooses the MTU itself; a notification has no long form, so the notified values, Status (14 bytes) and Given back (6), fit the default MTU's 20. |
| D120 | **The node advertises, connectable, every 1022.5 ms, the service UUID in the advertisement and its name `bark-` and its ID's twelve hex digits in the scan response; one connection at a time, and no advertising while connected** (spec "Phone link"). About a second to show in a scanning app, at one of the intervals an iPhone finds quickly (Apple's accessory guidelines), and an estimated 11 µA, a tenth of a sleepy node's duty-cycled receive, for the bench to measure. The name matches the ID on the node's display. One phone owns a node's inbox and message slot. |
| D121 | **The phone link is open for the prototype: no pairing, no bonding** (spec "Phone link", O23). The payloads are the app's ciphertext (O1), and the addresses, IDs, positions and flags the link carries are on the 433 MHz air in the clear already. What the open link allows is active: anyone in Bluetooth range, while the owner's phone is not connected, can empty the inbox and move the node by a map point. Open keeps the bench and the first app free of pairing; O23, likely a passkey on the node's display, comes before nodes leave the bench. *Superseded by D135, 2026-11-06.* |
| D122 | **The inbox is the core's: every node keeps the messages for its own application, up to 23, until the application confirms each by its ID, and a full inbox follows the delivery queue's rule, a stale message making way for a new one and a full one of fresh messages refusing the next with a wait; `AppEvent::Message` names the message, and the driver reads the oldest with `Core::inbox` and confirms it with `Core::confirm`** (spec "Delivery queue", "Receiving DATA", "Phone link", O22; `tables/delivery.rs` `INBOX`, `relay/forward.rs` `arrive`, `has_room_for`; `core-interface.md` §4, §6; `bark-driver` `Request`; the simulator's and the tests' drivers confirm each message at once). Resolves O22. *Decided* by the user 2026-11-05. The simulator and the firmware share the core, so a node's messages are kept where both run the same code; and the rule for a full inbox is one the spec already has, so a message the node has taken is never lost for want of room: refused, it waits at its sender. The inbox is 23 × 264 B, 6 088 B in `Relay` and `SleepyNode`. S4 seed 1 at the design rate, release, is main's to the byte (the JSON, the CSV and the message log; the text output but for its wall time). |
| D123 | **An identity is one X25519 key pair made from a 128-bit seed that the person backs up as 12 words of a fixed 2048-word list; every key comes from the seed through libsodium's key derivation; all cryptography is libsodium's fixed constructions, `crypto_box` between identities, the sealed box for a first message, `crypto_secretbox` with the mailbox; no signatures, no forward secrecy** (spec "Phone link", O1; `bark-app-layer` `keys`, `seal`; `docs/app-layer-2026-11-05.md`, "Keys"). Resolves O1. | One call per operation and the same bytes in every implementation: RustCrypto's `crypto_box` 0.9.1 and `crypto_secretbox` 0.1.1, pinned, make `bark-app-layer/vectors.json`, which libsodium 1.0.18 opens, and the app uses libsodium itself. A box proves its sender to its receiver and the mailbox key proves a fetch, so a signature's 64 bytes buy nothing; forward secrecy needs a round trip, 32 bytes a message and state every phone of an identity would share. 128 bits is X25519's own security. *Decided by the user 2026-11-05.* |
| D124 | **A payload is a format byte and one of four forms: boxed to a contact (41 bytes over the plaintext), an introduction in a sealed box (49), a fetch to one's mailbox (41) and a mailbox's reply (17); the app writes at most 210 bytes; a boxed payload names no sender, and the receiver tries its contacts' keys; no padding** (spec "Phone link"; `bark-app-layer` `seal`). | Trying costs milliseconds and saves 32 bytes, and names nothing a relay could link. 210 = 227 − 17, so that every payload can come back out of a mailbox. Padding would cost a text up to 28 % more airtime and hide little, as destination, hop count and the first hop's sender are in the clear. *Decided by the user 2026-11-05.* *2026-11-06:* a reply also seals the item's number, so it adds 20 bytes and the app writes at most 207 (D128's line of that day). *2026-11-06 (D140):* and the count of items still unsent, 2 bytes: a reply adds 22, and the app writes at most 205. *2026-11-06, on the bench:* the sender introduces itself until a message from the receiver, of either form, has opened, not until it has verified the receiver: only a box verifies, so two phones holding each other's cards sent introductions all day and neither was verified (spec "Phone link"). *2026-11-06:* a first message naming a verified contact adds its text, marked as a first message's, and changes nothing else; dropping it lost one that waited in a mailbox while a later box overtook it, and its `sent`, which whoever seals it writes, cannot tell that from a forgery (spec "Phone link"). |
| D125 | **A boxed message's plaintext is a flags byte, `sent`, the sender's clock in seconds and later than any it sent before, the address to answer to when the contact may not have it, and records: text (UTF-8, or windows-1251 when it holds the text), acknowledge, card with its length; a flag bit gives a box its direction** (spec "Phone link"; `bark-app-layer` `plain`, `text`). | `sent` orders, names a text and stops replays, without two phones of one identity sharing a counter; the network's message ID cannot, as the mailbox changes the destination it hashes. Single-byte Cyrillic nearly doubles a Ukrainian text's room. The card record carries its length, a byte the note did not count, since a text follows it in a first message. *Decided by the user 2026-11-05.* *2026-11-06, the user:* the address is the node's the sender writes from, not the one on their card (D132). |
| D126 | **The app acknowledges and resends nothing by itself: the person may press "acknowledge", which sends a record naming the text by `sent`, at the cost of a short text, and may send a text again** (spec "Phone link", "Addresses"). | The design rate keeps its 23 texts a person a day; an automatic acknowledgement would cost two thirds of a text for each one. *Decided by the user 2026-11-05.* *2026-11-06, the user:* a changed card too goes to the contacts only when the person presses "Send my card", or confirms a new home or none; the app sending it by itself when the home's position changed flooded the mesh that day. *2026-11-10:* reactions take the acknowledgement's place (D185). |
| D127 | **A card is version, identity, address, the address's time and a name of up to 32 bytes, 50 to 82 bytes, shown as `BARK:` and base32 in a QR code of version 6 or less at error correction M; its address is the home node's when the person has one, else their node's current one; when it changes the person sends their card to their contacts** (spec "Phone link"; `bark-app-layer` `card`). | Base32 is in the QR code's alphanumeric set, so the card is also text that can be pasted, for a tenth more modules. A card in a message is the same bytes. *Decided by the user 2026-11-05.* *2026-11-06, the user:* card version 2 carries an address only when it is a home node's, as any other is only where the person was and where they write from comes in every header (D132): version, flags (bit 0, a home address follows), identity, when the home was set or cleared, the home's address or nothing, and the name, 38 to 70 bytes without a home and 51 to 83 with one; changed in place, as no card is out yet (D109's rule). |
| D128 | **A person may set a fixed relay as their home, giving it a mailbox key derived from the identity; the node keeps every payload for its node ID in a ring in flash, which its phone link's inbox reads, and on a fetch sealed with that key, newer than the last, sends each item after the fetch's number, sealed again with the key under a nonce of the fetch's time and the item's number, to the fetch's address; the app fetches only when the person asks, for 23 items at most; the mailbox is an application over the core's interface, not a change to the core** (spec "Mailbox", "Capacity and sizing"; `core-interface.md` §4; `bark-app-layer` `seal`). | Not everyone has a spare phone. The node cannot read the mail; sealed again, a second reply is a new message, and its number reaches only the requester. `since` keeps the node free of state per requester and bounds a loss by the next fetch. Fetched mail crosses the city twice, and the fetch costs four fifths of a text more, so the app spends that air only when a person wants their mail, never by itself. The ring's pages come from the SoftDevice's region if the board's bootloader runs without it, else 8 of the update's staging: a bench test decides. *Decided by the user 2026-11-05; fetching only when asked, 2026-11-06.* *2026-11-06, the user, after a bench fetch that found nothing new got no answer:* a fetch is answered once and ends, with the items after `since` or one 17-byte reply that says nothing is new, so the person can tell no mail from a loss; a later item waits for the next fetch. *The same day:* the fetch carries no count and gets every item after `since`, as a cap only spreads the replies over more fetches and back-pressure paces them; so that a requester that does not know which numbers the ring holds (`since` 0, or away longer than the ring lasts) can open them, a reply's nonce holds its place in the round, 1 to 256, and its plaintext the item's number (spec "Mailbox"). *2026-11-06 (D140):* the fetch carries no `since`; it gets the newest 32 items not yet sent, each marked sent in the ring, with the count of those still unsent. |
| D129 | **The firmware runs the L76K as the core says, and keeps the time of day from its fixes: the driver tells its application after every call to rest, search until an instant or track, ends a search at the first fix it hands, and hands a fix only if its satellites' C/N0 lies in a real sky's band and the core finds it within the hearing radius of a relay it knows (`Core::fix_in_reach`); UTC from a fix's RMC sentence is kept on the node's clock while its error can be under a minute, for people, not for the protocol** (spec "Position"; `bark-driver` `Gps`, `gnss.rs`, `utc.rs`; `bark-proto` `Core::gps_tracks`, `Core::fix_in_reach`, `gps::HEARING_RADIUS_M`; `bark-fw/src/gps.rs`, `utc.rs`; `core-interface.md` §4, §5; `docs/gps-time-2026-11-05.md`). The core had decided when the GPS runs since D80 and D82, and the firmware left the receiver running at 29 mA from boot, 696 mAh a day, the cell in 29 hours. The neighbour check moves from the simulator's driver into the core as a query, so that the simulator and the firmware ask the same rule; the simulator's runs are unchanged. The receiver rests with its WAKEUP line low, 20 µA, and its UART off; on battery its cost per day is 0.48 mAh with an anchor, at most 5.3 mAh for a resting relay that never sees the sky. The C/N0 band is O18, the receiver's whole scale until measured. The time keeps the minute for 2.7 days on a crystal taken at ±250 ppm, 1.4 days on the RC oscillator. To fit the slot, trouble-host's own log lines, 9.9 KB, are dropped. | *Built 2026-11-05; on the host only.* The position is parsed into counts in integers, as `Position::from_degrees` rounds them; the pins, the rate and WAKEUP's polarity wait for the bench (`docs/gps-time-2026-11-05.md`, "On the bench"). |
| D130 | **The phone link gains Clock, `28bc0008-…`, at link version 2: the app writes its UTC time and UTC offset at every connection; the node keeps the offset across a reboot, takes the time only while it has none from its GPS, and gives its time, offset and the time's source to a read** (spec "Phone link", "Position"; `bark-link` `Clock`, `NodeTime`, `VERSION`; `bark-fw/src/ble.rs`, `store.rs`, `utc.rs`; `bark-app`; `docs/gps-time-2026-11-05.md`). A sleepy node with an anchor never searches (D91), so without the phone it would have no time to show. | D131 | **The loader moves to `0x1000`, the MBR's end, and the SoftDevice's 148 KiB become ours: slots of 98 and 99 pages from `0x6000`, and a mailbox of 17 pages at `0xE2000`** (`docs/softdevice-2026-11-05.md`; `bark-image/src/layout.rs`; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Flash layout"). The board's bootloader starts the application at the SoftDevice's end address when the SoftDevice's information structure holds its magic number, and at `0x1000` when it does not; it runs without a SoftDevice, and its drive writes the application family anywhere in `0x1000..0xEA000`, so a UF2 that carries the loader also erases what is left of the SoftDevice. Of the 37 pages, 10 more for each slot give 40 KiB of room over the 351 368 B node image, and 1 + 16 for the mailbox hold 256 items, eleven days at 23 a day; 88, 89 and 37, or 106, 107 and none, were the other splits. *Tested on f4f5 2026-11-06*: with the SoftDevice erased and the loader at `0x26000` the drive came back by itself within 11 s, `INFO_UF2.TXT` reading `SoftDevice: not found`; with the loader at `0x1000` the firmware booted, and the 1200-baud touch gave the drive again. *2026-11-06:* the mailbox's state page, which holds its key, moves to `0xF2000`, its ring of 16 pages below it from `0xE2000`: at `0xE2000` the key was in the drive's `CURRENT.UF2`, readable after a double tap (`docs/flash-plan-2026-11-06.md`, "What must stay inside, and why"). The secrets now lie in `0xEA000..0xF4000`, which no UF2 writes or reads. *2026-11-06 (D139):* the slots and the ring are laid out again: the running slot takes 227 pages to `0xE9000`, the staged slot, the coded pieces and the mailbox's ring move to the external flash, and `0xEA000..0xF2000` is spare. |

A fix's HDOP tells good from bad (HDOP under 2: none beyond 69 m of the run's median), but the core is handed no HDOP, and a walker's pocket gives poor fixes too. With the loss of fix only pausing the run, a walker whose fixes come and go is still carried, and the GPS a far fix keeps running stays on. A walker is carried after 251 s at 1.4 m/s (179 s to the radius), 239 s at 1.5 m/s, against 108 s before; until then it heartbeats the place it left. `s1-line`, `desk`, `ring`, `corridor`, `mesh-burst` and `s2-block` repeat main to the byte: a still node in the simulator gets no fix. The walking relays of `dense-movers.toml`, `dense-power.toml` and `district.toml` turn carried about 143 s later, and the walking sleepy nodes without an anchor of those and of `dense.toml` and `strip.toml` may; these were not run. | Every still board at a window turned carried under 50 m; the radius is the measured wander's whole width with a margin, since a place is one fix and may lie at its edge. The rule is from a day's logs only. *Decided by the user 2026-11-06: widen now from the day's data and check against the night's logs; built 2026-11-06. *Checked against the evening's logs 2026-11-06, 16:45 to 20:35, three still boards, 39 037 fixes: none turned carried; the farthest fix from a board's first lay 280 m off, beyond 250 m for 10 s, in the first ten minutes after a boot at HDOP 25, and 248 m from its median; fixes lay beyond 100 m for up to 246 s. The receiver's speed over ground does not tell a still board from a walker: it errs with the position, a still board's speed averaging up to 4.9 m/s over a minute and 1.8 m/s over three against a walker's 1.4, and the displacement reckoned from speed and course agrees with the position's in those minutes; HDOP under 2 still left fixes 189 m from the median. Counting a far fix only at HDOP 3 or under, no run beyond 200 m lasted at all in the 40 board-hours and none beyond 150 m over 34 s; speed, course and step size do not separate the excursion from a walker (`docs/gps-bench-2026-11-06.md`, "The night"). A part costs one byte over a whole text: 159 bytes of text in a box without an address (157 since D140). 16 parts, 2.5 KB, are already more than two thirds of a person's 23 texts a day. The sender cuts between two characters as a person sees them, after the last space in the part's last quarter; the receiver shows what has come with a line for each missing part; one tap acknowledges every part that has come. | D138 | **A board that leaves the bench has its debug port closed: every image is protected unless built with `BARK_OPEN=1`, and first thing at every boot the firmware programs UICR.APPROTECT to `Enabled` on every chip revision, and on build code F also writes `APPROTECT.FORCEPROTECT`; an open build programs `HwDisabled` and writes `APPROTECT.DISABLE` on build code F and does nothing on earlier ones; after a UICR write it resets** (`bark-fw/src/protect.rs`; `bark-image` `protect`; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Read-out protection"; `docs/approtect-2026-11-06.md`). | The internal flash holds the bond's keys and the mailbox key, and a probe on the SWD pads reads it all unless the chip's protection is on. Protected by default, so that no image leaves the bench open by mistake; the bench's builds, `scripts/bench.py` and the released console are open. `Enabled` on revision 3 too, as the Product Specification advises, so that the port stays closed whatever a later image or the bootloader writes; protection is then one way on both revisions, undone only by a probe's erase of the whole chip, which also erases the bootloader (the note gives the maker's recovery). The firmware and not the loader, which is the same for both builds and never updated; the chip closes the port from reset, so nothing runs open before the firmware but the first boot after the flash, until its write. The bench loses only the pads, as the board's bootloader takes UF2s through the NVMC. | D139 | **What is large and not secret moves to the board's 2 MiB external flash: the staged image (228 sectors), the coded pieces (22) and the mailbox's ring (64 sectors, 1024 items); the running image, the loader, its state, the staged image's record, the mailbox's state and the stored state stay inside, and the running slot grows from 98 pages to 227** (`docs/flash-plan-2026-11-06.md`, section 1; `bark-image/src/layout.rs`, `ext_flash.rs`, `boot.rs`; `bark-loader`; `bark-fw/src/ext_flash.rs`, `mailbox.rs`, `update.rs`; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Flash layout", "Updates" and "Mailbox"). One driver serves the loader and the firmware: SPI mode 0 by software on the chip's four pins, the chip asleep in deep power-down between uses, its ID checked against `BA 60 15`. The loader raises the rail, checks the chip and hashes the staged image against the record before any copy; embassy-boot swaps between the two flashes. The firmware stages through the driver and keeps the mailbox's ring on the chip, its writes waited out with the executor free. Every board takes the UF2 with the loader once. | The CPU runs only from internal flash, and the loader must run with the NVMC alone; the record holds the hash the firmware checked, so that a clip on the external chip cannot pair a new image with a matching record; the keys stay inside, above the drive's `0xEA000`. The slot ends at the record, the last page the drive writes, so every UF2 still writes it whole and the record erased. Software SPI rather than an SPIM: every input of the chip stays at a rail between uses, which its 0.5 µA needs, no peripheral or interrupt is shared, and its speed is near the 8 MHz of SPIM0 to SPIM2. The external writes need no MPSL timeslot: they do not stop the CPU. *Decided by the user 2026-11-06; built the same day, on the host only*: the swap, the swap back and the power cuts on two flashes in RAM, the cancelled swap without a chip, and a swap and its swap back through the driver on a fake chip (`bark-image/src/boot/tests.rs`); loader 8 820 B of 16 KiB, node image 378 784 B of 929 792 B. The reply's plaintext is the item's number (3), the count (2) and the item, so a reply adds 22 bytes and the app writes at most 205; the reply that says nothing is new stays 17 bytes and means none remains. A place is the reply's rank in its round, 1 to 32, so the requester tries 32 places, not 256. A reboot ends a round, so that no place seals two items; what it had not handed over stays unsent. The node counts the unsent items when it reads the ring at boot and keeps the count; it reads a slot's mark byte alone while it looks for the batch. The fetch's address goes stale when the person moves, so a round must end within the delivery TTL, 600 s, and one press should cost a bounded amount of air; the count tells the person what is left. 32: a full bucket sends 33 full frames with the reply reserve left (spec "Send queue"), so a batch leaves the home node and passes each relay on its way without waiting for a refill, with a frame to spare. Marking at hand-over rather than on an acknowledgement keeps the mailbox free of state per requester and of a round trip; a reply lost on the way is still in the ring, and the phone reads it at home. No `since`: the home knows what it has sent, and a requester restored from its words knows nothing. Nothing on the node grows with the batch: a round is a few numbers, and its replies go one at a time through the send slot. *Decided by the user 2026-11-06; built 2026-11-06.* |
| D141 | **The phone link gains a characteristic from version 4, Neighbours: the app writes a slot and reads the live neighbours from it on, 13 to a read, with the slot to write next; a read gives the node's noise floor, its own position and, per neighbour, its ID, position, averaged RSSI in tenths of a dBm, seconds since heard and eight flags; the app draws them on a map of OpenStreetMap tiles, a line from the node to each coloured by the link's quality: fragile below the node's margin, 4.1 dB over the floor, good from 6.9 dB, where a full frame gets through a fade of two standard deviations, fair between** (spec "Phone link"; `bark-link` `neighbours`; `bark-proto` `radio::full_frame_rssi_through_db`; `bark-fw/src/ble.rs`, `node.rs`; `bark-app` `link.dart`, `fake_node.dart`, `ui/map_screen.dart`). | The user asked for a map of the paired node and the neighbours it knows, with the quality of each link. Pages, because every value fits one PDU and the table's 64 neighbours of 18 bytes do not; from a slot, because a slot keeps its neighbour, so a neighbour that leaves between two reads shifts none of the rest. Each neighbour's length is in the value, so that a later version can append to it. The noise floor rides along so that the app reads the margin as the node does; the RSSI in tenths, so that rounding does not move a link across the node's margin. The node publishes the table after each step, as it does the status, into a static of 2.6 KB that starts zeroed, and the BLE task builds a page from it: 2.5 KB of flash. The anchor is the one neighbour a node routes through that is cheap to tell; a relay's next hop depends on the destination. OpenStreetMap's tiles need no key or account; `flutter_map` keeps them on the phone, and the app takes a kept tile as fresh for a year, since the cache serves no tile it holds stale when the server cannot be reached, so an area seen once draws again during a long outage. *Asked by the user 2026-11-07; built the same day, on the host only.* |
| D142 | **A paired phone is told when the node's attribute table has changed: the GATT service gains Service Changed and the database hash; every change to the table comes with a new link version, version 5 the first; the node keeps with the bond the version whose table the phone last saw and the phone's subscription to Service Changed, and on the bond's encrypted link, while the phone is subscribed and saw another version, indicates Service Changed over every handle and, once confirmed, keeps its own version as seen; the app discovers again, without Android's copy, a table that has no Service Changed** (spec "Phone link", Changes to the table; `bark-link` `bond`, `VERSION`; `bark-fw/src/ble.rs`; `bark-app` `ble_link.dart`). | Seen on the bench 2026-11-07: after the update to version 4 both paired phones kept the table they had found before, missed Neighbours, and worked again only once their person forgot the bond and paired again. A phone keeps a bonded node's table and discovers it again only when told (Bluetooth Core Vol 3 Part G §2.5.2), and the node's GATT service was empty, which tells a phone the table never changes. The version, rather than the table, names what the phone saw, as every change to the table already comes with one and the app reads it: a change to the table without a new version is not allowed. It costs two bytes of the bond's record that were zero, so a bond kept before reads as a phone that saw no version and has not subscribed. Every handle, since the phone then discovers the whole table once per version, a few round trips. The hash, built by the host already, lets a phone that reads it as it connects find a change before the indication. The phones paired before version 5 kept a table with no Service Changed, which no indication reaches and whose handles all move as the GATT service grows, so the app's check carries them across. 4 KB of flash. *Asked by the user 2026-11-07; built the same day, not yet tried on a board.* |
| D143 | **A swap is tested by resets, not power cuts: a bench loader, built with `BARK_LOADER_BENCH=reset` and only with `BARK_OPEN=1`, stops each swap and each swap back once, at a moment drawn from the RNG evenly over the time its two flashes spend erasing and writing, and resets the chip; and a node on battery at or below the level at which a fixed relay turns sleepy starts no swap** (spec "Implementation notes"; `bark-image/src/boot/cut.rs`, `record.rs` `CUTS_AT`; `bark-loader/src/main.rs`; `bark-fw/src/update.rs`, `console.rs`, `protect.rs` `BARK_OPEN`; `bark-fw/scripts/swapbench.py`, `uf2.py`; `bark-proto` `BatteryLevels::swap_allowed`; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Updates"). What cuts a swap short on these boards is a battery that dies in it, the reset button or the watchdog, and on a board without a battery, as on a roof with a solar panel, its power. The bench cannot cut a T-Echo's power from its USB hub: the board runs on from its battery. A reset cannot land inside an operation of the internal flash, since the CPU halts while the NVMC erases or writes when it runs from flash (nRF52840 Product Specification, NVMC); so the operation the moment falls in is done in part: an erase as a partial erase as long as the erase had run, which leaves the page as an incomplete erase does, its bits undefined (same section); a write as the words written by then. The external chip is left running its operation, and the reset drops its rail. The time is each operation's: a page erase 85 ms and a word 41 µs inside (same section), a sector erase 10 ms and a page program 1.3 ms outside, typical (ZD25WQ16B datasheet, Table 4-3); reads count for none, since a reset in one leaves the flashes as between two operations. The moment is written to the record at the boot that starts the phase, before its first copy, and a boot that finds it draws none, so a swap is stopped at most once and ends at the next boot; where it fell is written before the reset, and the firmware logs both at its next start. `uf2.py` packs a bench loader only with an open firmware. The bench loader is 11 300 B of 16 KiB against 8 820 B; the log of the record costs the node image 992 B (2026-11-07). *Tested on the host*: each microsecond of a phase drawn by 119 or 120 of the 2^32 draws; the last moment falls in each phase's last word, so the time drawn over is the sum of the operations; 48 swaps, half of them swapped back, each stopped once on both flashes, in erases and writes, ending at the next boot with the expected image whole (`bark-image/src/boot/tests/cut.rs`). | A power cut from a hub tests nothing a T-Echo meets, and on the bench there is no hub that switches its ports. A reset in the loader reaches the same states of the flashes a power cut does, on every board, with no rig, and the draw from the chip's RNG is fresh at each boot. Once per phase, so that each round ends in a known number of boots and a failure is a finding, not a loop. The battery rule: a cell that gives out in the middle of a swap leaves the node with no image to run until it is charged, where without the swap it would run on asleep for days (spec "Sleepy nodes"); on USB nothing limits it. The console, reached over USB, refuses `commit` and `reboot` with a swap asked for by the rule; the phone link has no update yet, and will ask the same. *Decided by the user 2026-11-07; built the same day, on the host only.* |
| D144 | **The running firmware stages only an image signed by a key it holds: Ed25519 (RFC 8032) over the header's first 48 bytes, the SHA-256 (FIPS 180-4) of the image among them, in one of the header's three slots of a byte naming the key and its signature; the firmware holds up to three public keys, from `BARK_SIGNING_KEYS` at build time; it checks the version and the signature as the staging starts and the hash once the last byte has come; a protected build without keys holds none and stages nothing, and `scripts/uf2.py` and `image.py pack` refuse it, a protected build with the bench key fails, an open one without keys takes the bench key; one signature by a held key is enough** (`bark-image/src/sign.rs`, `boot.rs` `Stager::begin`; `bark-fw/build.rs`, `src/update.rs` `BARK_SIGNING_KEYS`, `stage`; `bark-fw/keys/`; `bark-fw/scripts/image.py` `keygen`, `sign`, `check`, `uf2.py`; `.github/workflows/release.yml`; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Updates"; spec "Implementation notes"). The release workflow signs the packed image with the private key in the secret `BARK_SIGNING_KEY`, under the id the built firmware gives that key, and fails if it holds none. The check is `ed25519-compact` 2.6.0, built for size, which also refuses a weak key and a signature not in its canonical form; its verification adds 17.4 KB to the node image where `ed25519-dalek` 2.2.0 added 25.4 KB (2026-11-07, `llvm-size`, a build that calls it); until the node stages it holds the keys alone, 288 B, and the console grows 17.7 KB. The script signs with the `cryptography` package. | A node on a roof is to be updated from the phone without a climb, and nothing in the radio range of its link may update it but the project. The firmware and not the loader checks, as D79 says, so that a key can be replaced. 48 bytes and not the image: the firmware hashes the image anyway, and the signature then binds the hash, the version and the length, and is checked before the image is sent. The version rule, above the running one, with the signature, stops a captured older image from being staged again; a bench build is version 0 and takes any signed one. Three keys, so that the next can be brought in by a release still signed with the last, and a second, kept apart, signs when one is lost. The quiet moment loses nothing a reboot would: a message held in RAM, a phone's link or its update; the power rule is D143's. The phone wins the slot from a gathering, as a person at the node chose that image and a gathering may take days, but not from an image being staged, whose record and swap a begin would undo half way. 372.8 ms is the longest a check may be held for a packet under way (`core-interface.md` §3 rule 8), so a later piece would go on an older check than the core allows for; one not sent is to the core a piece lost on the air. The core keeps no answer to an offer and has no command to stop a gathering, so a node whose gathered image fails its header's checks keeps telling its neighbours that image until it reboots, and refuses the same name meanwhile. *Built 2026-11-08, on the host and in the node image.* To make room, the same day: the store decodes two data pieces at a time and not eight and keeps a bit a piece for each lane, not the slots' indices, 6 952 → 3 928 B; the neighbour entries keep the image their section names once, among four, 128 → 112 B an entry (spec "Updates over the mesh"); the task serves between the store's steps, so that a piece asked for while a whole image is staged, about 3 s, is not filled, its future 2 304 → 1 600 B; the GPS's raw sentences for the bench are kept only in a console build, 736 B. Measured on main's D155 (the release image, open, version 7, `uf2.py --stack`, `llvm-nm`, 2026-11-09): the store 3 928 B, the task's future 1 600 B, the running image's table and header 776 B; the stack 115 024 B, against 120 256 B without the update and the 24 576 B floor; the image 471 064 B, 31 blocks. For the bench, `bark-fw/scripts/uf2.py --header IMG` makes a holder without staging: it writes the header of the packed, signed image of the same build into the UF2's record with the loader's checked word, as a swap into it leaves it, and refuses an image of another version or hash. Since 2026-11-09 the release workflow makes both its UF2s so, from the release's signed image, with and without the loader: up to v21 a node flashed with a release ran it but held no header, and served nothing until it took an image over the mesh or the phone. |

| D159 | **An image installed over the mesh confirms itself only once the node has heard another node, or reverts and is given up; one installed from the phone confirms as before, and the app warns when the node then hears no one.** The record of a staged image says how it came: a word after the bench loader's, `FROM_MESH`, all bits cleared, written by the mesh's staging (`Stager::written`) before it asks for the swap, erased for a person's staging from the phone or over USB. At the first boot of an image gathered over the mesh the firmware withholds the confirmation until the core says `AppEvent::Heard`, the first frame from any other node since boot that decodes (a heartbeat, a DATA, an ACK, a piece or a status, once a boot), then confirms through the MPSL's flash timeslots; with none within `update::heard_within`, three worst-case rotations of heartbeats and a heartbeat's lateness, 3 × 13 × 15 s + 872.8 ms = 585.9 s of its start, it resets itself and the loader swaps the previous image back. The watchdog is fed, as before, only from the confirmation on, so the loader's 600 s still ends the trial of an image that hangs or whose own deadline never fires; the deadline is checked against it at compile time. The image back, at its start and before it marks itself booted, writes the failed image's 32-byte name, from the record's header, which a swap back leaves, into the page `GIVEN_UP` at `0xEA000`, the first of the spare pages above every UF2, 128 slots, erased and begun again with the newest when full; the mesh's policy refuses an offer whose 4-byte name a slot begins with, so the node never gathers it again and never serves it, and a release after it, another name, is taken as usual. Any image swapped back is given up, a phone's too, which the phone may still stage. After an update it drove, the app reads Status's neighbours heard, sleepy ones too, a byte added to Status at link version 11 (an older node's relays known stand in for it), and if the node still hears none 585.9 s after it came back, the update screen and a bar over the conversations say the node hears no one and the update may have broken its radio, and the way back. For the bench, `BARK_UPDATE_BENCH=deaf` builds an image deaf to every frame on the boot that swapped it in, and only then (spec "Updates over the mesh", the install; "Phone link", Updates; `bark-image/src/boot.rs` `start`, `confirm`, `Origin`, `record.rs` `ORIGIN_AT`, `given_up.rs`, `layout.rs` `GIVEN_UP`; `bark-proto` `AppEvent::Heard`, `update::heard_within`; `bark-fw/src/update.rs` `started`, `trial`, `update/mesh.rs` `given_up`, `node.rs`; `bark-app` `updater.dart` `hearsNoOne`, `ui/update_screen.dart`, `ui/conversations_screen.dart`, `fake_node.dart`; `core-interface.md` §4; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Updates"; "Phone link", Status; `bark-link` `Status::heard`). | A node that gathered an image over the mesh had a neighbour to take it from, so an image that runs but cannot hear or be heard, a wrong radio setting, a broken frame format or a timing fault, is caught by requiring that it hear one, before it confirms and leaves the node deaf beyond the next image's reach. A node updated from the phone may be the only node around and must not revert for ever for lack of company, so there the person holding the phone is told instead. Any node, not one known before: who sent the frame does not matter to what it proves, and the core keeps no neighbours across a reboot. It proves only that the image hears, not that it is heard. Three rotations: each neighbour's heartbeat reaches the node's channel once a rotation and a listening receiver loses about one in ten (spec "Channel at boot"), so two miss a lone neighbour one time in a hundred and the third, the margin, one in a thousand, since a revert gives the image up for good; the October plan's 10 minutes (`mesh-update-2026-10-25.md`, "Revert") was two rotations and a margin, unfixed. The given-up page is inside, beside the record, so that a node's own flash keeps it, and above every UF2, so that a copy over USB does not clear it. Neighbours heard, not relays known: a node whose only neighbours are sleepy hears them, so its radio works, and the relays count alone would warn of it falsely; the byte fit the 20 a notification carries at the default MTU, and Settings' "Hears N nodes" reads the same count, so the two agree. The way back the app gives is USB: the node stages from the phone only an image above its own version (D144), so the previous release cannot be sent from the phone. *Decided by the user 2026-11-09; built the same day, on the host and in the node image.* The open release build of version 16: the image 481 472 B against main's 478 640, the stack 111 528 B against 112 008. *Tested on the host*: the record's origin; a gathered image on trial through a reset, confirmed, and one that hears no one swapped back and given up, by its whole name and its 4-byte one; a power cut at each of the four operations after a swap back still gives it up; the given-up page's round trip, a name twice written once, and the full page begun again; the core tells the first frame from another node once, not its own nor bytes that do not decode, and again after a reset; the app's warning shown 585.9 s after the update on the fake node, which hears no one after it, and not on one that hears its relays, nor on one that hears only sleepy nodes. The bench step (Phase 2, "An image that hears no one") is to be run. |
| D160 | **A node's screen shows a QR code of the project's page by default, its touch pad shows the lines for 30 s, and its button held 3 s flips the role until the power changes; the project's page is published from `site/`** (spec "Nodes", "Screen and buttons"; `bark-proto/src/node/relay/power.rs` `Forced`, `force`, `event.rs` `AppCommand::ForceRole`, `Trace::Forced`, `tests/forced.rs`; `bark-driver` `Request::ForceRole`; `bark-screen/src/qr.rs`, `qr/symbol.rs`, `scripts/qr.py`, `schedule.rs`; `bark-fw/src/button.rs`, `display.rs`, `node.rs`; `site/index.html`, `.github/workflows/pages.yml`, `release.yml`; `core-interface.md` §4). The code says `zond.github.io/bark`, version 3 at level H, the smallest that holds its 19 bytes at H (ISO/IEC 18004), 5 px a module with an orb web over 61 of its 841 modules and threads in the margin outside a 2-module clear zone, and one line under it, the ID or the pairing code; `scripts/qr.py generate` writes the modules, a host test encodes the address again and compares, and the drawn 200 × 200 picture decodes with rqrr in the host tests and with ZXing-C++ and OpenCV at 0.75 to 3 times its size in `qr.py check`. The screen draws a change of picture and a new pairing code at once, on the code the end of a pairing a refresh after, while the lines show a change of the role, the phone or the power a refresh after, and nothing else: the gap and the stale time go. The pad on P0.11 is read as D175 says (it was: no pull, its idle level read at boot and a change from it lasting 40 ms a touch, `docs/touch-pad-2026-11-09.md`); the button, pressed shorter than its hold, flips the picture as a touch does, since one board's pad gives no edge at all. The lines label the buttons at their left edge, on the margin nearer each, both being on the case's left side (the maker's case model, `docs/enclosure-2026-11-09.md`): `REBOOT` at the top for the reset button, which stays the chip's reset, and `SLEEP (3s)` or `WAKE (3s)` at the bottom for the user button, the role a hold would give, each after an arrow pointing at its button; the ten lines move down 4 px. Each 3 s hold flips the role the moment the 3 s pass, and the node is forced until the next change of power, which returns it to the power's rules; a forced relay on battery still leaves at its low battery, which ends the force too, and a flip to relay is refused there; a relay flipped sleepy leaves as at a low battery, flushing first; a reboot forgets the force. The screen never says a role is forced: the role line is as before. Each release carries `bark.apk` and `bark.uf2` beside the numbered files, so the page's button links the latest app. | *Decided by the user 2026-11-09.* Most people who see a node do not own it, and a code is how a passer-by gets the app without typing; a private node's code serves a guest too, who can use a public node. The lines are for the owner, who touches to read them, so the screen draws almost only when a person acts, where it drew every gap; a picture of the code never grows stale, so it needs no uptime. 30 s is three times the reading of ten lines; 3 s thirty times an ordinary press, against bumps and bags. The forced role is for people who know better than the power: a node on a power bank looks plugged in, and one on battery may be where its neighbours need a relay. A flip, not a choice of role, since the button is one input and the core knows the role; nothing on the screen says forced, as the role line says what the node does and the label what a hold would make it. The power's change ends the force, as the act that says the node's circumstances changed, and the low battery still wins, since the cell is the owner's. The force fits the battery state's padding: the relay stays 95 016 B. The open release image of version 16: flash 482 648 B against main's 478 592 B, 4 056 B more; the stack 111 864 B against 112 008 B (`uf2.py --stack`, 2026-11-09). *Built 2026-11-09 on the host; for the bench (Phase 2).* |

The link time lets the newer of a card and a message win, as a card shown long after the link ended would otherwise move a contact back to a node the person left; to the minute, the span a place stays fresh, so that the code on screen does not change every second. | D164 | **A release names two versions that move apart, the node firmware's F and the app's A, in a manifest it carries, `release.json`, `{"release": N, "app": A, "firmware": F}`; the workflow builds a side as version N only if its files changed since the previous release, the published one of the highest number below N, and otherwise attaches that release's files of the side unchanged, under their version; a tag that changes neither makes no release; the app reads the manifest of the latest release, or takes both as N for a release without one, v21 and older, and compares its own version with A and the node's image with F** (`.github/workflows/release.yml`; `scripts/release-changed.sh`, `release-changed-test.sh`, run by `ci.yml`; `bark-app` `releases.dart`, `updater.dart`, `ui/settings_screen.dart`, `ui/conversations_screen.dart`, `ui/debug_screen.dart`; `README.md`, "Releases"; `bark-app/README.md`, "Newer releases"; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Updates"; spec "Phone link", Updates). The app's own version is still the N it was built at, `--dart-define=BARK_VERSION=<N>`. The check costs one more request a day, the manifest's download, from the release's files rather than the API. The "Check for updates" line reads, for example, "App up to date (22); node firmware 21, up to date". | Asked by the user 2026-11-09: a release for an app fix minted a new image that every node gathered over the mesh and swapped in for nothing, each swap a reboot and two erases of the running slot (`bark-fw/README.md`, "Updates"), and a firmware-only release made every phone download the app again. One tag per release keeps one place to look and one order; the manifest says what each side is without the app inferring it from file names. | D168 | **A node keeps up to eight phones, a record for each: the bond, the person's name as their card has it and their recipient tag at the node, both written to Recipient, their mailbox key once they make the node their home, the last of their messages confirmed, the day last linked and the record's number; a pairing by the code on the screen adds a record, or replaces the bond of a record whose bond names the same phone, and a node with every record in use refuses it with Pairing Failed, no code shown, `PHONES FULL` on its screen for 30 s and Node's `room` at 0, but a node nobody is paired with takes it into the record whose phone was linked longest ago; every paired phone is an owner, with a standing link, its own inbox and every owner's setting, Public being the node's, one for all; the node takes a link for each paired phone and one more, nine at most, and advertises while one is free; every node, a home or not, keeps every message in its ring, and a phone's messages are those with its record's tag, read after the last it confirmed; a slot's mark is a bit for each record, set at arrival for the records whose tag the item carries, cleared by that record's fetch or confirmation; a fetch opens under the key of one record and answers that record's items, one round a record, the rounds of several records taking the send slot in turn; Home sets and clears only the writing phone's key, and frees a record holding the same key and no bond; Phones, a new characteristic, lists the records and removes another phone's bond or home, or both, with a fresh six-digit code the node draws on its screen when asked, taken within 30 s and on one try; a phone still unpairs itself by Public's 2 with no code, keeping its home; link version 13** (spec "Phone link", Finding the node, Pairing, The removed phone, Node, Sending, Given back, Receiving, Home, Public, Phones; "Mailbox", The ring, The fetch, The answer; "Screen and buttons"). | *Decided by the user 2026-11-10.* A household shares one node, and a pairing that adds instead of replacing removes the risk that a visitor pairing by the screen code takes a café's node from its keeper; the node trusts everyone who pairs alike, as being at the node was already the only guard. Eight: the mark is the slot's one byte, a bit a record, so one person's reading marks nothing of another's, and it covers a family of four to six with room; the host holds nine bonds, eight records of at most 128 B take a quarter of a 4 KiB flash page. Nine links cost seven more than two, 7 × 5 616 B = 39 312 B of static RAM, leaving the stack 72 552 B of the 111 864 B of D160, above the 24 KiB floor; each linked phone's connection events cost a sleepy node current, unmeasured. The ring on every node, where a private node that was nobody's home kept its messages in the core's inbox: that one queue of 23 would hold every person's mail behind an absent one's, give it up once stale, and lose it at a reboot. A code for a removal, as the link reaches through walls and a phone paired once may be lost, stolen or a former household's; 30 s is the Security Manager's timeout (Core Vol 3 Part H §3.4); one try a code makes guessing cost a code drawn where people at the node see it. A removed bond's phone learns as a replaced one did, its next encrypted link ended; a removed home's person sees it in Home's new `mine` at their next link there, while their fetches elsewhere go unanswered. The day last linked, not the time, so that a record is written again at most once a day. Older apps (link version 12 and before) see the same characteristics: Recipient takes the tag alone, so their person's name stays empty; Home's `set` keeps its meaning, anyone's home, with `mine` appended; Node's `room` is appended, and with D177 its `oldest`, while Recipient's write from version 13 carries the app's version and oldest before the name; they see no Phones, their Inbox gives only their person's messages, and their Settings' line that a node keeps one phone is wrong until the app is updated. Unverified, for the bench: that the controller's resolving list holds eight bonded phones' keys (LE Read Resolving List Size at boot), and that the external flash takes eight programs of one byte between erases (ZD25WQ16B datasheet). *Built 2026-11-10 in the node image and the app, not yet proven with two phones on a board* (`bark-link` `phones`; `bark-app-layer` `mailbox`, `flash_ring`; `bark-fw/src/phones.rs`, `ble.rs`, `store.rs`, `mailbox.rs`, `node.rs`, `display.rs`; `bark-screen`; the host's `set_refuses_pairing`, `bark-fw/vendor/README.md`): the store keeps a record as an entry of four slots, what each read or fetch moves as one of a slot, and the node's settings as one; an older image's bond and the mailbox's key become record one. The build changed the spec in five places: a full node refuses a pairing only from a phone no record's bond names, so that a phone whose record holds its bond pairs again; Home joins the phone to the bondless record of its person's key, keeping that record's place, where freeing it would orphan its unfetched mail's bits; a record keeps the ring's newest item when it was made and claims no older one, as a freed record's bits linger in the marks; a removal's 0x13 leaves the code untried; and the screen says `3 PHONES PRIVATE` and `REMOVE` with the code, as `3 PHONES, PRIVATE` and `REMOVE CODE 012345` are 17 characters of a line's 16. Nine links cost 41 120 B, 7 × 5 874 B, the slots' tasks 2 240 B each where D150 counted 1.9 KB; the open release image's static RAM grows 44 552 B and its stack has 65 864 B against `main`'s 110 408 B, its flash 16 480 B more (`nm`, `uf2.py --stack`). The boot logs the resolving list's size against eight, and each mark is read back after its program, the first right one and each power of two of the wrong ones logged (`bark-fw/README.md`, "Phone link", "Mailbox"). |
| D169 | **A node on battery shows how long its battery lasts, `BATTERY 90%/3D`: it learns two drains, percent of the cell an hour, one as a relay with its GPS at rest and one as a sleepy node, each first 100 % / (850 mAh / its current), 0.754 % an hour at 6.41 mA and 0.0696 at 0.59 mA, then moved by samples of at least 15 h as a moving average over 92.8 h; a sample starts an hour after a change of the way the node runs, of the power or of the calibration, and a change or a relay's GPS tracking ends it unused; the time left is until the cell is flat under the node's rules, a relay's segment to the low level and a sleepy node's from there; the drains are kept across a reboot in the stored record, flag 0x20, two bytes of steps of 2.2 % from the first, in layout version 1, as a record without them reads as before** (spec "Sleepy nodes", How long the battery lasts, "Implementation notes", "Screen and buttons"; `bark-proto/src/drain.rs`, `node/relay/power.rs` `battery_left`, `battery_drains`, `running`, `store.rs` `Stored::drain`, `event.rs` `Trace::Drain`, `tests/battery.rs`; `bark-screen` `Left`, the BATTERY line; `bark-fw/src/node.rs`, the 10 s line's `battery_left_min`, `drain_relay`, `drain_sleepy`, `console.rs` `status`; `core-interface.md` §4, §6; `bark-fw/README.md`, Display). | *Decided by the user 2026-11-10.* A person at a node on battery wants to know how long it has, and the level alone does not say: at 90 % a relay on battery has 16 days, four of them relaying, and a sleepy node 54. The board has no fuel gauge, so the estimate is honest only slowly. The level is the core's mapping of the cell's voltage, in whole percent, a straight line between Maxim AN4189's 30 % and 100 % points where the curve is not straight: one percent of charge reads as 0.4 of the line's between 30 and 50 % and 1.6 near full. 15 h: the time a sleepy node takes to drain the smallest step the percent shows, 1 % at 0.0696 % an hour, 14.4 h, rounded up; a step is about 5.7 mV, three of the converter's steps, and the load moves a reading by a few millivolts, so a drop over minutes is noise. 92.8 h: the time a relay takes to cross the line from full to 30 %, so that the average spans the line's bends; it closes 57 to 64 % of the way from the first drain within that time and is within 2 % after three, a sleepy node's within 5 %, in the core's tests with a cell read through the mapping. The first drains are of the radio's currents (`battery-role-2026-10-25.md`; spec "Delivery"), the divider and the GPS in standby; the 0.65 mA that note gives a sleepy node predates the currents of D92 and the rested GPS of D80 and D91. A carried node on battery is sleepy (D152), so a carried way of its own would never run; a relay's GPS tracks on battery only through the grace and a far fix, too short for a sample, so it stops one rather than having a drain of its own. *Limits.* What it learns is in the line's percent, so between 30 and 50 % a sleepy node, whose average follows the bends, shows up to about twice the time it has, and near full less; a reading off by the calibration's 1 %, or 3 % before one, moves the level shown and the time left with it, as it moves the turn to sleepy (D81), and the drain, a difference, hardly; the microcontroller's and the display's current are not in the first drains, and the readings bring them in over days; the T-Echo's own cell and temperature are not known. The simulator runs the estimate: on `grid-down.toml` shrunk to 1.5 km and a 20 h outage, every delivery figure and the battery series are byte for byte those of main; the stored state is written once more a sample, about once a node and 15 h on battery. The app could show the time left beside the level, which needs a field in Status (spec "Phone link"), left for the link's next version. *Built 2026-11-10 on the host.* |
| D170 | **The reboot into an image gathered over the mesh waits only for what a reset would lose, within a bound: from the staging on, while its power allows a swap and no phone's update holds the slot, the node drains, taking no message, every DATA that would give it work refused as for want of room with a place in the room's line and its application's not taken; it reboots once its send queue, its delivery queue and its inbox are empty, no reply owed takes a DATA, its mailbox writes nothing and no exchange with a phone is half done (a write not yet answered or its reply not yet sent, a notification not yet sent, a pairing), or once it has drained for the delivery TTL, 600 s; what it holds then it writes to its external flash, each logged by message ID, and after the reboot offers again as its own, ahead of the phone's and the mailbox's, the same message by its ID; a phone's standing link does not hold the reboot back, and the node ends every link before it resets** (spec "Send queue", "Bans", "Updates over the mesh", the install; `bark-proto` `AppCommand::Drain`, `DropReason::Draining`, `relay/drain.rs` `drained`, `held`, `Held`, `tests/drain.rs`; `bark-driver` `Request::Drain`; `bark-app-layer/src/carry.rs`; `bark-image/src/policy.rs` `Moment`, `Links`, `drains`, `quiet`, `layout.rs` `external::CARRY`; `bark-fw/src/carry.rs`, `ble.rs` `doing`, `close_all`, `update/mesh.rs` `moment`, `drain`, `node.rs`; `bark-sim` `DropCounts::draining`; `core-interface.md` §4; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Flash layout", "Updates"). Revises D153's quiet moment, which asked for no phone link and no message held. | Found on the bench 2026-11-10: node 3112 gathered image 23 over the mesh at 06:17 and had not rebooted into it four hours later, as its owner's phone had held a standing link to it since 04:03, as the app does with its paired node, and the rule asked for no link at all; so a home node whose owner is at home never installed. *Decided by the user the same day*, with a second requirement: nodes must install at 64 times the design's traffic, where a relay's queues are never empty, so "nothing held" had to become relative and bounded. A standing link that is idle loses nothing to a reset: the app looks for its node whenever the link is down and connects again once it is back (`bark-app/README.md`, "Screens"), so its person sees the node out of reach for the minute a swap takes. Half done is defined by what the link does, not by a timer: a write is answered within a step of the node, a notification goes at the next connection event and a pairing ends by its own protocol. The drain is the protocol's own push-back (D29–D36): refused, a message waits at its sender and goes round the node where another way is left, the wait reaching the origin, so the queues empty and nothing is dropped; the room's line, one ban apart, spaces the senders' returns as for any want of room. It opens again on a low battery or a phone's update, as no reboot can come then and refusing for hours would cost the network for nothing. A take answered before the drain stands, since its sender is done with the message: the host test found one dropped there before this was so. The bound is the delivery TTL: by then each message in the delivery queue has waited for its node as long as the queue gives a node that sleeps or is dark, and a forward has had twenty hops' budgets of 30 s; it counts from the whole second the drain began, rounded up, a `u32` that fits beside a flag in the node's forwarding state, so the relay stays 95 016 B. What is left is carried, not dropped: DATA carries no origin, and the message ID is a hash of the destination and the payload alone, so the node offers the same message again; the cost is that it starts at count 0 with no parent here, so a search that runs out of neighbours ends at this node instead of going back to its parent, as for any message of its own. 167 messages at most, the send queue's 39 places and 64 hop places, the delivery queue's 41 and the inbox's 23, a 256-byte page each, 11 sectors of the external flash, which leaves 64 KiB free; a slot's ID is its check, and the region is erased once all are offered again, a reset before then offering them again, which the network answers as copies. The links are ended first, as the phone's own reboot does, so that each phone sees the node leave at once. Status has no free flag to announce the reboot before it, its eight flags all in use, so the node says nothing more without a new link version. *Tested on the host*: four sources sending through one relay as fast as it lets them, which fills its queue; drained from then, it refuses only for draining, empties in 2.9 s with nothing dropped anywhere, and after its reboot carries the load again; a message for a node that stays dark is still held at the bound, drained then and not a second before, and offered again after the reboot it is the same message, back in the delivery queue; the policy's quiet moment and drain; a carried slot's round trip, an erased and a torn one keeping nothing. *Built 2026-11-10 on the host and in the node image; the bench is to be run.* *Simulated 2026-11-10* in the district, a tenth of the city at its densities, at 64 times the design rate, every node installing within one hour, seeds 1 to 3 (`update-under-load-2026-11-10.md`): every node rebooted within the bound and nothing it held was lost to the reset, but 77 to 93 more texts a run were lost in the network than without the installs, mostly at nodes just back from their swap (a kept message offered with no parent by a node that knows few neighbours yet, a sender's only way on silent for the swap), and two thirds of the relays waited out the bound, mostly for texts in their delivery queues; delivery fell by a third while the installs went on. |
| D171 | **The order in which the app takes a node, the home, then another paired node, then a public one, holds among the nodes it hears at a usable signal, −85 dBm or above, the line below which Settings says "weak"; a home or paired node heard below it is passed over for a node further down the order heard above it, and a paired node so passed over sends the phone to the public node at once, not after two rounds; when none is heard usable, the order no longer holds and the phone takes the node it hears strongest, of whatever kind, home, paired or public; a round's scan ends early only on the home, or the one paired node, heard usable; a node heard without an RSSI ranks as the weakest; the help beside the connection line says when the paired nodes are heard too weakly** (spec "Phone link", Which node a phone uses; `bark-app` `ble_link.dart` `FoundNode.usable`, `weakDbm`, `listen`; `app.dart` `chooseNode`, `_stopAt`, `ownWeak`; `ui/common.dart` `connectionHelp`; `bark-app/README.md`). Revises D166's home however weak. | *Decided by the user 2026-11-10*: a phone at the edge of its home's reach that ties itself to the home holds a link that will not last, while a paired or public node near it would. The line is the one Settings already shows as "weak", so what the person reads and what the app does agree; like it, it is a choice, an advertisement's strength heard before any link. No hysteresis is added: the line is applied only when the phone takes a node, and a link once up is kept until it drops or the phone moves up (D172), never left for its signal, so a node heard about the line does not move the phone back and forth. At once to the public node, as the paired node was heard and judged, not missed; the two rounds of D163 are for a node one scan may not hear. Below the line signal alone decides, by the user's amendment of 2026-11-10: among nodes that may all fail to hold a link, the strongest is the likeliest to hold, and the home's or a paired node's worth does not outweigh that. *Built 2026-11-10 on the host.* |
| D172 | **On a paired node that is not the home, while the phone is paired with the home and the search, not the person's tap, made the link, the app scans for the home once a minute, the guest errand's interval (`guestCheck`), and moves to it when it hears it at a usable signal (D171) and nothing on the link would be cut: no Send written and not yet answered, no inbox message read and not yet confirmed, no fetch awaiting its answer, no firmware update sending, being checked or rebooting; while one is, it waits for the next minute; moving closes the link to the paired node, which the person sees as the line changing, with no dialog; the app never moves between two paired nodes, nor between two public ones, and on the home it does not scan** (spec "Phone link", Which node a phone uses; `bark-app` `app.dart` `lookAround`, `chooseNode`, `_lookAround`, `_above`, `settled`, `movesHome`; `session.dart` `inFlight`; `updater.dart` `underWay`; `ui/common.dart` `connectionHelp`; `bark-app/README.md`). Revises D166, under which the search did not run beside a standing link. | *Decided by the user 2026-11-10*: a person who came home with the phone on another node of theirs stayed there until its link dropped. A guest moves up already: its link lasts one errand, and the search's next round, at most 15 s on, takes a paired node it hears. A minute, not a round of 15 s: on a standing link a scan serves only this, and a scan of 4.1 s a minute listens about 7 % of the time against 27 %, for a home reached at most 45 s sooner; a minute is also how long the spec takes a person on foot to stay within a relay's reach ("Where a text goes", Why a minute), and it takes one of Android's five scans in 30 s. Only up, as two nodes of one place in the order, heard in turn at like signals, would move the phone back and forth, each move a new link. Nothing in flight, as a Send cut between its write and the answer may go twice, and a fetch's answer comes to the node it was sent from. A link the person made by a tap stays theirs until it drops, as they may be at that node for its settings or an update.
