| D178 | **A node keeps a log of its cell's voltage on the external flash, so that the cell's own curve can be learned from a full discharge: a sample at the first reading after a boot, or at the boot itself on USB; at each reading an hour after the last sample on battery; at the first reading after USB goes, and when USB comes with the last reading on battery; and at the first reading after the node turns sleepy or relay or its GPS starts or stops; each sample 16 bytes, a sequence number, UTC seconds if the node knows the time else 0, the uptime, the reading as the converter gave it, the power, the mode, why it was taken, and a CRC-16; 2048 samples in 8 sectors at `0x1F0000`, a ring that erases its oldest sector when full, kept across reboots and image swaps; the node logs a summary at boot, a console image prints every sample with `discharge` and erases them with `discharge erase`, and `bark-fw/scripts/discharge.py` fits the curve; D169's estimate is unchanged** (`bark-image` `discharge.rs` `Sampler`, `Ring`, `Oldest`, `Summary`, `layout.rs` `external::DISCHARGE`; `bark-fw/src/discharge.rs`, `node.rs`, `gps.rs` `awake`, `console.rs`; `bark-fw/scripts/discharge.py`; `bark-fw/README.md`, "Flash layout", "Console", "Discharge log"). | *Decided by the user 2026-11-10.* D169's estimate reads the level off a straight line through three points of a typical cell, and most of its error comes from that line (D169, Limits); the board's own curve would remove it, and only a full discharge shows the curve. That takes about 16 days for a relay that turns sleepy at its low level and 59 for a sleepy node, on battery, where no host reads the USB log, so the samples are kept on the node. *An hour*: a sleepy node's 59 days are 1416 samples, which fit; D169's sample of 15 h spans 15, so where its steps fall shows; a step of the line is about 5.7 mV, three of the converter's, so a finer interval would show the readings' noise. The boot's sample at the first reading tells how the cell rested before the node's load; the samples when USB goes and comes mark the discharge's ends; the mode's mark where the current changes, which the fit needs. The reading is kept as read, and the console prints the calibration the node keeps beside it, so the script applies it once. *8 sectors*: 1416 samples are 5.5 sectors, a sector is erased whole before it is written again, and boots and changes add samples; half of the 64 KiB D170 left free, the other half kept free. *Wrap, not stop*: the discharge to read is the one just ended, and a log that stopped when full would lose it to an earlier run nobody erased; wrapping loses the oldest sector, 256 samples, from more than 74 days before at a sample an hour. A torn record fails its check and the next goes after it; an erase cut short leaves older samples, which the reader passes over as out of order. The radio's duty is not logged: the driver does not count its airtime, and the mode carries most of the current. *The fit* (the script's docstring): a constant current in each mode, D169's 6.41 and 0.59 mA and the GPS's 29 mA; the fraction left at a sample is one less the charge used up to it over the whole run's; a run in one mode does not depend on its current, one that turns sleepy at its low level depends on the ratio, for which the drains the node learned can be given; it prints the voltage at every 5 % from 0 to 100, which `battery_percent` would read by straight lines in place of its three points, the levels becoming its 20 and 30 % and the full voltage its 100 %. The writes go between the driver's steps, as the carried messages' do: a program of 16 bytes an hour on battery and an erase every 256 samples. The node image grows by 3.4 KB and its stack's room is 32 B less, 110 376 B. *Reading it out*: plug the board in, and its boot line says `discharge: N samples over H h, A mV to B mV`; stage a console image (`scripts/image.py stage`), run `scripts/discharge.py /dev/ttyACM0 --csv run.csv`, which sends `discharge`, writes the CSV and prints the fit; `discharge erase` before the next run; stage the node image again. *Tested on the host*: a record's round trip, a torn and an erased record holding none; the ring read oldest first across a wrap that loses one sector; a torn record skipped and the next written after it; an erase cut short, its old samples passed over; when each sample is taken; the script on a synthetic discharge along AN4189's curve, a relay to 20 % then sleepy, its readings noisy by 1.5 mV, read back within 4 mV of the curve at all 21 points. *Built 2026-11-10 on the host and in the node and console images; the bench run is to come.* |

## Phases

### Phase 0: protocol core (done)

Built 2026-10-14 and 15: the workspace; `bark-proto`, the protocol core, with the codecs of spec "Packet formats", the tables, forwarding and the search around gaps (spec "Forwarding"), the busy estimate and the home channel (spec "Home channel selection"), the heartbeat and the duty-cycle budget; `bark-driver`, the loop that runs the core against a radio and a clock (`firmware-adapter-2026-10-15.md`); and the contract between them, `core-interface.md`. The core first had two kinds of node; D46 (2026-10-17) made them one node with a sleepy setting, and D47 to D51, D58 and D61 to D64 define what a sleepy node does (spec "Sleepy nodes"). The codecs round-trip under property tests (`bark-proto/src/wire.rs`), and the tests in `bark-proto/tests` run nodes through the core with a fake clock.

### Phase 1: simulator (done)

Built from 2026-10-14 (D2, `simulator-design.md`): a discrete-event simulator that runs the real core, carries its frames as bytes and reads scenarios from `scenarios/`. What it showed:

- Forwarding, the ACK and the search around a dead relay (`s1-line.toml`, `s1b-hidden.toml`), a block of relays and sleepy nodes (`s2-block.toml`) and channel choice beside an interferer (`s3-interferer.toml`), each the twin of a bring-up step.
- One collision domain at the rates of spec "Capacity and sizing" (`s4-capacity.toml`). On 2026-10-15 it carried about half its texts, the duty-cycle cap of windowsill relays in the centre binding first (`capacity-research-2026-10-15.md`). The decisions from D29 on come from the collapses found there; since D52 the runs switch nodes on over time, with random IDs, drifting clocks and walkers (`realism-2026-10-18.md`). On 2026-10-19 it delivered 96 % of its reachable texts at the rate and 77 to 90 % at three times it, and three domains side by side (`strip.toml`) 95 % at the rate (D66, D67).

The radio's unknowns, adjacent-channel rejection, SF5 sensitivity, fading and the hearing radius, keep the datasheet's values and assumptions until Phase 2 measures them.

### Phase 2: bring-up (boards since 2026-10-29)

Four T-Echo 433 boards are on the bench, the first two since 2026-10-29. Each board shows its node ID on its e-paper display at boot, so that boards can be told apart unplugged. The firmware runs the core, a relay on USB and on battery sleepy at once if carried and at a low battery if fixed (D71, D81, D152), or a measurement console, behind a loader that swaps in staged images (D79); the first two days on the boards are `docs/bench-2026-10-30.md`. Toolchain: the `thumbv7em-none-eabihf` target, a UF2 converter, text logging over USB serial (no debug probe). Test rig: at least three T-Echo 433, SMA attenuators, one interferer source (a fourth board is fine), a USB logic/UART sniffer for timing.

The bring-up order. Each step is a milestone on real boards; steps 1 to 3 come first, the rest are Phase 3. Every measurement goes back into the simulator.

1. `lora-phy` at SF5/125 kHz on two T-Echo 433: transmit, receive, channel activity detection (CAD), signal strength (RSSI); retune and sweep times, the noise floor and the preamble the modem needs to lock measured; adjacent-channel rejection with two boards and attenuators (O5).
2. Listen-before-talk + rotating heartbeat + neighbour table on three boards: each board lists the other two with channel and position within one rotation; a board switched on beside the other two lists them, and they it, within its first rotation's burst, about 15 s, each answering it once (D73). Restarted, a board comes back on its stored home channel (`stored Some(..)` in its log).
3. Busy estimate + home-channel selection: put a continuous-wave interferer on one channel, watch boards avoid it; check that three boards that all hear each other do not keep switching.
4. Greedy forwarding + per-hop ACK across three relays in a line, with attenuators or distance so A cannot hear C, or on one desk with A and C built deaf to each other (`BARK_IGNORE`), which tests the routing and not the hidden terminal (`docs/bringup-2026-11-06.md`). Demotion when the middle relay is powered off.
5. Sleepy node: anchor, beats, delivery in the window, queue, the watch for the anchor's heartbeat, and duty-cycled receive between the windows with the long preamble (`Action::Sniff`, `core-interface.md`): a long-preamble frame sent to a board checking every 361 ms, 100 times, caught every time. **This is the loop every user feels; prototype it before anything clever.**
6. BLE GATT + minimal phone app.
7. Field test: a dozen relays on windowsills across a few blocks.

Open on the bench, from the first days on (2026-10-30) to now; the storm the first runs found is settled by D89 to D102 (`storm-2026-10-30.md`):

- Steps 4 and 5 on the three boards of one desk, prepared 2026-11-06 and not yet run (`docs/bringup-2026-11-06.md`): a line of A and C built deaf to each other with B between, its twin `scenarios/line.toml`; a sleepy node built to report battery on the host's USB (`BARK_ON_BATTERY=1`); a board switched off by its bootloader; and `bark-fw/scripts/bench.py` to build, flash and log from the host and to read each text's path, the long-preamble catches and the anchor's watches from the logs.
- A survey build of the console for a board left on battery away from the host, built 2026-10-30 and not yet placed (`BARK_SURVEY=<channel>`, `bark-fw/README.md` "Survey build"): beacons at every power and length with each frame saying its power, and a log of the noise floor by channel kept in memory until the board is plugged in again. One placement gives the RSSI calibration, the loss curve as far as the path allows, the noise floor away from the desk and, over a day, the fading of one link.
- Whether the desk's noise floor, 40 dB above thermal, is the room's (screens, chargers, the host's USB ground) or the board's: the survey build in a quiet room answers it.
- D88's driver on the boards: the phases of `lbt` against the simulator's defaults, the offset the self-check finds and whether it holds under a signal, the probed delay at boot and after a night, a transmit from the standby a CAD ends in, and the reading after the CAD if the self-check fails (`lean-driver-2026-10-30.md`, "What the bench still has to confirm").
- A listen-before-talk on the home channel answered by the listening receiver, and ACKs without one: measured in the simulator, D93 (ACKs at once, adopted) and D94 (the probe, not adopted); on the board, that an ACK sent at once arrives within the sender's 100 ms, and what a held check does to it.
- The oscillator's start on a cold board: D88 probes it and follows it in use, up to 4 ms; what a cold board's search lands on, and whether the hourly probe brings it back down as the board warms, is to be seen on a board left outside overnight. Whatever it lands on, the node's receive cycle keeps the gap the long preamble spans: the excess over 0.5 ms comes off its sleep (D92).
- A sleepy node with an anchor runs no GPS (D91): on the bench, a node on battery with an anchor is to log no `locate` request, one that hears no relay a search at once and then one a delivery TTL, and a search cut short when an answer to its beat arrives.

The bench list, with what each number is expected to be and where it goes. `bark-fw/scripts/measure.md` says how to take each; the console (`bark-fw --features console`) prints the timing ones.

| Measurement | How | Expected | Goes to |
|---|---|---|---|
| Retune time | console `ch`, `retune_us` | measured 668 to 680 µs for any hop with every command sent, 388 µs with the three that change on the lean driver, the simulator's default (`lean-driver-2026-10-30.md`, D88) | Spec "Busy estimate", the simulator's retune (D86, D88) |
| Oscillator start | the probed delay at boot, console `tcxo`; `probe` after a night outside; the error count in the node's `driver` line | 200 to 500 µs on one board at room temperature, the flag flickering below 200 µs (`docs/bench-2026-10-30.md`); the search lands at 0.5 ms there (D88); a cold board is open | `bark-driver/src/tcxo.rs`, the wake of spec "Duty-cycled receive and the long preamble" |

| Resets in a swap | `scripts/swapbench.py ID 50` (bark-fw): the bench loader of D143 and a console image of version 1 copied to a board, then 50 rounds, in turn a console image one version up and a `panic` image one version up, each staged with `scripts/image.py stage --reboot`; the loader resets the chip once in each swap and each swap back, at a moment drawn evenly over its erases and writes | after every reset one whole image runs, the one expected: the new version confirmed after each of the 25 swaps up, the previous one back after each of the 25 swaps back; each swap ends at the boot after its reset; never a board that stays silent or shows `TECHOBOOT`. The script prints per round the time from `reboot` to the port back and where each reset fell. A USB power cut was dropped: the T-Echo runs on from its battery, which has no switch, when its USB power goes. The images carry the console, which does not probe the 32.768 kHz clock; a node image's swap is proven by "An update from the phone" (D148). 50 rounds are 75 swaps, each an erase of every page of the active slot: under 1 % of the flash's 10,000 cycles (nRF52840 Product Specification, flash endurance), where 200 rounds would spend 3 %, and 6 % of the state page's, for moments drawn at random. The moments that matter are few, and the next row walks them. Passed 2026-11-07 with D148's loader on one board, 50 of 50 rounds: every one of the 75 resets fired, the resets fell on internal erases and writes of pages across the whole slot and on the external flash's erases and writes, from 1 % to 99.7 % through; from `reboot` to the port back 56.9 / 57.5 / 58.2 s for a swap and 113.5 / 114.6 / 116.1 s for a swap and its swap back (min / median / max). | D79, D139, D143, D148, `bark-image/src/boot/tests.rs` (power cuts at every flash operation on the host) |
| Resets at chosen moments of a swap | `scripts/cutbench.py ID` (bark-fw): the bench loader of D143 and a console image of version 1 copied to a board, then a round for each of 19 moments (`scripts/cutbench.py --list`), each the index of an operation, an erase of a page or a write, on either flash at the boot that starts the phase, as the host test that cuts the power at every operation counts them: in the swap, the record's word that notes the image checked, and the operation before and the one after each progress word of the first and last pair of copies, the last word itself taking the place of the one after; in the swap back, the same around its first and last pair, and the state page's word that marks the progress invalid, its erase and its word. The console's `cut` writes the moment to the record after `commit`, and `none` for the other phase; the loader does that operation half way and resets | after each reset the phase ends at the next boot with one whole image running, the one expected: the new version confirmed after each of the 9 moments of the swap, each with a console image one version up; the previous one back after each of the 10 of the swap back, each with a `panic` image one version up whose swap is not stopped; the log names the operation expected, its flash, erase or write, and page, and the other phase as not reached; never a board that stays silent or shows `TECHOBOOT`. The script stops at the first round that fails and says the moment and what was seen. 19 rounds are 29 swaps, each an erase of every page of the active slot, 0.3 % of the flash's 10,000 cycles (nRF52840 Product Specification, flash endurance), and 48 erases of the state page, two for each update and one more for each swap back, 0.5 %; each sector of the staged slot is erased at most twice in a round and three times with a swap back, 48 of the external flash's 100,000. Passed 2026-11-08 on one board, 19 of 19 rounds: the swap's nine cuts each ended with the new version confirmed, 57 to 58 s from `reboot` to the port back; the swap back's ten, the state page's own three writes among them, each ended with the previous version back, 112.5 to 114.9 s; every round named the operation the host predicts. On the host: for every one of the 1 363 operations of the swap and the 1 365 of the swap back, a moment chosen there stops the operation the power cut of the same index takes, and the next boot ends the phase with the expected image whole; `--list` checked against the host's count (`bark-image/src/boot/tests/cut.rs`). | D79, D139, D143, `bark-image/src/boot/cut.rs` `operations` and `choose`, `bark-image/src/boot/tests.rs` (power cuts at every flash operation on the host) |

Bluetooth switched off on the phone half way: `update: link closed with … bytes held`, and on reconnecting the app goes on from them. Under D148's loader the new image logs `lfclk: crystal, already running`, under an older one `lfclk: RC oscillator, calibrated, already running`. Passed 2026-11-07 with D148's loader: version 0 to 1, Bluetooth switched off at 109 200 bytes and on again, the link back in 2 s and the transfer resumed; 61 s from `reboot` to the port back, then `lfclk: crystal, already running` and `update: version 1 confirmed`. | D145, D148 |

Passed twice before D158; the second time, 2026-11-09, version 11 of 471 544 bytes was whole 3 642 s after the gathering began, and confirmed after its swap. ### Phase 3: protocol on hardware

Bring-up steps 4 and 5 (Phase 2) on four boards, three relays in a line and a sleepy node; then step 6, the BLE link of spec "Phone link" and a minimal app for it. The field test, step 7, needs more boards or RAK4631 EU433 relays (spec "Hardware", O12).

## Questions settled

- **Q1 (D3), decided 2026-10-14.** The core does no I/O. An async node task would need a mini-executor in the simulator and makes 20k simulated nodes expensive. The contract is `docs/core-interface.md`.
- **Q2, decided 2026-10-14.** The link model is a sparse table of received power for each pair in range, filled by a log-distance generator with a rooftop bonus and a wall loss, or given pair by pair, with shadowing per pair and fading per channel over time (`bark-sim/src/links.rs`, `phy.rs`).
- **Q3, decided 2026-10-14 and 2026-10-18.** Sleepy nodes stay at their points and go dark at random (2026-10-14); since D52 a share of them walk, and D59 added riders in vehicles and groups that go dark together (`simulator-design.md`).
- **Q4, decided 2026-10-14.** The simulator carries the bytes the core encodes, so a codec bug fails it too and airtime follows from the real lengths.
- **Q5 (D13), decided 2026-10-14.** The 8-symbol preamble was wrong for SF5 (12 minimum). The spec now uses 12 everywhere, and the sweep-loss argument, redone, spreads the sweep.
- **Q6, decided 2026-10-14.** Scenarios are TOML files in `scenarios/`, the stable interface, and the builder in `bark-sim/src/scenario.rs` serves the tests.
- **Q7, decided 2026-11-06.** Texts are not compressed, and a mailbox reply carries one item. Measured on texts of the kind people send, bytes per text as UTF-8 / windows-1251 / brotli 11 / zstd 19 / deflate 9: English of 26 characters 26 / 26 / 30 / 35 / 28; English of 130, 132 / 132 / 100 / 108 / 104; Swedish of 120, 121 / – / 101 / 103 / 101; Ukrainian of 110, 205 / 115 / 112 / 145 / 141; Ukrainian of 37, 37 / 22 / 41 / 46 / 37. A short text, the common kind, grows; a long Latin one gains a fifth; a Cyrillic one gains nothing over the windows-1251 encoding D125 already uses, with no code at all. Packing two or three small items into one reply would save some 60 to 70 bytes an item but makes the reply, its nonce and the requester's bookkeeping carry a count (D128); the user dropped both.
- **Spec open items resolved.** O2, the packet formats: built and tested (`bark-proto/src/wire.rs`, `core-interface.md` §7), changed only by a decision. O4, the neighbour table for rooftop relays that hear hundreds: D69. O8, transmit power control: D40. O9, the crate layout: D7. O13, an owner asking its neighbours whether they hear a node: not built (2026-10-18); an owner that does not hear the node queues for it. O14, hidden terminals: D22. O15, a relay at its cap holding a packet the sender could have sent elsewhere: D29 rule 1. O17, how a solar relay reports its power: D81, under which a fixed relay on battery relays until its battery is low and again at the high level, whatever it reports. O20, what the application is told when its message is refused at its own node: D117, the phone is the queue and the app is given the message back with its wait. O22, what a node does with messages for its application while the app is away: D122. O1, the app's end-to-end encryption: D123 to D128. O23, the phone link's security before nodes leave the bench: D135. O21, the dense city over many seeds on main as it stands, D102 to D106 together, since every overload result so far is from one to three seeds (`dense-power.toml`): a simulator task, not a question of the specification; moved here 2026-11-05 and still to be run.
